\chapter{Introduction}

\section*{Overview}
\label{section: Overview}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{\protect\numberline{}Overview}

Adeles have gone by various names over the course of mathematical history. A term first introduced by Chevalley as a wordplay on ideals \cite{ChevalleyPaper}, they have been referred to as the "ring of valuation vectors" by John Tate in his seminal thesis \cite{TatesThesis}, "repartitions" by Jean-Pierre Serre \cite{serre2012algebraic}, before eventually the original term\footnote{This is a slight simplification; Chevalley originally only came up with the multiplicative ideles, it was Weil who generalized the additive adeles and ended up popularizing this name.} stuck around. Whatever one may choose to call them privately, they have now become a staple of the language of the modern number theorist, and this thesis is an exploration of them.\\


\textbf{Chapter I} covers the motivations behind and basic properties of the restricted product to get the reader familiar with the adelic language.\\
\textbf{Chapter II} recounts the substance of Tate's famous PhD thesis, which proves the classical Hecke functional equation between zeta functions of global fields using an adelic formulation. We follow primarily the exposition of \cite{ramakrishnan1998fourier}, \cite{weil1974basic} and Tate's thesis itself \cite{TatesThesis}, modernizing the notation as we go along.\\

None of the material covered in this thesis is novel, recent, or even niche mathematics, and there are a plethora of sources on this; the References section of this thesis likely contains but a tiny fraction of them. To me this thesis is two things primarily: (1) a process by which I teach and familiarize myself with this field of mathematics via written exposition, and (2) a celebration of this elegant algebraic object that has become so ubiquitous in number theory. If for some inexplicable reason you, the reader, are someone distinct from myself or my advisor, I hope you find beauty and fascination galore from reading about this stuff. I know I sure did writing it.



\newpage

\section*{Format, Background, and Conventions}
\label{section: Format, Notation and Conventions}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{\protect\numberline{}Format, Background, and Conventions}

\subsection*{Format}
In the days of old, mathematics was written densely and read in black and white on paper due to cost and typesetting considerations. With the wonders of modern technology I have chosen to make use of a variety of boxes and colors throughout this text because it makes it far less painful to read, review, and skim this work. The three main classes of boxed environments used will be as follows:
\begin{defn} 
    A bright, almost-blaring red-backgrounded, red-striped box to house a vital Definition$^{\textsc{TM}}$.
\end{defn}
\begin{example}
    An amber-striped environment to indicate an important or prototypical Example$^{\textsc{TM}}$ of a concept is contained in its confines.
\end{example}
\begin{theorem}
    A neutrally-toned gray-shaded, box to house an important result, whether that be a Theorem$^{\textsc{TM}}$ (such as this particular box), a Proposition$^{\textsc{TM}}$, etc.
\end{theorem}

As the reader might have deduced, Definitions, Examples and Results follow a [Subsection.Number] numbering scheme, all using a common counter. Equations throughout will follow a (Section-Number) numbering scheme.\\

As is standard in mathematical writing I've also filled this text with technically unnecessary but hopefully-enriching Remarks$^{\textsc{TM}}$. Less-standard variants of asides I've used here are Sanity Checks$^{\textsc{TM}}$ (statements that should be intuitively obvious and verifiable in some epsilon time to an attentive reader) and Warnings$^{\textsc{TM}}$ (cautionary interventions about potentially confusing or unintuitive notation, terminology or concepts).\\

\subsection*{Assumed Background and Conventions}

A writer of mathematics is always challenged with balancing mathematical acessibility with the limits of time and space. As such, we assume some familiarity with measure theory, functional analysis, algebraic number theory and algebraic geometry, all at an undergraduate level. As a litmus test we suggest the reader go through the following subsection on Conventions and Notations. The goal of this text is that it should be broadly understandable to a reader comfortable with some dense subset of the conventions described below.\\

\textbf{Miscellaneous Notation Conventions}
\begin{itemize}
    \item $\nn, \zz, \qq, \rr$ and $\cc$ will denote the sets of Naturals, Integers, Rationals, Reals and Complex Numbers respectively. Naturals include 0.
    \item $S^1$ denotes the circle, i.e the group of norm-1 complex numbers.
    \item $\ff_q$ denotes the (unique, up to isomorphism!) finite field with $q$ elements. In general by $\char(K)$ we denote the \textbf{characteristic} of $K$.
    \item When we have an infinite product or a vector with infinitely many components we say \textbf{"almost everywhere"} to mean "in all but finitely many components". If the components of the infinite product are indexed by $i \in I$ then we write $(x_i)_i$ to mean the "vector" of a single element in the infinite product, and $x_i$ to mean the $i$-th coordinate.
    \item For a subset $A$ of a set $X$, we denote by $A^c$ or $X \setminus A$ the complement of $A$ in $X$.
    \item Given a function $f$ defined on a space $X$ and a subset $Y \subseteq X$, we write $f \vert_Y$ for the restriction of $f$ to $Y$.
    \item We recall the complex-valued \textbf{gamma function} $\Gamma(z) := \int^\infty_{0} t^{z-1}e^{-t} \dt$, a priori defined (and convergent!) on $\re{z} > 0$ and extends to $\cc$ via meromorphic continuation with poles at integers $k \leq 0$.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Measures and Topological Groups}
\begin{itemize}
    \item The phrase \textbf{"almost everywhere"} means we are considering a set whose complement has measure zero.
    \item By a \textbf{locally compact group} $G$ we mean $G$ is Hausdorff and every point has a compact neighborhood. Note we will \textit{always} assume the Hausdorff hypothesis. In many cases the locally compact groups we work with are also assumed to be abelian. We use the abreviation "\textbf{LCA group}" in such cases.
    \item Recall that every locally compact group admits a (left-invariant) \textbf{Haar measure}, which we often denote by $\dx$ or $\dy$ or $\dz$, and so on. This will be a slight abuse of notation since we may change the variable depending on what is being integrated, but before any such integration we explicitly describe the Haar measure. Given a Borel (i.e measurable) set $A \subseteq G$, we denote by $\vol(A, \dx)$ the measure of $A$ with respect to $\dx$.
    \item For $G$ an LCA group, we denote by $\hat{G}$ the \textbf{Pontryagin dual group} of continuous characters $G \to S^1$. We will assume the standard classical results of Pontryagin Duality for an LCA group, and refer the reader \cite{rudin1991fourier} for a detailed exposition on them.
\end{itemize}


\textbf{Algebra and Number Theory}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Given subgroups $H, K$ of a commutative group $G$ with operation denoted by addition, we denote by $H + K$ the subgroup of all elements of the form $h+k$ with $h \in H$ and $k \in K$. 
    \item All rings are assumed to be commutative and unital; $R^\times$ will denote the set of invertible elements of a ring $R$.
    \item We use $H \leq G$ and $I \ideal R$ to denote "$H$ is a subgroup of the group $G$" and "$I$ is an ideal of the ring $R$" respectively. If $r_1, r_2, \ldots$ is a set of elements in a group $G$ (resp. a ring $R$), we denote by $\brangle{r_1, r_2, \ldots}$ the subgroup (resp. ideal) generated by the $r_i$.
    \item The symbol $\pp$ \textit{always} refers to a prime ideal, and in the case that we are working in a discrete valuation ring we denote a choice of uniformizer by $\pi$. When necessary we add subscripts to indicate the relevant field we are working in. When working in a Dedekind domain we denote by $N\fraka$ the \textbf{norm} of a nonzero ideal $\fraka$, i.e the cardinality of $R/\fraka$.
    \item By a \textbf{local field} we mean a (non-trivially!) valued field whose additive group is locally compact with respect to the topology induced by the valuation. Note this convention includes $\rr$ and $\cc$, though they are sometimes excluded from the definition in other texts. 
    \item We say a local field $F$ is \textbf{Archimedean} if it is isomorphic to $\rr$ or $\cc$, and \textbf{non-Archimedean} otherwise. For $F$ non-Archimedean with absolute value $\abs{\cdot}_F$ we denote by $\calo_F$ its \textbf{valuation ring} (i.e the ring of elements with absolute value $\leq 1$).
    \item By a \textbf{global field} $K$ we mean a finite extension of $\qq$ (in which case we call $K$ a \textbf{number field}) or of $\ff_p(t)$ (in which case we call $K$ a \textbf{function field}). For any (non-trivial!) valuation $v$ on $K$ we denote by $K_v$ the \textbf{completion} of $K$ with respect to $v$; this is always a local field. When $K_v$ is non-Archimedean we write $\calo_v$ to denote the valuation ring of $K_v$. Note this convention particularly in the case of $K = \qq$; when we write $\zz_p$ we are \textit{always} referring to the $p$-adic integers! We assume the reader is familiar with the standard fact that every local field arises as the completion of a global field. 
    \item We denote by $\fanp_K$ the set of all \textbf{places} (i.e topological equivalence classes of absolute values) of a global field $K$. We may speak of a place $v$ being Archimedean, non-Archimededan, real, complex, if $K_v$ is Archimedean, non-Archimededan, real, complex, respectively. In particular when $\char(K) > 0$ it has no Archimedean places. 
    \item We recall that if $K'/K$ is an algebraic extension of fields than any nontrivial absolute value $\abs{\cdot}_K$ on $K$ extends uniquely to $K'$. Thus if $v \in \fanp_K$ and $w \in \fanp_{K'}$ are such that there exists an absolute value on $K$ corresponding to $v$ whose unique extension corresponds to $w \in \fanp_{K'}$, then we say $w$ \textbf{lies over} $v$, denoted $w \mid v$.
    \item We say a place $v$ of a global field $K$ is \textbf{infinite} if it is Archimedean, and \textbf{finite} otherwise. In particular, all places of a global function field are finite.
\end{itemize}


\textbf{Algebraic Geometry}
\begin{itemize}
    \item An \textbf{algebraic variety} $X$ is an integral, separated, finite-type affine scheme over a field $k$. An \textbf{algebraic curve} is a 1-dimensional algebraic variety. The \textbf{function field} $k(X)$ is the stalk of the (unique!) generic point of $X$, or equivalently the field of fractions of $\calo_X(U)$ for any non-empty open $U$.
    \item Recall the function field of a smooth proper algebraic curve over $\ff_q$ (hence projective!) is a global field of positive characteristic. We also assert the converse; that any positive-characteristic global field $K$ in which $\ff_q$ is algebraically closed  arises as the function field of some smooth projective algebraic curve geometrically connected over $\ff_q$ (see \S 1.6 of \cite{Hartshorne} for a detailed proof, modulo the notion of geometric connectedness).
    \item For any (separated) $k$-scheme $X$ with structure morphism $X \to \spec(k)$, if we let $\cali$ be the ideal of the diagonal subscheme of the fiber product $X \times_{\spec(k)} X$, we recall the \textbf{module of K\"ahler differentials} $\Omega^1_{X/k}$ is given by $\cali /\cali^2$ equipped with the $k$-linear derivation $\d: \calo_X \to \cali/\cali^2$ given by $f \mapsto f \tensor 1 - 1 \tensor f$. This is universal among $k$-linear derivations from $\calo_X$ to quasi-coherent $\calo_X$-modules $\calf$, in the sense that any such derivation $\delta: \calo_X \to \calf$ uniquely factors through it. Intuitively we may view it as the sheaf of 1-forms on $X$.
\end{itemize}

\chapter{Hello, Adele}
\label{chapter: Hello, Adele}

\begin{myquote}{In retrospect, we see now that the real numbers appear there as $\ldots$ neither more nor less interesting to the arithmetician than its $p$-adic companions, and that there is at least one language and one technique, that of the \textbf{adeles}, for bringing them all together under one roof and making them cooperate for a common purpose.}{Preface to \textit{Basic Number Theory}}{Andr\'e Weil}

\end{myquote}


The adelic language gives a beautiful solution to an age-old problem: that of doing Fourier analysis on a global field $K$. The classical approach was to pass to a tensor product of $K$ with $\rr$ (over $\qq$), but with the discovery of (infinitely many) non-trivial absolute values, it was not too far a stretch to imagine a structure that captured the information of all the completions at once, and "bringing them all together", in the words of Weil.\\

Instead of taking just the direct product of all the completions, however, we take the \textit{restricted direct product}, which imposes an "almost-everywhere integrality" condition on the elements. The reason for this is twofold: (1) to give the group a locally compact Hausdorff structure, without which we could not do the appropriate harmonic analysis, and (2) to make the natural embedding of $K$ discrete and cocompact, which will be invaluable.\\


This chapter proceeds in a somewhat reverse historical order. Section \ref{section: Adeles&Ideles} recounts the basic results on the adeles and their unit group (the ideles) in somewhat reverse historical order (or rather, one might say history discovered the ideles in the reverse order). We build the theory in this section with pedantry and care, to allow for a full appreciation for the nuances of the adelic formulation.

\begin{remark}
    Some texts (e.g \cite{bernstein2013introduction}) use the accented terms "ad\'eles" and "id\'eles", but Chevalley introduced these terms more as a wordplay on "ideals," and so they don't necessarily exactly correspond to French words. Besides, I've never seen accents used for these terms by a French writer other than Chevalley's original paper (see \cite{weil1974basic} and \cite{lang1994algebraic}), so I have chosen not to accent them in this text either.
\end{remark}

\newpage
\section{Adeles and Ideles}
\label{section: Adeles&Ideles}

\subsection{Introduction: Meeting the Adeles and Ideles}
\label{subsection: Intro Adeles and Ideles}

Recall the ring of profinite integers; 
\begin{equation*}
    \hat{\zz} := \invlim_{n \in \zz} \zz/n\zz = \prod_{p \text{ prime}} \zz_p,
\end{equation*}
where the inverse limit is taken over the natural maps of quotient rings ordered by divisibility. Imbued with the natural profinite topology, this is a compact Hausdorff space, and thus has all the nice properties that come along with that. 

\begin{warning}[Overloaded Notation]
Throughout this thesis, for a locally compact Hausdorff topological group $G$ we use $\hat{G}$ to denote the Pontryagin dual of $G$. This is visibly not what we mean by $\hat{\zz}$ above. Unfortunately the notation $\hat{R}$ is commonly to denote completions of rings, so distinguishing the two is simply a matter of context.
\end{warning}

Suppose we wanted to do a similar thing with $\qq$ and all of its completions. We run into a slight issue here, since $\prod \qq_p$ is not locally compact. Local compactness (and Hausdorfness) is rather necessary if one desires to do any sort of analysis with topological groups, since we would like to have a natural Haar measure on our group. Amending this issue is precisely the motivation behind the construction of the \textit{restricted product}, which we dive into now.\\


Let $J$ be an indexing set such that for every $v \in J$ we have a locally compact Hausdorff group $G_v$ indexed by $v$, satisfying the property that for $J_\infty$ a finite (possibly empty) subset of $J$, we have a compact open (and thus closed!) subgroup $H_v \leq G_v$ for all $v \in J_\infty$.

\begin{defn}
\label{def: resprod}
The \textbf{restricted direct product} (also just called the \textbf{restricted product}) of the $G_v$ with respect to the $H_v$ is defined as 
\begin{equation*}
    \rprod_{v \in J} G_v := \set{(x_v) \in \prod_{v \in J} G_v : x_v \in H_v \text{ for all but finitely many } v}.
\end{equation*}
\end{defn}

\begin{remark}[Notation]
The notation I've used for the restricted product (product and coproduct symbol overlayed on one another) is non-standard, but more distinct than the usual $\prod'$. I first saw this notation in \cite{MIT18.785notes}.
\end{remark}

Let us denote this restricted product as $G$. Note $G \subseteq \prod G_v$ (the usual direct product of groups). We want to topologize $G$, but the subspace topology induced by the product topology on $\prod G_v$ is insufficient. For reasons that will become clear later, we'll want subsets like $\prod_{v \in I} G_v \times \prod_{v \notin I} H_v$ to be open for finite $I \supseteq J_\infty$, which won't be true in the subspace topology unless $H_v = G_v$ for all but finitely many $v \in J$.

\begin{defn}
\label{def: resprod topology}
    The \textbf{restricted product topology} on $\rprod G_v$ is given by the following neighborhood basis of $g = (g_v)_v$:
    \begin{equation*}
        \set{\prod U_v : U_v \subseteq_{\text{open}} G_v, \ g_v \in U_v \text{ and } U_v = H_v \text{ for all but finitely many } v},
    \end{equation*}
    i.e. the (non-empty) open sets around $g$ are exactly the ones that contain $H_v$ in cofinitely many components, and arbitrary open neighborhoods of $g_v$ in the rest.
\end{defn}


\begin{observation}
Suppose we have a family of compact open subgroups $H'_v \subseteq G_v$ such that $H'_v = H_v$ for all but finitely many $v \in J$. Then we note that $\rprod G_v$ with respect to the $H_v$ is the same (both set-theoretically and topologically) as $\rprod G_v$ with respect to the $H'_v$. It thus suffices to specify the $H_v$ for all but finitely many $v$. A parallel observation we make is that for any finite positive integer $n$ we have the topological isomorphism:
\begin{align}
\label{eqn: resprod dir-prod isomorphism}
    \rprod_v G_v^n  &\cong \prod^n \brack{ \rprod_v G_v} \nonumber \\
    \brack{ \ldots, \brack{g_{v, 1}, \ldots, g_{v, n}}, \ldots}_v &\mapsto \brack{\brack{g_{v, 1}}_v, \ldots, \brack{g_{v, n}}_v},
\end{align}
where, naturally, $G^n_v$ is the $n$-fold direct-product of $G_v$ with itself. 
\end{observation}

We introduce a handy piece of notation that will come in useful later. Let $S$ be any finite subset of the index set $J$ containing $J_\infty$. Then we define
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn: G_S restricted prod}
    G_S := \prod_{v \in S} G_v \times \prod_{v \notin S} H_v,
\end{equation}
endowed with the usual product topology. We note, for any $S$, that this is by definition an open neighborhood of the identity $(e_v)_v$ of $G$. Moreover, for any finite $T \supseteq S$ consider the inclusion map $i_{S, T}: G_S \inj G_T$, which is an open embedding. Then we see that the restricted product $G$ is in fact the directed union of the $G_S$'s as open subspaces:
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn: resprod colim expression}
    G = \dlim_{S} G_S. 
\end{equation}
We leave it to the reader to verify this abstract-nonsense (but ultimately useful and simpler!) characterization, which ultimately comes down to checking the relevant universal property is satisfied.


\begin{prop}
\label{prop: restricted product properties}
Let $G_v, H_v$ be as defined above and $G$ be the restricted product. Then
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
    \item $G$ is a locally compact group.
    \item A subset $Y \subseteq G$ has compact closure if and only if $Y \subseteq \prod K_v$ for some family of compact subsets $K_v \subseteq G_v$ such that $K_v = H_v$ for all but finitely many $v$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
\underline{Part (a)}\\
We first verify that it is indeed a topological group. The group structure is clear; for continuity of the group maps, recall any basic open set is of the form
\begin{equation*}
    \calu := \prod_{v \in S} U_v \times \prod_{v \notin S} H_v
\end{equation*}
for some finite indexing set $S$ and opens $U_v \subseteq G_v$. Since each $H_v$ is a subgroup, the preimage of $\calu$ under the inversion map is:
\begin{equation*}
    \prod_{v \in S} \inv{\brack{U_v}} \times \prod_{v \notin S} H_v,
\end{equation*}
which is also open since each $G_v$ is a topological group, thereby giving us continuity of inversion.

For multiplication, the topological isomorphism in (\ref{eqn: resprod dir-prod isomorphism}) gives us $G \times G \cong \rprod_v (G_v \times G_v)$, allowing us to similarly look at preimages component by component. In particular, consider any element
\begin{equation*}
    \brack{ \brack{k_v, k'_v}_{v \in S}, \brack{k_v, k'_v}_{v \notin S}}
\end{equation*}
in the preimage of $\calu$ under the group law $m = \rprod_v m_v$, defined in the natural way. By definition, away from some finite index set $T \supseteq S$, we must have that $(k_v, k'_v) \in H_v \times H_v$ for all $v \notin T$, and clearly $H_v \times H_v$ is in the pre-image of $\calu$. For any $v \in T$, we know from the fact that $G_v$ is a topological group that $(k_v, k'_v)$ has some open neighborhood $W_v \times W'_v$ contained in the pre-image of $U_v$. Thus:
\begin{equation*}
    \brack{ \brack{k_v, k'_v}_{v \in S}, \brack{k_v, k'_v}_{v \notin S}}  \in \prod_{v \in T} \brack{W_v \times W'_v} \times \prod_{v \notin T} \brack{H_v \times H_v} \subseteq \inv{m}(\calu),  
\end{equation*}
and so multiplication is continuous. Thus $G$ is a topological group, and it remains to show local compactness. Since each $H_v$ is compact, Tychonoff tells us $\prod_{v \notin S} H_v$ is also compact. Thus $G_S$ is the product of finitely many locally compact spaces with a compact space, and is thus itself locally compact. Moreover, the product topology on $G_S$ is the \textit{same} as the subspace topology induced by $G$, and since any $(x_v) \in G$ is contained in such a $G_S$, we conclude $G$ must be locally compact as well.\\

\underline{Part (b)}\\
If $Y$ is contained in some product of compact sets $ K = \prod K_v$ (which is compact by Tychonoff) its closure $\bar{Y}$ is a closed subset of a compact subset of a Hausdorff space $G$, and thus $\bar{Y}$ is compact too, proving one direction.

For the other direction, suppose $\bar{Y}$ is a compact subset of $G$. Since the open sets $G_S$ defined above cover $G$, a finite number of such $G_S$ must cover $\bar{Y}$. Note a union of a finite number of $G_S$ is just a set of the form $G_{\fans}$, for $\fans$ the intersection of all the index sets $S$. Since the projection maps $\rho_v: G \to G_v$ are continuous, each $\rho_v(\bar{Y}) \subseteq G_v$ is compact. And since all but finitely many components of $G_{\fans}$ are equal to $H_v$, it follows $\rho_v(\bar{Y}) \subseteq H_v$ for all but finitely many $v$. Thus $\bar{Y}$, and therefore $Y$, is contained in a set of the desired form.
\end{proof}

\begin{warning}
    Our work above seems (and may very well be) a tad pedantic with the details. However it is worth being careful about constructions such as this; one might be tempted to think that if we have \textit{any} directed system of topological groups with inclusion that the colimit (with the colimit topology) must surely be a topological group. The editors of MIT's second-edition \textit{Encyclopedic Dictionary of Mathematics} thought so too (see Article 210, pg. 805). This turns out not to be the case: the colimit of the directed system of $G_i := \qq \times \rr^i$ does not have continuous addition. The key issue here is that colimit topologies can fail to commute with taking direct products. However \cite{Shimomura-topgrp-colimit} shows that we do get a nice output in this colimit construction when the groups are all locally compact.
\end{warning}



\begin{observation}
    For each $v$ we have a natural topological embedding $G_v \inj G$ into the $v$-th component of the restricted product. Thus we naturally identify $G_v$ with a closed subgroup of $G$.
\end{observation}


We are now ready to define the adeles. Let $K$ be a global field, and let $K_v$ denote its completion at a place $v$. With respect to addition, note $K_v$ is a locally compact additive group. For all finite places $v$, recall $K_v$ admits its local ring of integers $\calo_v$ as an open compact subgroup.

\begin{defn}
    The \textbf{adele group} $\aa_K$ of a global field $K$ is the restricted direct product of $K_v$ with respect to the open subgroups $\calo_v$. Since this admits a natural topological ring structure as well, it is also referred to as the \textbf{adele ring}.
\end{defn}

Note we have an embedding
\begin{equation*}
\begin{split}
    K & \inj \aa_K\\
    x &\mapsto (x, x, \ldots)
\end{split}
\end{equation*}
This is well-defined since $K$ always has a natural embedding in $K_v$ for any place $v$, and any $x \in K$ is integral (i.e has absolute value $\leq 1$) at almost all places $v \in \fanp_K$.


Similarly, for each place $v$ we can conisder $K_v^\times$, the locally compact group of units of $K_v$ with respect to multiplication. Then the local integral units $\calo_v^\times$ are an open compact subgroup of each $K_v^\times$.
\begin{defn}
The \textbf{idele group} $\ii_K$ of a global field $K$ is the restricted direct product of the $K_v^\times$ with respect to $\calo_v^\times$.
\end{defn}

We have a similar embedding
\begin{equation*}
    \begin{split}
    K^\times &\inj \ii_K\\
    x &\mapsto (x, x, \ldots)
    \end{split}
\end{equation*}
which is again well-defined by a similar argument as with the adeles. This motivates the following definition.

\begin{defn}
\label{def: principal adeles}
The elements in the image of the diagonal embedding of $K$ (resp. $K^\times$) into $\aa_K$ (resp. $\ii_K$) are called the \textbf{principal adeles} (resp. \textbf{principal ideles}).
\end{defn}


\begin{warning}
The notation $\ii_K$ and $\aa^\times_K$ is used interchangeably in some literature, but one should be careful; although the group $\ii_K$ is \textit{algebraically} the same as the group of units $\aa_K^\times$, this isn't a topological homeomorphism if we take the naive subspace topology $\aa_K^\times \subseteq \aa_K$. In general, recall for any topological ring $R$, we topologize the unit group group $R^\times$ by the subspace topology of its image under the injection
\begin{align*}
R^\times &\inj R \times R\\
r &\mapsto (r, \inv{r}),
\end{align*}
where of course the codomain is given the natural product topology. We do this because taking the subspace topology on $R^\times$ from $R$ may fail to make it a topological group, as in the Example below.
\end{warning}

\begin{example}
    \label{example: subspace neq idelic topology}
    Take $K = \qq$, and consider the sequence of ideles $(1, 1 + n!, 1 + n!, \ldots) \in \aa_\qq^\times$, i.e 1 at the infinite place and $1 + n!$ at all the finite places. For any prime $p$ we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{n \to \infty}  \abs{1 - (1 + n!)}_p = \lim_{n \to \infty} p^{- \ord_p(n!)} = 0,
    \end{equation*}
    and thus this sequence converges to 1 in the subspace topology of $\aa_\qq^\times$. If $\aa_\qq^\times$ were a topological group, we should expect its reciprocal $(1, 1/(1 + n!), 1/(1 + n!), \ldots)$ to also converge to 1, by continuity of inversion, but we show now this is not the case.

    To see more explicitly what's happening, take an open neighborhood, in the \textit{subspace topology}, of $1 \in \aa_\qq^\times$. Every such neighborhood has the form
    \begin{equation*}
        U := \brack{\rr \times \prod_{p \in S}U_p \times \prod_{p \notin S}\zz_p} \cap \aa_\qq^\times,
    \end{equation*}
    where $S$ is some finite collection of primes, and $U_p$ is a neighborhood of $1 \in \qq_p$ for each $p \in S$. However, consider the following set:
    \begin{equation*}
        V := \rr^\times \times \prod_{p < \infty} \zz_p^\times,
    \end{equation*}
    which is visibly open in the idelic topology. We claim $U \nsubseteq V$ for any $U$ described above. The reason for the non-inclusion is that for any $U$ we can select some $(x_p)_p \in \aa_\qq^\times$ such that $x_p \in \zz_p \setminus \zz_p^\times$ for some $p \notin S$. This thus means $(x_p)_p \notin V$. In particular, this is why the sequence $(1, 1/(1+n!), 1/(1+n!), \ldots)$ doesn't converge to $1$ in the idelic topology: for no $n$ does that element lie in the set $V$ constructed above, since $1+n!$ is always divisible by \textit{some} prime. Thus we see that the relative open sets are too coarse, i.e. the idelic topology is \textit{finer} than the induced subspace topology. 
\end{example}
    
The natural question, then, is whether the topology on $\ii_K$ as the unit group of $\aa_K$ is the restricted product topology. The answer, as it neatly turns out, is yes!

\begin{prop}
\label{prop: alternative idelic topology.}
The subspace topology on $\ii_K \subseteq \aa_K \times \aa_K$ (the image under the embedding $u \mapsto (u, \inv{u})$) is exactly the restricted product topology.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof} 
Suppose $\aa_K^\times$ is given the idelic topology, and let $\phi: \aa_K^\times \to \ii_K$ be the natural bijection. It is relatively clear that $\phi$ is an open map; indeed, for any basic open set in the idelic topology
\begin{equation*}
    U = \prod_{v \in S} U_v \times \prod_{v \notin S} \calo_v^\times,
\end{equation*}
we have that the image of $U$ is:
\begin{equation*}
    \phi(U) = \brack{\brack{\prod_{v \in S} U_v \times \prod_{v \notin S}\calo_v^\times } \times \brack{ \prod_{v \in S} \inv{U_v} \times \prod_{v \notin S} \calo_v^\times }} \cap \ii_K,
\end{equation*}
which is visibly open in the topology of $\ii_K$.

Thus it remains to show $\phi$ is continuous if $\aa_K^\times$ is given the idelic topology. Take an open neighborhood $U$ of the multiplicative identity of $\aa_K \times \aa_K$. Any such neighborhood contains one  of the form
\begin{equation*}
    U = \brack{\prod_{v \in S} U_{1, v} \times \prod_{v \notin S} \calo_v } \times \brack{\prod_{v \in T} U_{2, v} \times \prod_{v \notin T} \calo_v },
\end{equation*}
where $S, T$ are finite indexing sets containing the infinite places. In the following expressions, for any set $S_v \subseteq K_v$ we let $S^\times_v$ be shorthand for $S_v \cap K_v^\times$. Taking intersection of $U$ with the image of $\phi$ gives us the intersection of $\ii_K$ with the following set:
\begin{align*}
    U \cap \phi(\aa_K^\times) = & \prod_{v \in S \cap T} \brack{U_{v, 1}^\times \cap \inv{\brack{U_{v, 2}^\times}}} \times \brack{\inv{\brack{U_{v, 1}^\times}} \cap U_{v, 2}^\times}\\
& \times  \prod_{v \in S \setminus (S \cap T)} \brack{U_{v, 1} \cap \calo_v^\times } \times \brack{\inv{\brack{U_{v, 1}^\times}} \cap \calo_v^\times }\\
& \times  \prod_{v \in T \setminus (S \cap T)} \brack{\calo_v^\times \cap \inv{\brack{U_{v, 2}^\times}}} \times \brack{\calo_v^\times \cap U_{v, 2}^\times}\\
& \times  \prod_{v \notin S \cup T} \calo_v^\times \times \calo_v^\times.
\end{align*}
Note in the above (rather heinous) expression we used our observation in (\ref{eqn: resprod dir-prod isomorphism}) to "absorb" the direct product of $\aa_K$ into the restricted product itself. In any case, pulling back to $\aa_K^\times$ corresponds to just looking at the first coordinate in each of the products. We note that for all the $v \in S \cup T$ the set in the $v$-th component is visibly an open neigborhood of the identity, and since this is still a finite set the pullback is indeed equal to $\calo_v^\times$ on cofinitely many components. We deduce it is an open set of the idelic topology, thereby proving $\phi$ is continuous and thus a homeomorphism onto its image.
\end{proof}



It will be useful to explore the behavior of the adeles under base-change - in particular, extending scalars by to finite separable extensions. This type of technique will allow us to show results for any $\aa_K$ by just considering $K = \qq$ or $K= \ff_p(t)$.

\begin{prop}[Adelic Base Change]
\label{prop: adelic base change}
Take $E/K$ a finite separable extension of global fields, and let $\set{u_1, \ldots u_n}$ be a $K$-basis of $E$. Then, recalling we have a canonical isomorphism $E \tensor_K K_v \bij \prod_{w \mid v} E_w$, the natural map
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: A_E to A_K^n iso}
    \begin{split}
    \alpha: \bigdsum^n_{j=1} \aa_K &\to \aa_E\\
    ((x_{v, j})_v)_j &\mapsto \sum_j u_j(x_{v, j})_v
    \end{split}
\end{equation}
is an isomorphism of topological groups.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
For every place $v$ of $K$, we define:
\begin{equation*}
    E_v := \prod_{w \mid v} E_w = E \tensor_K K_v.
\end{equation*}
where the product is taken over all places $w$ of $E$ lying above $v$, of which there are at most $n$. This is manifestly a topological algebra over $K_v$, into which we have a natural diagonal embedding. We also recall the classical fact that $E_v$ admits $\set{u_1, \ldots, u_n}$ as a $K_v$-basis, and thus the map
\begin{align*}
    \alpha_v :  K_v^{\dsum n} & \bij E_v\\
    (x_j) &\mapsto \sum_j u_j x_j
\end{align*}
is an algebraic and topological isomorphism of $K_v$-vector spaces.

In a similar vein, for $v \nmid \infty$ we can define
\begin{equation*} 
    \calo_{E_v} = \prod_{w \mid v} \calo_{E_w},
\end{equation*}
and again the $u_j$'s define a topological and algebraic isomorphism 
\begin{equation*}
    \alpha_v : \calo_{K_v}^{\dsum n} \bij \calo_{E_v}
\end{equation*}
for all $v$ away from a finite set $S_0$ of places containing the Archimedean ones. For any $S \supseteq S_0$, consider the products $\aa_{K, S}$ and $\aa_{E, S}$ as defined in (\ref{eqn: G_S restricted prod}). We note then that:
\begin{equation*}
    \brack{\aa_{K, S}}^{\dsum n} = \bigdsum^n_{i=1} \brack{\prod_{v \in S} K_{v, i} \times \prod_{v \notin S}\calo_{K_v, i}} \cong \prod_{v \in S}E_v \times \prod_{v \notin S} \calo_{E_v} =: \aa_{E, S}.
\end{equation*}
Thus for any such $S$, by appropriately restricting the collection $\set{\alpha_v}$ induces a map
\begin{equation*}
    \alpha_S := \brack{\aa_{K, S}}^{\dsum n} \bij \aa_{E, S}.
\end{equation*}
Recalling our abstract-nonsense equivalent definition of the adeles in (\ref{eqn: resprod colim expression}), we deduce that the map $\alpha$, as the colimit of the directed system of isomorphisms under inclusions, is a topological and algebraic isomorphism.
\end{proof}





\subsection{Adelic Characters and Measures}
\label{subsection: Characters and Measures}

We review the basic notion of Pontryagin dual before continuing.

\begin{defn}
    \label{def: character and quasi-character}
Let $G$ be a locally compact topological group. 
\begin{itemize}
\item A \textbf{quasi-character} of $G$ is an element $\chi \in \Hom_{\mathrm{cont}}(G, \cc^\times)$.
\item A \textbf{character} of $G$ is an element $\chi \in \Hom_{\mathrm{cont}}(G, S^1)$.
\end{itemize}    
\end{defn}


\begin{remark}
    Some texts refer to "quasi-characters" as "characters" and refer to "characters" as "unitary characters". We use the other convention because it is consistent with Tate's formulation in his thesis \cite{TatesThesis} and because it gives us an excuse to say and write the prefix "quasi".
\end{remark}

Pontryagin duality is the necessarily bedrock upon which one builds the theory of Fourier analysis on (locally compact Hausdorff) groups. Our goal will be to show the adeles are in-fact self-dual, hence giving us a nice Fourier transform on this space.

We first begin with the construction of the so-called \textit{standard characters} $\psi_F$ of local fields, as these are the building blocks of the adelic characters. Throughout we denote by $F$ a local field, and in the non-Archimedean case we write $\calo_F$ for its (local!) valuation ring with unique maximal ideal $\pp$ and uniformizer $\pi$.

\begin{itemize}
    \item For $F = \rr$, we define
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eqn: standard character on R}
        \psi(x) := e^{- 2\pi i x}.
    \end{equation}
    \item For $F = \qq_p$, we define our character by the composition of the following maps:
    \begin{equation*}
        \psi: \qq_p \surj \qq_p/\zz_p \bij \zz[1/p]/\zz \inj S^1,
    \end{equation*}
    where the first map is the canonical quotient map, the second is the inverse of the map $\alpha + \zz \mapsto \alpha + \zz_p$, and the last is the restriction of the canonical isomorphism $\rr/\zz \bij S^1$ via $e^{2 \pi i x}$. Calculating this chain of maps, it is not too hard to verify that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eqn: standard character on Q_p}
        \psi(x) := e^{2 \pi i r_x /p^{n_x}},
    \end{equation}
    where $n_x$ is a non-negative integer such that $p^{n_x} x \in \zz_p$, and $r_x \in \zz$ represents $p^{n_x} x$ modulo $p^{n_x}$.
    \item All the remainder of the characteristic zero cases fall under when $F'/F$ a finite separable extension of any of the above $F$, so we may define
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eqn: standard character on any local field}
        \psi_{F'}(x) := \psi_F \brack{\tr_{F'/F} (x)}.
    \end{equation}
    \item In positive characteristic (i.e $F \cong \ff_q(\!(t)\!)$ for some choice of uniformizer $t = \pi_F$), we may consider the module of \textit{continuous} K\"ahler differentials, whose construction we recall here. First consider the following inverse limit.
    \begin{equation*}
       \hat{\Omega}^1_{\calo_F/\ff_q} := \invlim_{N \geq 1} \Omega^1_{(\calo_F/\brangle{\pi_F^N})/\ff_q}.
    \end{equation*}
    Note each term of this inverse limit is given by $\calo_F/ \brangle{N t^{N-1}, t^N} \dt$, and thus the limit is a rank one free $\calo_F$-module. By construction we see this to be the initial object in the category of finitely generated $\calo_F$-modules $M$ with an $\ff_q$-linear derivation $\calo_F \to M$. We then take the ``generic fiber"
    \begin{equation*}
       \hat{\Omega}^1_{F/\ff_q} := F \tensor_{\calo_F} \hat{\Omega}^1_{\calo_F/\ff_q},
    \end{equation*}
    which again by construction is seen to be initial among $F$-vector spaces $V$ with $\ff_q$-linear derivations $F \to V$. This is one-dimensional over $F$, and so any element of it may be written in the form $f \dt$ for $f \in F$. Expressing it in its Laurent series expansion $f = \sum a_i t^i$, we may define the \textbf{residue} of $\omega = f \dt$ (with respect to $t$) as the coefficient $a_{-1}$ of $\dt/t$. One can show (e.g in Chapter II of \cite{serre2012algebraic}) that this value is independent of choice of uniformizer $t$. Thus, making some choice of nonzero $\omega \in \hat{\Omega}^1_{F/\ff_q}$, we may define the nontrivial additive character
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eqn: standard character on F_p((t))}
        \psi(x) := \exp \brack{\frac{2 \pi i}{p} \tr_{\ff_q/\ff_p}(\res(x \omega))}.
    \end{equation}
\end{itemize}

\begin{defn}
    \label{def: standard character}
We will call the characters described in (\ref{eqn: standard character on R}), (\ref{eqn: standard character on Q_p}), (\ref{eqn: standard character on F_p((t))}) and (\ref{eqn: standard character on any local field}) as the \textbf{standard characters} of their respective local fields.
\end{defn}

\begin{warning}
In characteristic zero, the above definition is in some-sense God-given, as the quotient and trace maps involved in the constructions are canonical. In positive characteristic the above definition will always be dependent on some non-canonical \textit{choice} of $\omega \in \hat{\Omega}^1_{F/\ff_q}$.
\end{warning}

In the function field case, the standard character has the following \textit{global} geometric interpretation: consider $X$ a smooth proper geometrically connected algebraic curve over $\ff_q$ with function field $K$. Let $\Omega^1_{K/\ff_q}$ be the one-dimensional $K$-module of meromorphic 1-forms on $X$ (i.e the stalk at the generic point of the $\calo_X$-module of K\"ahler differentials $\Omega^1_{X/\ff_q}$). Any $\omega \in \Omega^1_{K/\ff_q}$ can be written as $f \dt$ for some $f \in K$, with some choice of separating transcendence basis $\set{t}$ over $\ff_q$. Pick some $v \in \fanp_K$, corresponding to a point of $X$, and assume the function $t: X \to \bp^1_{\ff_q}$ is unramified and does not map to infinity at this point. Then the coefficient $a_{-1}$ in the expansion of $f$ seen as an element of $K_v$ (which is isomorphic to $ \ff_{q_v}(\!(t - t_v)\!)$) is the \textbf{residue} of $\omega$ at $v$, written $\res_v(\omega)$. We refer the reader to Chapter II of \cite{serre2012algebraic} for a more detailed exposition. Later we will see that this formulation is practically necessary for defining an adelic character in a clean way. 

We now build the notion of a conductor of a (quasi-)character, which will be an invaluable tool in computations later.

\begin{defn}
\label{def: conductor}
Let $\psi$ be a nontrivial additive quasi-character on a non-Archimedean local field $F$. Then the \textbf{conductor} of $\psi$ is the ideal $\pp^m$, where 
\begin{equation*}
    m := \min \set{r \in \zz : \psi \vert_{\pp^r} = 1},
\end{equation*}
i.e the conductor is the largest fractional ideal of $\calo_F$ on which $\psi$ is trivial.\\
Now suppose $\chi$ is a multiplicative quasi-character on $F^\times$. Then its \textbf{conductor} is $\pp^n$, where
\begin{equation*}
    n := \min \set{r \geq 0 : \chi \vert_{1 + \pp^r} = 1},
\end{equation*}
i.e the conductor is the largest possible unit congruence subgroup of $\calo_F$ on which $\chi$ is trivial. By convention, if $\chi$ is trivial on $\calo_F^\times$ then its conductor is $\calo_F$ (i.e $n = 0$).
\end{defn}

\begin{remark}[Terminology]
    The origin of the term "conductor" comes from Dedekind, who denoted by "\textit{F\"uhrer}" (whose meaning was closer to "guide", "attendant" or "conductor" in the original German, rather than its modern assocations) a nonzero ideal of an order in a number field, which is why classical texts denote conductors using $\frakf$. Sometimes the term "conductor" also refers to integers $m$ and $n$ themselves rather than just the ideals. In the case that $m = 0$ (resp. $n= 0$), we will say that $\psi$ (resp. $\chi$) has \textbf{trivial conductor}.
\end{remark}




\begin{theorem}[Local-Fields are Self-Dual]
    \label{thm: self-duality of local fields}
    Let $F$ be a local field equipped with an absolute value $\abs{\cdot}$, and fix a non-trivial continuous additive character $\psi \in \hat{F}$. Then the map
    \begin{align*}
        \Psi: F & \mapsto \hat{F}\\
        a & \mapsto (x \mapsto \psi(ax))
    \end{align*}
    is an isomorphism of topological groups.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Since left-multiplication gives a continuous automorphism of the additive group of a field, we immediately see the map $\Psi$ is a well-defined group homomorphism. We also see it is injective by the fact that $\psi$ was assumed to be non-trivial, so $\ker(\Psi) = 0$. So it remains to show $\Psi$ is a surjective homeomorphism.

We begin by showing $\Psi$ is a continuous map. For $\lambda, \epsilon \in \rr_{> 0}$, note sets of the form
\begin{equation*}
    W(\lambda, \epsilon) := \set{\chi \in \hat{F} : \abs{1 - \chi(x)} \leq \epsilon, \ \forall x \in \bar{B_{\lambda}(0)} \subseteq F}
\end{equation*}
form a neighborhood base for the trivial character in the compact-open topology. Fix such an open set for choice of $\epsilon, \lambda$, and now for continuity it suffices to show there exists some open neighborhood $U$ of $0 \in F$ carried into $W(\lambda, \epsilon)$ via $\Psi$. However, note by continuity of our original character $\psi$ that there exists some $\delta > 0$ such that 
\begin{equation*}
    x \in B_\delta(0) \implies \abs{1- \psi(x)} < \epsilon.
\end{equation*}
Then note the open set $U := B_{\delta/\lambda}$ suffices, since for any $y \in U$ and $x \in \bar{B_{\lambda}(0)}$ we have $\abs{xy} < \delta$, and so $\abs{1 - \Psi(y)(x)} < \epsilon$. Thus $\Psi$ is continuous. Running the construction in reverse, we can also easily verify that $\Psi$ is in fact an open map (thus a homeomorphism) onto its image.

Finally we need to show surjectivity. We claim that the image $H := \Psi(F)$ is first of all complete (as a topological group, not a metric space!). Take any Cauchy sequence $\set{\Psi(x_n)}$ in $H$, then we claim $\set{x_n}$ must also be Cauchy in $F$. Suppose not, then there exists an infinite sequence of distinct (and hence arbitrarily large) positive integers $\set{n_i}_i$ and some $\epsilon > 0$ such that we could bound $\abs{x_{n_i} - x_{n_j}} > \epsilon$ for all $i \neq j$. Fixing some $c \in F$ for which $\psi(c) \neq 1$, which exists by the nontriviality assumption, we may consider closed ball of radius $\lambda := \abs{c}_F/\epsilon$. By construction then, for any $i \neq j$ we would have some $y_{i, j} \in F$ such that $(x_i - x_j) y_{i, j} = c$. However, then we would have
\begin{equation*}
    \abs{1 - \Psi(x_{n_i} - x_{n_j})(y_{i, j})} = \abs{1 - \psi(x_{n_i} - x_{n_j})(y_{i, j})} = \abs{1 - \psi(c)}  \ \forall i \neq j.
\end{equation*}
But this contradicts the Cauchy property of $\set{\Psi(x_n)}$ in the compact open topology, which says that for all $n$ larger than some fixed $N$ we must have $\abs{1 - \Psi(x_n)(y)} <  \abs{1 - \psi(c)}$ for all $y \in \bar{B}_{0, \lambda}$. Thus the sequence $\set{x_n}$ must also be Cauchy. 

In particular, completeness of $F$ implies completeness of its image $H$ under $\Psi$, and thus $H$ is a closed subgroup of $\hat{F}$. Therefore, both $H$ and $\hat{F}/ H$ are LCA groups, and by Pontryagin duality considerations we can identify 
\begin{equation*}
    \hat{\hat{F}/H} \cong \set{x \in \ \doublehat{F} (= F) : x(h) = 1 \ \forall h \in H },
\end{equation*}
where of course by $x$ we mean the "evaluation at $x$" map. But by nontriviality of our original $\psi$, there exists some $y \in F$ for which $\psi(y) \neq 1$, so for any $x \in F \setminus \set{0}$ we note that $\psi_{y \inv{x}} \in H$ evaluates to $\psi(y) \neq 1$ on $x$. It follows that $\hat{\hat{F}/H}$ is trivial, from which we deduce $H = \hat{F}$, giving us surjectivity.
\end{proof}



We now proceed to discuss the theory of characters on the adelic groups, with the key result being that of Pontryagin duality commuting with the restricted product. This result in fact holds for any restricted product, so the following analysis is done in greater generality so we can hit the adeles and ideles with one stone, as it were.



\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma: characters on restricted products}
As with the notation of Definition \ref{def: resprod}, let $\set{G_v}_v$ be a countable set of locally-compact abelian Hausdorff groups with compact open respective subgroups $H_v$ for almost all $v$, and let $G$ be their restricted product. 
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
    \item (Breaking Down) Any quasi-character $\chi \in \Hom_{cont}(G, \cc^\times)$ is trivial on almost all $H_v$.
    \item (Building Up) Fix quasi-characters $\chi_v \in \Hom_{cont}(G_v, \cc^\times)$ such that $\chi_v \vert_{H_v} = 1$ for almost all $v$. Then $\chi := \prod \chi_v$ lies in $\Hom_{cont}(G, \cc^\times)$.
\end{enumerate}
The same results hold if we replace every instance of "quasi-character" with "character".
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\underline{Breaking Down}\\
Since $\cc^\times$ has "no small subgroups", we may fix a neighborhood $V \ni 1$ that does not contain any non-trivial subgroup. By considering pre-images we know there must exist some open neighborhod $U  = \prod U_v$ of the identity of $G$ such that $\chi(U) \subseteq V$. Let $S$ denote the (finite!) index set such that $U_v \neq H_v$ exactly when $v \in S$. Consider the following subgroup of $G$:
\begin{equation*}
    H := \prod_{v \in S} {1} \times \prod_{v \notin S} H_v \subseteq U.
\end{equation*}
Note that $\chi(H) \subseteq U$, but since its image must be a group it must be $\set{1}$, giving us what we want. In particular, for any $x = (x_v)_v \in G$, if we define
\begin{equation*}
    \chi_v(x_v) := \chi( \ldots, 1, 1, x_v, 1, 1, \ldots ),
\end{equation*}
we deduce for any $x$ that $\chi_v \vert_{H_v} = 1$ almost everywhere, and $\chi = \prod \chi_v$.\\

\underline{Building Up}\\
By checking definitions one confirms that $\chi$ is a well-defined homomorphism $G \to \cc^\times$, so it remains to verify continuity. Let $S$ denote the the (finite!) set of indices where $\chi_v \vert_{H_v} \neq 1$, and let $s := \abs{S}$. For any subset $X \subseteq G$, we can define 
\begin{equation*}
    X^{(s)} := \set{\prod_{i = 1}^s g_i : g_i \in X},
\end{equation*}
i.e. the set of all elements of $G$ that are formed as products of exactly $s$ elements of $X$. Note that for any neighborhood $V \ni 1 $ in $\cc^\times$, there must exist some other neighborhood $W$ such that $W^{(s)} \subseteq V$ (one could achieve this by taking an arbitrarily small ball around 1, for example). By a standard result of character theory, for every $v \in S$ we must have some neighborhood $U_v$ of the identity of $G_v$ such that $\chi(U_v) \subseteq W$. It follows that:
\begin{equation*}
    \prod_{v \in S} U_v \times \prod_{v \notin S} H_v \subseteq \inv{\chi}(W^{(s)}) \subseteq \inv{\chi}(V),
\end{equation*}
from which it follows that $\chi$ is continuous.
\end{proof}

We now have the tools needed to construct the dual group of the restricted product. For each component $G_v$, by local compactness we have the dual group $\hat{G_v}$ of continuous characters that satisfies Pontryagin duality. Let $J_\infty$ be the finite index set corresponding to infinite places, then for every $v \notin J_\infty$ we define
\begin{equation*}
    K(G_v, H_v) := \set{\chi_v \in \hat{G_v} : \chi_v \vert_{H_v} = 1},
\end{equation*}
i.e characters with trivial restriction to $H_v$. By selecting a neighborhood $V \ni 1$ in $S^1$ small enough to not contain any non-trivial subgroups of $S^1$, we note that $K(G_v, H_v)$ is a basis element of the compact-open topology of $\hat{G_v}$, and so is open in $\hat{G_v}$.\\
Since $\chi_v \in K(G_v, H_v)$ is trivial on $H_v$, it factors through the quotient $G_v/H_v$, inducing a topological group isomorphism
\begin{equation*}
    K(G_v, H_v) \bij \hat{G_v/H_v}.
\end{equation*}
By openness of $H_v \subseteq G_v$, we get that the quotient $G_v/H_v$ is a discrete group, and standard character theory, the dual $\hat{G_v/H_v} \cong K(G_v, H_v)$ must be compact. As such, with respect to the compact open subgroups $K(G_v, H_v)$, it makes sense to define the restricted product of the dual groups $\hat{G_v}$.

\begin{theorem}[Restricted Product Commutes with Dual Groups]
    \label{thm: resprod commutes with dual grps}
    With the notation above, we have an isomorphism of topological groups
    \begin{equation*}
        \hat{G} \bij \rprod \hat{G_v}.
    \end{equation*}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Consider the map 
\begin{align*}
    \phi: \rprod \hat{G_v} & \to \hat{G}\\
(\chi_v)_v &\mapsto \prod_v \chi_v .
\end{align*}
By the construction of the restricted product and Lemma \ref{lemma: characters on restricted products}, this is a well-defined group isomorphism. It remains to show $\phi$ is a homeomorphism.

By translation invariance of open sets in topological groups it suffices to prove bicontinuity at the identity in the domain and codomain. From part (b) of Proposition \ref{prop: restricted product properties}, we recall any compact neighborhood of the identity $K \subseteq G$ is of the form $\prod K_v$ for compact $K_v$ that are equal to $H_v$ almost everywhere. For $U$ an open neighborhood of $1 \in S^1$, consider the open neighborhood of the identity
\begin{equation*}
    W(K, U) := \set{\chi \in \hat{G} : \chi(K) \subseteq U},
\end{equation*}
i.e a basis element of the compact-open topology on $\hat{G}$. We want to show any such $W(K, U)$ is open. For any $\chi \in W(K, U)$, let $S$ be the (finite!) set of indices for which $\chi$ is non-trivial on $K_v$, and let $s = \abs{S}$. As in the Lemma {lemma: characters on restricted products}, we can select some neighorhood $V \ni 1$ in $\cc^\times$ such that $V^{(s)} \subseteq U$, and consider the following open neighborhood of the identity in $\rprod \hat{G_v}$:
\begin{equation*}
    X := \prod_{v \in S} W(K_v, V) \times \prod_{v \notin S} K(G_v, H_v) \subseteq_{\text{open}} \rprod \hat{G_v}.
\end{equation*}
We note that $\inv{\phi}(\chi) \in X$ by construction, so in particular we have $\phi(X) \subseteq W(K, U)$, and so $\phi$ is continuous.\\
For continuity in the other direction, let $X = \prod W(K_v, U)$ be an open neighborhood of the identity in $\rprod \hat{G_v}$. Then, note
\begin{equation*}
    \hat{G} \supseteq W(\prod K_v, U) \supseteq \phi(X),
\end{equation*}
proving continuity in the other direction.
\end{proof}

The above result tells us the following neat isomorphism for the dual adele and idele groups, which will allow us to work component-wise without worry.

\begin{corollary}
    \label{cor: adeles and ideles dual commutes w resprod}
    For a global field K,
    \begin{equation*}
        \hat{\aa_K} \cong \rprod \hat{K_v}, \quad \ \hat{\ii_K} \cong \rprod \hat{K_v^\times}, 
    \end{equation*}
    where the restricted products are taken with respect to the various (compact!) $\hat{K_v/\calo_v}$ and $\hat{K_v^\times /\calo_v^\times}$ respectively.
\end{corollary}

The key step to showing self duality of the adeles (which is ultimately what will put our adelic Fourier theory on a sound basis) will be the construction of \textit{some} nontrivial additive character on $\aa_K$ with the property that it is trivial on the canonically embedded $K \inj \aa_K$.  This property will be key to deducing results like the adelic Poisson summation formula in \S \ref{subsection: Adelic Fourier Theory}, which will be crucial to showing the functional equation of the global zeta function in \S \ref{subsection: global zeta function} -- the crux of Tate's thesis!


\begin{prop}
    \label{prop: exists non-trivial adelic character}
For any global field $K$, there exists a non-trivial character $\psi$ on $\aa_K$ that is trivial on the image of $K$ under the diagonal embedding. We call these \textbf{standard} adelic characters.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
When $K$ is a number field, on each component we may choose the character defined in Definition \ref{def: standard character}; since this is given by $\psi_v := \psi_p \circ \tr_{K_v/\qq_p}$ and $\psi_p$ has conductor $\zz_p$ by construction, it is clear that $\psi_v$ has conductor $\inv{\tr}(\zz_p) = \inv{\frakD_v}$. This is trivial -- i.e equal to $\calo_v$ -- at all the (cofinitely many!) $v \in \fanp_K$ unramified over $\qq$, so the global character
\begin{align*}
    \psi_K: \aa_K &\to S^1\\
    (x_v) & \mapsto \prod_v \psi_v(x_v).
\end{align*}
is in fact well-defined. This character is non-trivial, since $\psi_v$ are all non-trivial characters, so we can see that the $\psi(\ldots, 1, x_v, 1, \ldots) \neq 1$ for any $x_v$ such that $\psi_v(x_v) \neq 1$.

To verify triviality on $K$ it suffices to consider the case $K = \qq$ by the identity $\psi_K = \psi_\qq \circ \tr{\aa_K/\aa_\qq}$ (which is easily verified from our construction in (\ref{eqn: standard character on any local field})). For any $x \in \qq$, recall we have a unique factorization
\begin{equation*}
    x = b + \sum_{p \text{ prime}} \frac{a_p}{p^{\ord_p(x)}}, \ a_p, b \in \zz,
\end{equation*}
where $a_p$ is nonzero for only finitely many primes. Recalling the expression for our $\psi_v$ defined in (\ref{eqn: standard character on R}) and (\ref{eqn: standard character on Q_p}), we have:
\begin{align*}
    \psi_\qq(x) &= \prod_{v \in \fanp_\qq} \psi_v(x)\\
    &= e^{- 2 \pi i x} \prod_{p \text{ prime}} e^{2 \pi i a_p/p^{\ord_p(x)}}\\
    &= e^{- 2 \pi i b},
\end{align*}
which we deduce is equal to 1 since $b \in \zz$. Thus $\psi$ is trivial on $\qq$, as desired.

When $K$ is a function field, we recall from (\ref{eqn: standard character on F_p((t))}) that the standard character on each $K_v$ is given by some choice of $\omega_v \in \hat{\Omega}^1_{K_v/\kappa_v}$, but how to make such a choice on every place? We use the \textit{global} meromorphic 1-forms discussed in the paragraph following Definition \ref{def: standard character}.
Fixing some global nonzero $\omega \in \Omega^1_{K/\ff_q}$, for any $v \in \fanp_K$, if $\kappa_v$ denotes the residue field at $v$ we have the residue map
\begin{equation*}
    \res_v : K_v \tensor_K \Omega^1_{K/\ff_q} (= \hat{\Omega}^1_{K_v/\ff_q} )  \to \kappa_v.
\end{equation*}
We may then define
\begin{equation*}
    \psi_v(x_v) := \exp \brack{\frac{2 \pi i}{p} \tr_{\kappa_v/\ff_p} (\res_v(x_v \omega)) }
\end{equation*}
To see that this is well-defined we note at each $v$ this has conductor precisely $\pp_v^{-d_v}$, where $d_v$ is the order of vanishing of $\omega$ at $v$. More precisely, it is the largest integer so that $\omega \in \pp_v^{d_v} \Omega^1_{X, v}$. But of course $\omega$ is regular and nonvanishing at cofinitely many points, so $\psi_v$ has trivial conductor at all such places.

For triviality on $K$ we make use of the classical result known as \textit{residue formula} (see Sections II-12 and II-13 in \cite{serre2012algebraic}), which says that the sum of the trace (to $\ff_q$) of the residues taken over all places of $K$ for any $\omega \in \Omega^1_{K/\ff_q}$ is well defined and equal to 0. The key point here is that $x \omega$ is yet another meromorphic 1-form for any $x \in K$. We thus get:
\begin{align*}
    \psi_K(x) = \prod_{v \in \fanp_K} \psi_v(x) &= \prod_v \exp \brack{\frac{2 \pi i}{p} \tr_{\kappa_v/\ff_p} (\res_v(x \omega)) }\\ 
    &= \exp \brack{\frac{2 \pi i}{p} \tr_{\ff_q/\ff_p} \brack{\sum  \tr_{\kappa_v/\ff_q} (\res_v(x \omega))} }\\
    &= e^0 = 1,
\end{align*}
as desired.
\end{proof}




\begin{corollary}[Adeles are self-dual]
    \label{cor: self-duality of A_K}
    Let $K$ be a global field, and $\psi$ a standard non-trivial character of $\aa_K$, with $\psi_v$ being nontrivial for all $K_v$ and having conductor $\calo_v$ for cofinitely many $v$. Then the map
    \begin{align*}
    \Psi: \aa_K &\to \hat{\aa_K}\\
    a & \mapsto \brack{\psi_a: x \mapsto \psi(ax)},
    \end{align*}
    is an isomorphism of topological groups.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The proof of Proposition \ref{prop: exists non-trivial adelic character} constructed such a character $\psi$ on $\aa_K$. By nontriviality of $\psi_v$, we know that $\Psi_v: K_v \bij \hat{K_v}$ is an isomorphism by Theorem \ref{thm: self-duality of local fields}. Moreover, since by construction the local character $\psi_v$ has conductor $\calo_v$ at cofinitely many places, we have an induced identification of $\calo_v$ with $\hat{K_v/\calo_v}$ at all such places. Then Corollary \ref{cor: adeles and ideles dual commutes w resprod} tells us the dual of the restricted product is the restricted product of the duals of each component, giving us our desired homeomorphism.
\end{proof}




\begin{proposition}
    \label{prop: K = dual of A_K/K}
    The map
    \begin{align*}
        \Psi': K & \to \hat{\aa_K/K}\\
        a & \mapsto (\psi_a: x \mapsto \psi(ax)) 
    \end{align*}
    is an isomorphism of discrete topological groups. In particular, if we fix some standard $\psi$ on $\aa_K$ with the properties stated in Corollary \ref{cor: self-duality of A_K}, then $\psi_a$ is standard on $K$ if and only if $a \in K^\times \subseteq \aa_K$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
As we will see later in Theorem \ref{thm: K discrete and cocompact in A_K}, $K \subseteq \aa_K$ is a discrete closed subgroup and the quotient $\aa_K/K$ is compact. By a standard result of Pontryagin duality, the dual $\hat{\aa_K/K}$ must be discrete, and in particular homeomorphically embed as a discrete closed subgroup of $\hat{\aa_K}$. By Corollary \ref{cor: self-duality of A_K} we've seen that $\hat{\aa_K} \cong \aa_K$, so we have that $\hat{\aa_K/K}/K$ is a closed, discrete $K$-subvector space of the compact $\aa_K/K$, from which we deduce the cardinality of $\hat{\aa_K/K}/K$ must be finite. However, any global field has infinite cardinality, so we must have $\hat{\aa_K/K} = K$, completing our proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
There is a more canonical way to see this duality when $K$ is of positive characteristic, though it requires some more algebraic geometry to formulate and appreciate appropriately. In the language of \S \ref{subsection: Adelic Fourier Theory}, we may identify $K$ with the the function field of a smooth projective curve $X$. For any divisor $D$ on $X$, one can define a line bundle $\call(D)$, whose nonzero sections over any open $U \subseteq X$ consist of all the functions $f \in K^\times$ satisfying $\smalldiv_U(f) \geq -D \cap U$. Then there is a \textit{canonical} isomorphism
\begin{equation*}
    H^1(X, \call(D)) \bij \aa_K/(\aa_K(D) + K),
\end{equation*}
where $\aa_K(D)$ is all the adeles $(x_v)_v$ such that $\ord_v(x_v) \geq - \ord_v(D)$ (by convention $\ord_v(0) = \infty$). The dual of the right side of the above is (canonically, via Serre duality) isomorphic to $H^0(X, \Omega^1_X \tensor \call(D)^*) = \Omega^1_X(D)$ (i.e global 1-forms with poles at worst $D$). Taking a directed union over all $D$ gives
\begin{equation*}
   \Omega^1_{K/\ff_q} \bij \hat{\aa_K/K}. 
\end{equation*}
The left side of the above is clearly 1-dimensional over $K$, which gives us our desired result. Chapter II of \cite{serre2012algebraic} gives a more detailed (albeit with some outdated notation) account of this.
\end{remark}

The above results motivate the following definition.


\begin{defn}
    \label{def: self-dual measure}
For an LCA group equipped with self-duality, a Haar measure $\dx$ is \textbf{self-dual} if it is equal to its own dual measure (i.e the measure with respect to which the Fourier inversion formula holds).
\end{defn}

Our goal next will be to find an explicit measure $\d{x_v}$ on $K_v$ that is self-dual (with respect to the isomorphism $K_v \bij \hat{K_v}$ induced by some \textit{choice} of non-trivial $\psi_v \in \hat{K_v}$) for each $v \in \fanp_K$. We begin by fleshing out the theory of measures on the general restricted product; specifically, we want a Haar measure that is a "restricted product" of Haar measures on each of the components. This will require a bit of care, since the restricted product is not actually a product space.

\begin{prop}
\label{prop: product measure on resprod}
For all but finitely many $v$, let $\d{g_v}$ be a (left) Haar measure on $G_v$ normalized so that $\vol(H_v, \d{g_v}) = 1$. Then there is a unique Haar measure $\dg$ on $G$ such that the restriction $\d{g_S}$ of $\dg$ to any $G_S$ is exactly the product measure for all finite $S \supseteq J_\infty$.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
Choose such a set $S$ satisfying the conditions of finiteness and containing $J_\infty$, and define $\d{g_S}$ as the product of the measures $\d{g_v}$. Note the measure of the compact group $\prod_{v \notin S} H_v$ is indeed finite, by how we've normalized things. It is a verification of axioms to deduce the rest that $\d{g_S}$ is indeed a Haar measure on $G_S$.\\
Suppose we have another finite set of indices $T \supseteq S$. Then, by construction,
it follows that $\d{g_S}$ coincides with the restriction of $\d{g_T}$ to the open subgroup $G_S \subseteq G_T$.

Since $G$ is locally compact, we know it has a (unique up to scaling!) Haar measure, which restructs to a Haar measure on any (clopen!) $G_S$ . Fixing any $S$, we can scale our Haar measure on $G$ so that when it restricts to $G_S$ it exactly coincides with $\d{g_S}$. Clearly this construction was independent of choice of $S$.
\end{proof}

The above result motivates the following.

\begin{defn}
\label{def: resprod measure}
Given $G$ the restricted product of LCA groups $G_v$, we define the \textbf{restricted product measure}
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: measure on restricted product}
        \dg = \rprod_v \d{g_v}
\end{equation}
as the unique Haar measure induced by Proposition \ref{prop: product measure on resprod}.
\end{defn}
Thus to construct a (self-dual) Haar measure on $\aa_K$ it suffices to define a (self-dual) Haar measure on each local component $K_v$ that gives measure 1 to $\calo_v$ for almost all $v$.\\
Since a Haar measure is unique up to scaling, it suffices to specify the finite (positive) volume of any pre-compact set with nonempty interior to determine the measure.
\begin{itemize}
    \item For $F = \rr$, let $\dx$ be the standard Lebesgue measure.
    \item For $F = \cc$, let $\dx$ be twice the standard Lebesgue measure, i.e. $\abs{\dz \wedge \d{\bar{z}}}$.
    \item For $F$ non-Archimedean, we set $\vol(\calo_F, \dx) = (N \frakD_F)^{-1/2}$, where we define $\frakD_F$ to be the fractional ideal inverse to the conductor of the standard characters defined in Definition \ref{def: standard character}. When $F$ has mixed characteristic (i.e arises as a completion of a finite separable extension of $\qq_p$) this is, as expected, the classical different of $F/\qq_p$. In either case, the volume is equal to $q^{-d/2}$, where $q$ is the order of the residue field and $d = \ord_F(\frakD_F)$.
\end{itemize}

\begin{prop}
    \label{prop: self-dual local measures}
The measures defined above are self-dual with respect to the standard characters defined in \ref{def: standard character}.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
The results for when $F = \rr$ or $\cc$ are standard in the theory of classical Fourier analysis, so we omit them here. When $F$ is non-Archimedean, recall that the Fourier inversion theorem tells us that it suffices to check for any nonzero Schwartz-Bruhat function. To that end, let $f$ be the characteristic function of $\calo_F$. We thus get
\begin{equation*}
    \hat{f}(y) = \int_F f(x) \bar{\psi(xy)} \dx = \int_{\calo_F} \bar{\psi(xy)} \dx.
\end{equation*}
We are (slightly) abusing notation here since $\hat{f}$ should be a function on $\hat{F}$, but Theorem \ref{thm: self-duality of local fields} allows us to do this with no worries.\\
We note that the different is the the inverse of the largest fractional ideal of $F$ on which $\psi$ is trivial; this is by construction in characteristic 0 and by definition in positive characteristic. Thus, for all $x \in \calo_F$, we have $\psi(xy) = 1$ if and only if $y \in \inv{\frakD_F}$. This gives us
\begin{equation*}
    \hat{f}(y) = \begin{cases}
    \vol(\calo_F, \dx) = (N\frakD_F)^{-1/2}, & y \in \inv{\frakD}_F\\
    0, & \text{ otherwise},
    \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
where the second equality follows from the fact that we are integrating a nontrivial character over a compact group. Taking the Fourier transform again we get:
\begin{equation*}
    \doublehat{f}(x) = \int_F \hat{f}(y) \bar{\psi(xy)} \dy = \int_{\inv{\frakD_F}} (N\frakD_F)^{-1/2} \bar{\psi(xy)} \dy,
\end{equation*}
where again we slightly abuse notation here using the isomorphism of $F$ with its dual. By similar logic to the previous step, for any $x \in F$, we note that $\psi(xy)$ restricted to the codifferent is trivial if and only if $x \in \calo_F$. Thus, we have:
\begin{equation*}
    \doublehat{f}(x) = \begin{cases}
    (N\frakD_F)^{-1/2} \vol(\inv{\frakD_F}, \dx), & x \in \calo_F\\
    0, & \text{otherwise}.
    \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Finally, we see that
\begin{equation*}
    \vol(\inv{\frakD_F}, \dx) = N\frakD_F \vol(\calo_F, \dx) = (N \frakD_F)^{1/2},
\end{equation*}
and thus \ $\doublehat{f}$ is the characteristic function of $\calo_F$. Thus \ $\doublehat{f} = f$, and in particular \ $\doublehat{f}(x) = f(-x)$ since $\calo_F$ is an additively closed subgroup, so the relevant Fourier inversion formula holds, proving self-duality of the measure.
\end{proof}

We are now ready to define the standard measure on the adeles.
\begin{defn}
\label{def: Tamagawa measure}
For every place $v \in \fanp_K$, let $\d{x_v}$ denote the self-dual Haar measure of $K_v$ with respect to the standard characters defined in \ref{def: standard character}. Then the \textbf{Tamagawa measure} on $\aa_K$ is given by 
\begin{equation*}
    \dx := \rprod_v \d{x_v}.
\end{equation*}
\end{defn}

\begin{sanitycheck}
    It's good to verify that the above is actually well defined. By Proposition \ref{prop: product measure on resprod}, it suffices to show that the self-dual Haar measures satisfy $\vol(\calo_v, \d{x_v}) = 1$ for almost all finite places $v$. This is true by the fact the different is trivial (i.e equal to $\calo_v$) at all the unramified -- hence cofinitely many -- places.
\end{sanitycheck}

If we impose the counting measure on $K$, that combined with the Tamagawa measure on $\aa_K$ induce a unqique Haar measure on the quotient $\aa_K/K$ compatible with the exact sequence of topological groups
\begin{equation*}
    0 \to K \to \aa_K \to \aa_K/K \to 0.
\end{equation*}



\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm: volume of A_K/K = 1}
The volume of $\aa_K/K$ (with respect to the uniquely defined measure discussed above) is 1. 
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
One can perform an explicit computation with fundamental domains to compute this, but this can actually be deduced in a much cleaner, computation-free fashion as a consequence of the adelic Riemann-Roch theorem. Thus, we mention this result here for narrative flow but will deduce it in Corollary \ref{cor: volume of A_K/K is 1}. In particular, it implies the Tamagawa measure is canonical even in the function field case! 
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    The above theorem is the starting point of some rather deep theory of a conjecture of Weil. Handwaving a lot of details, for any global field $K$, if we normalize measures as done above so the quotient $\aa_K/K$ has volume 1, then the volume of the certain Haar measure of $  G(K)\backslash G(\aa_K)$ is 1, for any simpy connected (as an algebraic group, NOT a topological space!) semisimple algebraic group $G$. This conjecture was proven in the number field case in the 1980s by Langlands, Lai and Kottwitz, and in the global function field case by Gaitsgory and Lurie in 2011.
\end{remark}


\subsection{Adelic Approximations and Quotients} 
\label{subsection: Adelic Approximations and Quotients}

In this subsection we outline various compactness and discreteness results of subsets and quotients of the adeles and ideles that will become invaluable to us when performing adelic analysis in Chapter \ref{chapter: Tate's thesis}.

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm: K discrete and cocompact in A_K}
Any global field $K$ is a discrete, closed, cocompact subgroup of its adele ring, i.e. $\aa_K/K$ is compact.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $K_0 = \qq$ in characteristic 0 or $\ff_p(t)$ for some \textit{choice} of separating transcendence bases $\set{t}$ in positive characterstic, and let $n := [K: K_0]$. Then by \ref{prop: adelic base change}, we have the following commutative diagram:
\begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
        \prod^n_{j=1}\aa_{K_0} \arrow[r] & \aa_K\\
        \prod^n_{j=1} K_0 \arrow[r] \arrow[u, hookrightarrow] & K \arrow[u, hookrightarrow]
    \end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
where the horizontal maps are topological isomorphisms. Thus $\aa_K/K$ is compact if and only if $(\aa_{K_0}/K_0)^n$ is compact, which is true if and only if $\aa_{K_0}/K_0$ is compact.

Thus it suffices to consider $K=K_0$, in which case we have exactly one infinite place (which in the case of positive characteristic corresponds to the "point at infinity" on $\bp^1_{\ff_p}$). Define the closed neighborhood $C$ of 0 in $\aa_K$ by 
\begin{equation*}
    C := \set{x \in \aa_K : \abs{x_\infty}_\infty \leq \frac{1}{2} \text{ and } \abs{x_v}_v \leq 1, \ \forall v \neq \infty}
\end{equation*}
Note the condition on the finite places $v$ simply amounts to saying that $x_v \in \calo_v$. If we show that $C \cap K = \set{0}$ and $\aa_K = C+K$, we will be done, since $C$ is visibly compact and isomorphic to $\aa_K/K$.

If $x \in K \cap C$, then we must have $x \in \calo_K$ (where in positive characteristic we may define $\calo_K := \ff_p[t]$, with the same choice of $t$ as discussed before), since $x \in \calo_v$ for every $v \nmid \infty$. If $K = \qq$ then this condition ($x \in \zz$) combined with the condition that $\abs{x_\infty}_\infty \leq 1/2$ forces $x = 0$. If $K = \ff_p(t)$, then $\abs{x}_\infty  = p^{-\ord_{1/t}(x)}$, which is $\geq 1$ for anything nonzero in $\calo_K = \ff_p[t]$. Thus $C \cap K = \set{0}$.

It remains to show, with $K = K_0$, that any $x = (x_v)_v \in \aa_K$ can be written as $y+c$, for $y$ a principal adele and $c \in C$. At each finite place $v$ we have the isomorphism
\begin{equation*}
    K_v /\calo_v \bij K/(\calo_{K, \pp_v}),
\end{equation*}
with $\calo_K$ a PID. Thus, for each finite $v$ we may pick a uniformizer $\pi_v$ for $\calo_v$ such that $\pi_v \in \calo_K$ and is a unit at all other finite places. Using this, at all the (finitely many!) places where $x_v \notin \calo_v$ we may subtract some $\calo_K$-unit multiplied by sufficient power of $\pi_v$ (i.e some element of $K$) from $x_v$ so that the difference lies in $\calo_v$. Moreover by construction this does not affect the valuation of any other element at any other place. Since there are only finitely many such subtractions to make, we can subtract a well-defined element $y_v \in K$ from $x = (x_v)_v$ so that $x_v \in \calo_v$ at all $v < \infty$. At the infinite place we may simply pick some $y_\infty \in \zz$ so that $\abs{x_\infty - y_\infty}_\infty \leq 1/2$ (i.e just pick the nearest integer) or $y_\infty \in \ff_p[t]$ so that $ \abs{x_\infty - y_\infty}_\infty = \inv{p}$ (i.e just pick a polynomial equal to the "polar part" of $x_\infty \in \ff_p[1/t]$). By construction then, if we let $S$ denote the finite set of places where $x_v \notin \calo_v$, we have:

\begin{align*}
y := y_\infty + \sum_{v \in S}y_v \in K, \quad x - y \in C,
\end{align*}
as desired.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    \label{cor: K^times discrete in I_K}
    The image of the diagonal embedding of $K^\times$ is discrete in $\ii_K$. 
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Via the map $K^\times \inj \ii_K \subseteq \aa_K \times \aa_K$ given by $u \mapsto (u, \inv{u})$, (recalling the ideles are topologically embedded in this way) restricting to the first coordinate immediately gives us our result. 
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The discreteness of the image of the embedding is a more delicate fact than one might think on first inspection, in the sense that if we omit \textit{any} place, the image of the diagonal embedding is also dense! We will see this when discussing strong approximation (Corollary \ref{cor: strong approximation}) later on.
\end{remark}


\begin{example}[Volume of Quotient in non-Tamagawa Measure]
    \label{example: measure of A_K/K in number field case}
    For various purposes one may not always want to use the Tamagawa measure on $\aa_K$. Here we delve into an example of the types of explicit computations that can arise from using these alternate measures.

    Let $K$ be a number field, and let $\dx$ be the measure induced on $\aa_K$ by setting $\vol(\calo_v, \d{x_v}) = 1$ for all finite places $v$, and otherwise $\d{x_v}$ is the self-dual measure as defined after Definition \ref{def: resprod measure}. Then: 
    \begin{equation*}
        \vol(\aa_K/K, \dx) = \abs{\disc(\calo_K)}^{1/2}.
    \end{equation*}
    \begin{warning}[Abuse of Notation]
        The reader will likely have noticed the measure $\dx$ is not actually a measure of the quotient group. What we technically mean is the Haar measure on the quotient induced -- via "coset Fubini" -- by the counting measure on (the discrete normal subgroup!) $K$.
    \end{warning}
    \begin{proof}[Proof Sketch]
        An examination of the proof of Theorem \ref{thm: K discrete and cocompact in A_K} shows that the set 
        \begin{equation*}
            C := [-1/2, 1/2] \times \prod_{p \text{ prime }} \zz_p 
        \end{equation*}
        is a fundamental domain for the discrete subgroup $\qq \subseteq \aa_\qq$, so $\vol(\aa_\qq/\qq, \dx) = \vol(C, \dx)$. We immediately see that $C$ has measure 1 = $\disc(\zz)$.\\
        To generalize the above construction, for $K/\qq$ a finite extension of degree $n$ we may use the isomorphism 
        \begin{equation*}
            \eta: \rr^n \bij K \tensor_\qq \rr \brack{ \cong \prod_{v \in \fanp_{K, \infty}} K_v} 
        \end{equation*}
        via the  $\qq$-basis $\set{\xi_1, \ldots, \xi_n}$ of $K$ coming from a $\zz$-basis of $\calo_K$. It is not too hard to show via similar methods that
        \begin{equation*}
            C := \eta \brack{[-1/2, 1/2]^n} \times \prod_{v \notin \fanp_{K, \infty}} \calo_v
        \end{equation*}
        is a fundamental set for $K$ in $\aa_K$. If $\d{x_\infty}$ denotes the product measure over the infinite places of $K$, then the proof comes down to showing
        \begin{equation*}
            \vol(\eta \brack{[0, 1]^n}, \d{x_\infty}) = \abs{\disc(\calo_K)}^{1/2}.
        \end{equation*}
        The key trick is to consider the maps
        \begin{equation*}
            \sigma_h : K \tensor_\qq \rr \to \cc, \ h \in [1, n]
        \end{equation*}
        that arise as ($\rr$-linear) extensions of the canonical $n$ distinct embeddings $K \inj \cc$. The problem then reduces to computing the pushforward measure $\prod_{h \in [n]} \d{\sigma_h(\theta(u))}$, where here $\theta$ denotes the inverse of $\eta$. One can check this measure to be $\abs{ \det(N)} \prod_{h \in [n]} \d{x_h}$, where $N$ is the matrix given by $\sqbrack{(\sigma_h(\xi_i))_i}$, i.e rows are given by the images of $u \in \rr^n$ under composition by $\eta$ followed by $\sigma_h$. One can also readily check the determinant of this matrix is the square of the discriminant of $K$. We refer the reader to Chapter 5 Section 4 of \cite{weil1974basic} for a more detailed exposition.
    \end{proof}
\end{example}

 Since we have discrete embeddings $K \inj \aa_K$ and $K^\times \inj \ii_K$, one might also hope for a similar cocompactness analogue to Theorem \ref{thm: K discrete and cocompact in A_K}; i.e that $K^\times$ is also cocompact in $\ii_K$. However, this is not the case. To analyze this phenomenon, we first standardize the absolute value functions we're using.
\begin{defn}
    \label{def: normalized absolute values}
For $K$ a global field, the \textbf{normalized absolute value} on its completion $K_v$ at a place $v$ is defined as follows:
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
    \item For $K_v = \rr$ we set $\abs{\cdot}_v$ to be the usual absolute value function.
    \item For $K_v = \cc$ we set $\abs{z}_v := z \bar{z}$, which is the square of the usual absolute value function on $\cc$.
    \item For $K_v$ non-Archimedean with uniformizer $\pi$ and residue field of order $q$, we set $\abs{\pi}_v := \inv{q}$, which extends uniquely to $K_v$. 
\end{enumerate}
\end{defn}

At first glance these normalized absolute values may seem arbitary and even a bit odd - why set it to be the square of the standard absolute value on $\cc$, for example? The answer is these absolute values are precisely the ones for which $\abs{x}_v$ is the modulus of the automorphism $y \mapsto xy$. That is to say,
\begin{equation*}
    \abs{x}_v = \frac{\mu(x\calo_v)}{\mu(\calo_v)},
\end{equation*}
for $\mu$ a Haar measure on $K_v$. Note we may replace $\calo_v$ in the above expression with any measurable set of finite positive measure. We leave the verification of these facts to the reader; they are not hard to check.

\begin{defn}
    \label{def: idelic absolute value}
    Let $K$ be a global field, and let $\abs{\cdot}_v$ denote the normalized absolute value on the completion $K_v$. Then the \textbf{idelic norm} (sometimes called the \textbf{idelic absolute value}) is defined by:
    \begin{equation*}
        \begin{split}
            \abs{\cdot}_{\ii_K} : \ii_K &\to \rr_{> 0} \\
            x = (x_v)_v &\mapsto \prod_v \abs{x_v}_v
        \end{split}
    \end{equation*}
\end{defn}
\begin{sanitycheck}
