    Note that $\abs{\cdot}_{\ii_K}$ does indeed converge, since by definition any $x = (x_v) \in \ii_K$ is integral ( i.e has local  norm $1$) for cofinitely many $v$. Moreover, this norm is visibly continuous, as a "product" of continuous norms.
\end{sanitycheck}

\begin{observation}
    The idelic norm of $x$ is, as one might expect, the modulus of the automorphism that is multiplication by $x$. We leave this fact to be checked by the reader.
\end{observation}

\begin{theorem}[Product Formula]
\label{thm: Adele product formula}
    Let $K$ be a global field. Then for every $x \in K^\times$ we have $\abs{x}_{\ii_K} = 1$
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $E/K$ be finite separable. Then 
\begin{align*}
    \begin{split}
        \abs{x}_{\ii_E} &= \prod_{u \in \fanp_K} \ \prod_{v \in \fanp_E, \  v \mid u} \abs{x}_v\\
        &= \prod_{u \in \fanp_K} \ \prod_{v \in \fanp_E, \ v \mid u} \abs{N_{E_v/K_u}(x)}_u\\
        &= \prod_{u \in \fanp_K} \abs{N_{E/K}(x)}_u,
    \end{split}
\end{align*}
and since the norm carries $E^\times$ into $K^\times$ showing the result for $K$ proves it for $E$. In particular, it suffices to prove this for $\qq$ or $\ff_q(t)$. Furthermore, mutiplicativity of the idelic norm allows us to verify the result for just the irreducibles of $\calo_K$.
\begin{itemize}
    \item For $K = \qq$, since $\calo_\qq = \zz$ we can check it for positive primes $p$ and $\zz^\times = \set{\pm 1}$. By definition $\abs{p}_q = 1$ for all primes $q \neq p$. Thus we only have to multiply the $p$-adic and infinite place, giving us:
    \begin{equation*}
        \abs{p}_{\ii_K} = \abs{p}_\infty \cdot \abs{p}_p = p \cdot \frac{1}{p} = 1.
    \end{equation*}
    The case of $\set{\pm 1}$ is clear.
    \item For $K = \ff_q(t)$, we only have to consider irreducible polynomials $p(t) \in \ff_q[t]$, and $\ff_q[t]^\times = \ff_q^\times$. Again this has trivial absolute value at all but two places. This gives us:
    \begin{equation*}
        \abs{p(t)}_{\ii_K} = \abs{p(t)}_\infty \cdot \abs{p(t)}_{p(t)} = q^{\deg p} \cdot \inv{\brack{\abs{\ff_q[t]/ \brangle{p(t)}}}} = 1. 
    \end{equation*}
    The case of $\ff_q^\times$.
\end{itemize}
This completes our proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    Some texts and notes (including \cite{ramakrishnan1998fourier} and the "Adele ring" Wikipedia page) refer to \ref{thm: Adele product formula} as the \textit{Artin product formula}. A less trivial result, also due to Artin, is the converse: that any field $K$ for which the "product formula" holds must be a global field. For a more precise statement with proof, we direct the reader to the 1945 paper of Artin and Whaples \cite{artin-whaples}.
\end{remark}



\begin{example}[Decomposition of $\ii_\qq$]
\label{example: decomposition of I_Q}
We claim we can topologically describe the idele group over $\qq$ as follows:
\begin{equation*}
    \ii_\qq = \qq^\times \times \rr_{> 0} \times \hat{\zz}^\times,
\end{equation*}
where $\hat{\zz} := \invlim_{n \in \zz} \zz/n\zz$, with the inverse limit taken via the natural maps induced by divisibility. The notation is unfortunate here; the hat here signifies completion, not Pontryagin dual.\\
To see this, consider the map
\begin{align*}
    \phi: \ii_\qq & \to \qq^\times\\
    x = (x_v)_v & \mapsto \sgn(x_\infty) \prod_{p} \inv{\abs{x_p }_p},
\end{align*}
which has open kernel. Recalling our normalized absolute values defined in \ref{def: normalized absolute values}, it is immediate from its construction that $\phi(x) = x$ for any $x \in \qq^\times \subseteq \ii_\qq$. Thus, $\ii_\qq = \qq^\times \times \ker(\phi)$ topologically. It is also immediate by construction that $\ker(\phi) = \rr_{\geq 0} \times \prod_p \zz_p^\times$, and the identity $\hat{\zz}^\times \cong \prod_p \zz_p^\times$ completes our proof.
\end{example}


\begin{observation}
    The product formula allows us to generalize the above example to see why $\ii_K/K^\times$ is \textit{never} compact. \\
    When $K$ is a number field, consider the idele 
    \begin{equation*}
        x := (t, 1, 1, \ldots ),
    \end{equation*}
    which is 1 everywhere except a single infinite place. Note then that $\abs{x}_{\ii_K} = t$ or $t^2$, depending on whether the place is real or complex. Regardless, this observation tells us that $\ii_K$ continuously surjects onto $\rr_{> 0}$ (a non-compact image!) under the continuous idelic norm map.

    When $K$ has positive characteristic with field of constants $\ff_q$, the residue field at any completion is a finite extension of $\ff_q$, so the norm of any idele must be a power of $q$. The product formula then tells us that the image of the idelic norm must be some nontrivial and hence finite-index subgroup of $q^\zz$, thus noncompact (as an infinite discrete group).
\end{observation}

Example \ref{example: decomposition of I_Q} gave an explicit description of the non-compactness of $\ii_K/K^\times$, but it fortunately also provides a direction in which we may amend this.

\begin{defn}
    \label{def: norm-one ideles and idele class group}
For $K$ a global field and the idelic norm $\abs{\cdot}_{\ii_K}: \ii_K \to \rr_{> 0}$, define the \textbf{ideles of norm 1} to be:
\begin{equation*}
    \ii^1_K := \ker(\abs{\cdot}_{\ii_K})
\end{equation*}
and the \textbf{norm-1 idele class group} to be the quotient:
\begin{equation*}
    C^1_K := \ii_K^1/K^\times
\end{equation*}
\end{defn}


We have the short-exact sequence
\begin{equation*}
    1 \to C^1_K \to C_K \to \abs{\ii_K}_{\ii_K} \to 1
\end{equation*}
which is in fact split-exact in the number field case, by our previous reasoning for non-compactness of $C_K$. We should guess that this $C^1_K$ should be the compact quotient we're looking for. Before getting into that, we make an interesting observation about the norm-1 ideles that will come in useful later.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma: I_K^1 topology equivalence}
The subspace topologies of $\ii_K^1$ taken with respect to $\ii_K$ or $\aa_K$ are the same.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Suppose we give $\ii_K^1$ the idelic subspace topology by default; it then suffices to show that the injection $\phi: \ii_K^1 \inj \aa_K$ is a homeomorphism onto its image. We have seen in Example \ref{example: subspace neq idelic topology} that the subspace adelic topology is coarser than the idelic topology, so $\phi$ is automatically continuous.\\
To show it is an open map onto its image, it suffices to show any basic open set of $\ii_k^1$ contains a subset open in the adelic subspace topology. To this end, take some basic open neighborhood of the multiplicative identity $1 \in \ii_K^1$  in the idelic subspace topology. This has the form:
\begin{equation*}
    U = \brack{ \prod_{v \in S} U_v \times \prod_{v \notin S} \calo_v^\times } \cap \ii_K^1
\end{equation*}
where each $U_v$ is some open ball around $1 \in K_v^\times$, and $S$ is some finite index set containing the infinite places. Now consider the set
\begin{equation*}
    W := \prod_{v \in S} W_v \times \prod_{v \notin S} \calo_v,
\end{equation*}
where the index set $S$ is the same as in the description of $U$, each $W_v \subseteq U_v$, shrunk appropriately so that for every $\gamma \in W$ satisfies the (strict!) inequality
\begin{equation*}
    \prod_{v \in S} \abs{\gamma_v}_v < 2.
\end{equation*} 
We see that $W$ an open neighborhood of 1 in the adelic topology. We claim that $W \cap \ii_K^1 \subseteq U$. To see this, note that, for $\gamma \in W \cap \ii_K^1$, by definition we have:
\begin{equation*}
    \abs{\gamma}_{\ii_K} = 1 = \prod_{v \in S} \abs{\gamma_v}_v \prod_{v \notin S} \abs{\gamma_v}_v < 2 \prod_{v \notin S} \abs{\gamma_v}_v,
\end{equation*}
where the final inequality follows by our construction of $W$. Since for every $v \notin S$, we have $\gamma_v \in \calo_V$, we have $\abs{\gamma_v}_v \leq 1$, which tells us in particular that $1 < 2 \abs{\gamma_v}_v$. This, in turn, implies $\abs{\gamma_v}_v > 1/2$ which then also implies $\gamma_v \in \calo_v^\times$ for all $v \notin S$, and so we have our desired containment in $U$.
\end{proof}

The main result we wish to show is the following.

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm: C^1_K is compact}
The norm-1 idele class-group $C^1_K$ is compact.
\end{theorem}

To prove this result we will need an adelic version of a classic result in the geometry of numbers. In particular, recall the Blichfeldt-Minkowski lemma that any compact, convex, origin-symmetric set of $\rr^n$ with volume greater than $2^n$ must contain at least one nonzero integer lattice point.
Using the (alternative, depending on your experience) characterization of a lattice as a discrete, co-compact subgroup of a finite-dimensional $\rr$-vector space, in light of Theorem \ref{thm: K discrete and cocompact in A_K} we are led to the following analogous statement about the diagonal embedding $K \inj \aa_K$. 

\begin{lemma}[Adelic Blichfeldt-Minkowski]
\label{lemma: adelic blichfeldt-minkowski}
For any $\gamma = (\gamma_v) \in \ii_K$, consider the (compact, convex and origin-symmetric!) subset 
\begin{equation*}
    X_\gamma := \set{x \in \aa_K : \abs{x_v}_v \leq \abs{\gamma_v}_v \ \forall v \in \fanp_K}.
\end{equation*}
Then there exists some constant $C > 0$ such that if $\abs{\gamma}_{\ii_K} > C$ then $X_\gamma \cap K$ meets $K^\times$. 
\end{lemma} 
\begin{proof}
Consider the following compact subset:
\begin{equation*}
    D := \set{(d_v) \in \aa_K : \abs{d_v}_v \leq 1/4, \ v \mid \infty, \ \abs{d_v}_v \leq 1, \ v < \infty }.
\end{equation*}
This closed subset is the product of the valuation rings at all the finite places and the balls of radius 1/4 (resp. 1/2) at all the real (resp. complex) places. Thus, under our normalized measure $\dx$ on $\aa_K$, this has finite and positive measure, since there are only a finite number of infinite places. Moreover, for any $d, d' \in D$ our construction tells us $\abs{d_v' - d_v}_v \leq 1$ for all places $v$.

Let $D_0$ be a fundamental domain for $K$ in $\aa_K$ (e.g we may choose our construction in the proof of Theorem \ref{thm: K discrete and cocompact in A_K}), which must also have finite positive measure by compactness of the quotient. We now define our constant $C := \dx(D_0)/\dx(D)$.\\
Since the idelic absolute value is the module of the multiplication automorphism, we get: 
\begin{equation*}
    \abs{\gamma}_{\ii_K} > C \implies \dx(\gamma D) > \dx(D_0).
\end{equation*}
Thus, the set $\gamma D$ cannot inject into $\aa_K/K$ under the quotient map, since it is covered by overlaps with $K$-cosets of $D_0$. It follows $\gamma D$ must contain two distinct elements $\gamma d_1, \gamma d_2$ that project to the same nonzero value in $\aa_K/K$, i.e such that $d := \gamma d_1 - \gamma d_2 \in K^\times$ (visibly $\gamma d_1 - \gamma d_2 \neq 0$). Thus, we have:
\begin{equation*}
    \abs{d_v}_v \leq \abs{\gamma_v}_{v} \abs{d_{1, v} - d_{2, v}}_v \leq \abs{\gamma_v},
\end{equation*}
and so $d \in X_\gamma$ by definition. Thus, $X_\gamma \cap K^\times$ is non-empty, completing our proof.
\end{proof}

We are now ready to show compactness of $C^1_K$.

\begin{proof}[Proof of \ref{thm: C^1_K is compact}]
    By Lemma \ref{lemma: I_K^1 topology equivalence}, it suffices to find some $W \subseteq \aa_K$, compact in the adelic topology, such that the continuous map
    \begin{equation*}
        W \cap \ii_K^1 \to C_K^1
    \end{equation*}
    is a surjection. To this end, consider a positive real $C > 0$, and pick any $\gamma \in \ii_K$ with $\abs{\gamma}_{\ii_K} > C$, and any $\beta \in \ii_K^1$ and define
    \begin{equation*}
        W_C := \set{x \in \aa_K : \abs{x_v}_v \leq \abs{\inv{\beta_v}\gamma_v}_v \ \forall v \in \fanp_K},
    \end{equation*}
    which is visibly compact in $\aa_K$. By the adelic Blichfeldt-Minkowski lemma (\ref{lemma: adelic blichfeldt-minkowski}), we may pick $C$ large enough so that $W_C$ contains a non-zero principal adele $\alpha \in K^\times \subseteq \ii_K^1$.\\
    We now set
    \begin{equation*}
        W := \set{x \in \aa_K : \abs{x_v}_v \leq \abs{\gamma_v}_v \ \forall v \in \fanp_K},
    \end{equation*}
    which is again visiby compact in $\aa_K$, and note by construction we have $\alpha \beta \in W$. We deduce $\alpha \beta \in W \cap \ii_K^1$, with image $\overline{\beta}$ in $C_K^1$. Since $\beta \in \ii_K^1$ was arbitrary, we deduce the quotient map is indeed surjective, and thus $C_K^1$ is compact.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    The above result is important for two primary reasons. Firstly, it is akin to the finiteness of the class group of a number field, in the sense that it implies "finiteness" of generalized ideal class groups of number fields. Secondly, as we will see in Chapter \ref{chapter: Tate's thesis}, compactness limits the possibilities of Hecke characters that arise when doing Fourier anaylysis following the method of Tate.
\end{remark}


We close with perhaps the most powerful approximation result on the adeles: strong approximation.

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm: Strong approximation of A_K}
    For $K$ a global field, let $w$ be a fixed place and let $S$ be a finite subset of $\fanp_K \setminus \set{w}$. For every $v$ in $S$, select arbitrary $a_v \in K$ and $\epsilon_v \in \rr_{> 0}$. Then there exists some $x \in K$ such that
    \begin{align*}
        \abs{x - a_v}_v \leq \epsilon_v, \ &\forall v \in S\\
        \abs{x}_v \leq 1, \ & \forall v \notin S \cup \set{w},
    \end{align*}
    with no restriction on $\abs{x}_w$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
We may express $K$ as a finite separable of $K_0 = \qq$ or $\ff_p(t)$ (which in the positive characteristic case relies on some choice $\ff_p(t) \inj K$). In the proof of Theorem \ref{thm: K discrete and cocompact in A_K} we constructed a comapct subset $C_0$ containing a fundamental domain for $K_0$ in $\aa_{K_0}$, which we recall here:
\begin{equation*}
    C_0 = \set{x \in \aa_{K_0} : \abs{x_\infty}_\infty \leq \frac{1}{2} \text{ and } \abs{x_v}_v \leq 1, \ \forall v \neq \infty}.
\end{equation*}
Note again here the notion of the infinite place rests on some choice of separating transcendence basis over $\ff_p$ in the positive characteristic case. By adelic base change (Proposition \ref{prop: adelic base change}), given a $K_0$-basis $\set{u_1, \ldots u_n}$ of $K$, we have the isomorphism
\begin{equation*}
    \aa_K = \bigdsum_{i = 1}^n \aa_{K_0}u_i,
\end{equation*}
where the diagonally embedded $K$ on the left side is mapped isomorphically onto diagonally embdedded $K_0$ on the right side. We may then define
\begin{equation*}
    C := \bigdsum^{n}_{i=1} C_0 u_i \subseteq \bigdsum_{i = 1}^n \aa_{K_0}u_i = \aa_K
\end{equation*}
Since we have $C_0 \surj \aa_{K_0}/K_0$, by construction we have $C \surj \aa_K/K$, and thus $C$ must contain a fundamental domain for $K$ in $\aa_K$. Moreover, since $C$ is a compact subset of $\aa_K$, part (b) of Proposition \ref{prop: restricted product properties} tells us that it is contained in some set of the form
\begin{equation*}
    W = \set{x \in \aa_K : \abs{x_v}_v \leq \delta_v},
\end{equation*}
where $\delta_v \in \rr_{> 0}$ is equal to 1 for all but finitely many places.

Now pick some $\gamma \in \ii_K$ satisfying the following conditions:
\begin{enumerate}[1)]
    \item $\abs{\gamma_v}_v \leq \epsilon_v / \delta_v$, for every $v \in S$;
    \item $\abs{\gamma_v}_v = 1$, for every $v \in \fanp_K \setminus (S \cup \set{w})$;
    \item $\abs{\gamma_w}_w > V \prod_{v \in S} \inv{\abs{\gamma_v}_v}$ for the place $w$, where $V$ is the "minimal volume" constant associated to $\aa_K$ by the adelic Blichfeldt-Minkowski lemma (\ref{lemma: adelic blichfeldt-minkowski}). 
\end{enumerate}
 Picking such a $\gamma$ is possible since the conditions imposed satisfy being cofinitely integral everywhere. Lemma \ref{lemma: adelic blichfeldt-minkowski} then tells us that $X_\gamma \cap K$ contains a non-zero principal adele $z$.


Now note, for any $b \in K^\times$, we may write $\aa_K = K + bW$, since multiplication by $b$ is an automorphism of $K$. Thus for an arbitary $x = (x_v)_v \in \aa_K$, we have
\begin{equation*}
    x = a + y,
\end{equation*}
for some $a \in K$ and $y \in zW$, where $z$ is the principal adele in $X_\gamma \cap K$ discussed earlier in this proof. Thus, we get 
\begin{equation*}
    \abs{x_v - a_v}_v = \abs{y_v}_v \leq \delta_v \abs{z_v}_v \leq  \delta_v \abs{\gamma_v}_v,
\end{equation*}
which, by construction, gives us our desired inequality for every place $v \neq w$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Strong Approximation]
    \label{cor: strong approximation}
    For any place $w \in \fanp_K$, the diagonal embedding
    \begin{equation*}
        K \inj \rprod_{v \in \fanp_K \setminus \set{w}} K_v := \aa_K^w
    \end{equation*}
    is dense. The corresponding statement also holds for $K^\times$ and $\ii_K^w := \ii^1_K \cap \aa_K^w$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
This is immediate from the preceding theorem; barring a restriction on the place $w$, we may make the norm of the difference between any given adele and a principal adele arbitrary small in every component. The second statement is a consequence of the fact that $K^\times \subseteq \ii^1_K$ and that $\ii^1_K$ inherits the subspace topology from $\aa_K$ (Lemma \ref{lemma: I_K^1 topology equivalence}).
\end{proof}

Comparing the above result to Theorem \ref{thm: K discrete and cocompact in A_K} gives us a better appreciation for the discreteness of the embedding $K \inj \aa_K$; if we remove even a single place from the restricted product the image is dense!

\begin{example}[Discrete and Dense]
    \label{example: finite adeles and ideles}
Let $K$ be a number field and let $S_\infty$ be the set of Archimedean places. Ignoring them, we get the so-called \textbf{finite adeles}, given by
\begin{equation*}
    \aa_K^{\infty} := \rprod_{v \nmid \infty } K_v \ .
\end{equation*}
A similar construction gives us $\ii_K^{\infty}$, the \textbf{finite ideles}. We remark that the intersection of $K^\times$ with the compact open $\prod_{v \nmid \infty } \calo_v$ is exactly the unit group $\calo_K^\times$, which is infinite except when $K = \qq$ or is imaginary quadratic.
\end{example}


\subsection{The $S$-Groups}
\label{subsection: S-Groups}

Recall that if $S$ denotes a finite set of places of a global field $K$, then the adele ring can be described as the direct limit
\begin{equation*}
    \aa_K = \dlim_{S} \aa_{K, S}.
\end{equation*}
It is useful to have "$S$-analogues" of certain compactness results and volume calculations, as we'll see in the chapter of Tate's thesis. Often (but not always!) we'll be interested in the cases when $S \supseteq S_\infty = \fanp_{K, \infty}$, i.e when $S$ contains all the infinite places of $K$.

\begin{defn}
    \label{def: S-versions}
    Take $S$ any finite set of places of a global field $K$ with $S \supseteq S_\infty$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
    \item For $S$-nonempty, the \textbf{$S$-adeles} of $K$ are defined as:
    \begin{equation*}
        \aa_{K, S} := \prod_{v \in S} K_v \times \prod_{v \notin S} \calo_v
    \end{equation*}
    \item For $S$ nonempty, the \textbf{ring of $S$-integers} is defined as:
    \begin{equation*}
        \calo_{K, S} := K \cap \aa_{K, S}
    \end{equation*}
    i.e the elements of $K$ that are $v$-integral for all $v \notin S$.
    \item The \textbf{$S$-ideles} are defined as:
    \begin{equation*}
        \ii_{K, S} := \prod_{v \in S}K_v^\times \times \prod_{v \notin S}\calo_v^\times 
    \end{equation*}
    By abuse of notation, letting $\calo_v^\times$ denote $\set{\pm 1}$ for $v$ real and $S^1$ for $v$ complex, we may also define the $S$-ideles (for both number fields and function fields) when $S \nsupseteq S_\infty$, and even when $S$ is empty! This notation will come in handy for computations in \S \ref{subsection: dedekind zeta function}.
    \item The \textbf{$S$-ideles of norm 1} defined as:
    \begin{equation*}
        \ii_{K, S}^1 := \ii_K^1 \cap \ii_{K, S}
    \end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
\end{defn}

\begin{sanitycheck}
    The $S$-ideles are algebraically the units of the $S$-adeles, and $\calo_{K, S}^\times = K^\times \cap \ii_{K, S}$.
\end{sanitycheck}

\begin{example}[Idelic form of $\cl(K)$]
    \label{example: idelic form of Cl(K)}
Let $\fanp_\infty$ denote the set of infinite places of a number field $K$. We denote by $\aa_{K, \infty}$ (resp. $\ii_{K, \infty}$) the open subgroup $\aa_{K, \fanp_\infty} \subseteq \aa_K$ (resp. $\ii_{K, \fanp_\infty} \subseteq \ii_K$).

By definition, for any idele $(x_v)_v \in \ii_K$, we must have that $\ord_v(x_v) = 0$ for cofinitely many places. Thus, if we denote by $\Div(K)$ (see \S \ref{subsection: Adelic Fourier Theory} for why we use this notation) the set of all fractional ideals of $K$, we note the map
\begin{align*}
    \fraki: \ii_K &\to \Div(K)\\
    (x_v)_v & \mapsto \prod_{v \text{ finite}} \pp_v^{\ord_v(x_v)}
\end{align*}
is a well defined surjective group homomorphism with kernel $\ii_{K, \infty}$. Moreover, for any principal fractional ideal $\brangle{x} \ideal K$, we note that it arises from the diagonally embedded $x \in \ii_K$. Thus, we get the idelic expression of the ideal class group:
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: idelic form of Cl(K)}
    \cl(K) \cong \ii_K/(K^\times \cdot \ii_{K, \infty}).
\end{equation}

\end{example}

The primary result of this subsecton will be the following theorem, which characterizes the unit group of $\calo_{K, S}$ defined above.

\begin{theorem}[$S$-unit theorem]
    \label{thm: S-unit theorem}
        For $K$ a global field and any finite set of places $S \supseteq S_\infty$, we have an isomorphism
        \begin{equation*}
            \calo_{K, S}^\times \cong \mu(K) \times \zz^{\abs{S} - 1},
        \end{equation*}
        where $\mu(K)$ is the (finite!) group of roots of unity in $K$.
\end{theorem}


\begin{example}[Dirichlet's Unit Theorem]
    \label{example: dirichlet's unit thm}
    When $K$ is a number field with $r_1$ real embeddings and $r_2$ complex embeddings, then setting $S = S_\infty = \fanp_{K, \infty}$,  Theorem \ref{thm: S-unit theorem} tells us that $\calo_K^\times = \calo_{K, S_\infty}^\times$ is finitely generated of rank $r_1 + r_2 - 1$, which is precisely the classical theorem of Dirichlet. Thus we may see \ref{thm: S-unit theorem} as a generalization of this result, of which we will proceed to give a neat proof using the adelic formulation.
\end{example}

To prove the $S$-unit theorem we will need a few intermediary results, the first of which is the following lemma.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma: root of unity if and only if norm one at all places}
For $K$ a global field, $x \in K$ is a root of unity (i.e has finite multiplicative order) if and only if $\abs{x}_v = 1$ for all $v \in \fanp_K$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
One direction is clear, so for the other one suppose $x$ has norm 1 at all places.

When $K$ is a number field, this is a classical result of Kronecker, but we recount a detailed proof here. We note $x$ must be an algebraic integer, all of whose complex embeddings have norm 1. Its minimal polynomial $f(t)$ is integral and has the form = $\prod_i (t - x_i)$ taken over all Galois conjugates of $x$. Consider the coefficient of $t^k$. It is the sum of $\binom{n}{k}$ terms, where $n$ is the degree of $f$ and where each term is a product of $x_i$'s, and thus must have complex norm 1. Thus the coefficient, since it lies in $\zz$, must be bounded in absolute value by $\binom{n}{k}$. Since $x \in \calo_K$, we know $n \leq [K : \qq]$. Moreover, for any $\sigma \in \gal(K/\qq),$ we note that $|\sigma(x^m)| = |\sigma(x)|^m = 1$ for all $m \geq 1$. Thus the minimal polynomials of all such $x^m$ have coefficients bounded by $\binom{[K : \qq]}{\floor{[K : \qq]/2}}$ (i.e the largest binomial coefficient), and their degree is also bounded by $[K: \qq]$. Thus the powers of $x$ are roots of a finite set of polynomials, so the cyclic group generated by $x$ is finite, making it a root of unity

When $K$ has positive characteristic with field of constants $\ff_q$, we can associate it with the function field of some smooth proper curve $X$ geometrically connected over $\ff_q$. The condition of having norm 1 at all places then tells us that $x$ is a rational function on $X$ with no zeroes or poles, hence lies in the field of constants $\ff_q$, and thus has finite order.
\end{proof}

Next, we need a generalization of the compactness of the norm-1 idele class group $C^1_K$ to the $S$-ideles. 

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma: O_K, S is cocompact}
    The quotient $\ii^1_{K, S}/ \calo_{K, S}^\times$ is compact, for any finite (possibly empty!) set $S$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Firstly $\calo_{K, S}^\times$ is visibly a subgroup of $\ii^1_{K, S}$, using the fact that $K^\times$ embeds in $\ii_K$ and the product formula (\ref{thm: Adele product formula}), thus the quotient makes sense. We also see that $\ii_{K, S}$ is by definition an open subgroup of $\ii_K$, and thus $\ii_{K, S}^1$ is open (hence closed!) in $\ii_K^1$ under the subspace topology. Since $C^1_K$ is a compact (Hausdorff) quotient, it follows $\ii^1_{K, S}/ \calo_{K, S}^\times$ is a closed subgroup, and thus also compact.
\end{proof}


We now are ready to prove the main result.

\begin{proof}[Proof of \ref{thm: S-unit theorem}]
    Consider the "adelic circle", defined as the product of all the norm-1 elements of each completion, i.e.
    \begin{equation*}
        C_\aa := \prod_{v \in \fanp_K} C_v^1,
    \end{equation*}
    where $C_v^1 := \set{x_v : \abs{x_v}_v = 1} \subseteq K_v^\times$. Note when $v$ is non-Archimedean then $C_v^1 = \calo_v^\times$. 
    By Tychonoff, this is a compact subgroup of $\ii_{K, S}$ for any $S$. Thus we have the (topologically!) exact sequence:
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eqn: S-unit thm SES1}
        1 \to C_\aa \to \ii_{K, S} \to \prod_{v \in S} (K_v^\times /C_v^1) \to 1.
    \end{equation}
    Note that when $v$ is non-Archimedean then
    \begin{equation*}
        K_v^\times / C_v^1 = K_v^\times / \calo_v^\times \cong \zz,
    \end{equation*}
    and when $v$ is Archimedean we have
    \begin{equation*}
        K_v^\times / C_v^1 \cong \rr_{> 0} \cong \rr. 
    \end{equation*}
    Thus, if we have $n$ non-Archimedean places and $m$ Archimedean places in $S$, (\ref{eqn: S-unit thm SES1}) becomes
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eqn: S-unit thm SES2}
        1 \to C_\aa \to \ii_{K, S} \to \zz^n \times \rr^m \to 1.
    \end{equation}
    Now we analyse how $K^\times \inj \ii_K$ meets the objects in the exact sequence in (\ref{eqn: S-unit thm SES2}). Firstly, we see $K^\times \cap C_\aa$ consists of all elements that have norm 1 at every place, which Lemma \ref{lemma: root of unity if and only if norm one at all places} tells us is precisely $\mu(K)$. For the middle object, we recover exactly $\calo_{K, S}^\times$. For the final object, let $L$ denote the image of $K^\times \cap \ii_{K, S} =: \calo_{K, S}^\times$ under the quotient map. This transforms (\ref{eqn: S-unit thm SES2}) to the following short exact sequence:
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eqn: S-unit thm SES3}
        1 \to \mu(K) \to \calo_{K, S}^\times \to L \to 1.
    \end{equation}
    It remains to show that $L \cong \zz^{n + m - 1}$. Consider the natural continuous map 
    \begin{align*}
        \phi: \prod_{v \in S} (K_v^\times /C_v^1) &\to \abs{\ii_K}_{\ii_K}\\
         (x_v)_{v \in S}  &\mapsto \prod_{v \in S} \abs{x_v}_v.
    \end{align*}
    When $K$ is a number field, this can abstractly be seen as a continuous homomorphism $\zz^n \times \rr^m \to \rr_{> 0}$ that is nontrivial on each copy of $\rr$, whose kernel is thus isomorphic to $\zz^n \times \rr^{m-1}$. Since the image $L$ of $\calo^\times_{K, S}$ lies within this kernel, it is in fact a discrete and cocompact subgroup. Any (nontrivial) subgroup of $\zz$ is isomorphic to $\zz$, and any discrete subgroup of $\rr^{m-1}$ must be finite free abelian, and the cocompactness conditions enforces a rank $m-1$ condition as well. Thus, $L \cong \zz^{n + m - 1}$, as desired.

    When $K$ has positive characteristic, recalling the image of the idelic norm is some finite index subgroup of $q^\zz$ (hence isomorphic to $\zz$), we can view $\phi$ as a surjective map $\zz^n \to \zz$ (recall a function field has no Archimedean places!) whose kernel thus must be isomorphic to $\zz^{n-1}$, as desired.
\end{proof}

The above result motivates the following definition.

\begin{defn}
\label{def: S-class group}
    For $S$ any nonempty finite set of places of a global field $K$ containing the infinite ones, the \textbf{$S$-class group} of $K$ is
    \begin{equation*}
        C_{K, S} := \ii_K/(K^\times \cdot \ii_{K, S})
    \end{equation*}
\end{defn}

\begin{observation}
    We claim the following inclusion:
    \begin{equation*}
        \ii^1_K/(K^\times \cdot \ii^1_{K, S}) \inj  \ii_K/(K^\times \cdot \ii_{K, S}) =: C_{K, S}.
    \end{equation*}
    has finite index, and is in fact an isomorphism in the number field case! When $K$ has characteristic zero, any element of the right side we may always represent it by an idele of norm 1 by adjusting the value in $K_v$ for any infinite place $v$. When $K$ is a function field we may adjust the value in $K_v$ for some $v \in S$ to generate some finite index subgroup of the value group of the idelic norm, and thus is follows we get a finite index image of the above inclusion. Putting these observations, along with previous results, together, we get the following result.
\end{observation}

\begin{prop}[Finiteness of $S$-Class Groups]
\label{prop: C_K, S is finite}
Let $S$ be any nonempty finite set of places of a global field $K$ containing the infinite places. Then $C_{K, S}$ is a finite group.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
    Follows immediately from the above observation and the proof of Theorem \ref{thm: S-unit theorem}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Should we allow $S$ to be empty in the above proposition statement (only possible in positive characteristic), we would get that $C_{K, \emptyset}$ is the direct product of $\zz$ with a finite group, a (useless, but perhaps mildly amusing) fact we leave the reader to check.
\end{remark}

The preceding result will be crucial in volume and residue calculations of $L$-functions over number fields, which will be covered in the subsequent chapter.

\begin{example}[Finiteness of the ideal class group]
\label{example: ideal class group}
When $S = S_\infty$ for $K$ a number field, we see that the quotient in (\ref{eqn: idelic form of Cl(K)}) from Example \ref{example: idelic form of Cl(K)} is precisely
\begin{equation*}
    C_{K, S_\infty} = \cl(K).
\end{equation*}
Proposition \ref{prop: C_K, S is finite} then is an idelic formulation of the fact that the ideal class group of a number field is finite. 
When one unravels the details, the adelic proof of finiteness of the ideal class group is essentially the same as the classical one, only packaged differently. The advantage of the adelic packaging lies in its versatility and in the uniformity of arguments that one can apply to tackle all global fields at once, as we will see throughout this text.
\end{example}






\chapter{The Thesis of Tate (a.k.a, $\GL_1$)}
\label{chapter: Tate's thesis}

\begin{myquote}{In [his thesis], Tate provides an elegant and unified treatment of the analytic continuation of the $L$-functions attached by Hecke to his Gr{\"o}{\ss}encharaktere... The power of the methods of abelian harmonic analysis in the setting of Chevalley's adeles/ideles provided a remarkable advance over the classical techniques used by Hecke.}{Ch. 6 of \textit{An Introduction to the Langlands Program}}{Stephen S. Kudla}

\end{myquote}

One can generalize classical Dirichlet characters to quasi-characters of ray class groups of number fields, and then one can generalize even further to continuous characters of the idele class group $C_K$ for $K$ a global field. It is a famous result of Hecke that $L(s, \chi)$ has analytic continuation and satisfies an appropriate functional equation for any number field $K$ and any idele class character $\chi$. The original formulation of this result involved a complicated computation with theta functions and Mellin transforms. Moreover in the functional equation a so-called \textit{root number} $W(\chi)$ associated to each quasi-character $\chi$ kept popping up, with no good explanation for its value.\\

In his 1950 PhD thesis John Tate made use of Fourier analysis on adele groups to reprove the results of Hecke, first by establishing local functional equations at all the completions of a global field and then using the adelic formulation to give an elegant description of a global functional equation on quasi-characters of the idele group. We do not discuss Hecke's original approach here, but we recommend the interested reader to peruse Chapter VII of \cite{neukirch2010algebraic} for a detailed exposition on that matter in order to gain a better appreciation of the relative elegance of Tate's pakaging.\\

Section \ref{section: Local Theory} covers the necessary local theory, the results of which will be patched together in subsequent sections. Section \ref{section: The Global Theory} sets up the theory of adelic Fourier analysis (with a quick detour on some Riemann-Roch) and proves the global functional equation for the zeta integral. Section \ref{section: Examples and Applications} then discusses some concrete examples and applications of Tate's approach in the theory of $L$-functions and Dedekind zeta functions.\\

The unacquainted reader may also question why the moniker of "$\GL_1$" for this chapter. The answer lies in the deeper theory of adelic automorphic forms, and their associated representation theory.


\newpage
\section{The Local Theory}
\label{section: Local Theory}
 

\subsection{The Local Functional Equations}
\label{subsection: the local func eqn}

In his thesis, Tate worked with some ad-hoc function spaces over local and global fields. We standardize our workings with the so-called \textit{Schwartz-Bruhat functions} on local fields. Over an Archimedean field (i.e $\rr$ or $\cc$), recall a \textbf{Schwartz} function (more commonly known as a \textit{rapidly-decreasing function}) is a smooth $\cc$-valued function $f$ such that
\begin{equation*}
    \lim_{\abs{x} \to \infty} p(x) D^kf(x) = 0
\end{equation*}
for all polynomials $p(x)$ and all $k$-th derivative operators $D^k$ (corresponding to $\d{^k}/\d{x^k}$ for $F = \rr$ and $\partial^{k}/(\partial x^i \partial y^{k-i})$, for all $i \in [0, k]$ for $F = \cc$). These functions classically form a useful space on which to do Fourier analysis, and so we define a similarly useful generalization for our adelic purposes. 
\begin{defn}
\label{def: Schwartz-Bruhat functions}
A \textbf{Schwartz-Bruhat} function on a local field $F$ is a Schwartz function if $F$ is Archimedean and a smooth (i.e locally constant, when $F$ is non-Archimedean), compactly-supported function if $F$ is non-Archimedean. We denote by $\fans(F)$ the complex vector space of Schwartz-Bruhat functions on $F$.
\end{defn}


Before analyzing the global zeta integral of a function on an adele group of a global field, we first understand the induced zeta functions on each of the local components. The goal of this subsection will be to describe a functional equation of zeta functions on a local field by introducing so-called \textit{local $L$-factors} associated to a character $\chi$ (and its dual $\chi^\lor$ under the Fourier transform) of a local field. In particular, these local $L$-factors will be realized as the gcd of some local zeta functions. We start with a slew of notations and definitions.\\

When $F$ is non-Archimedean, let $\calo_F$ denote its ring of integers, $\pp$ its maximal ideal with uniformizer $\pi$ and residue field $\ff_q$. For any local field, we may decompose its multiplicative group of units as
\begin{equation*}
    F^\times \cong \calo_F^\times \times \cals_F
\end{equation*}
where $\calo_F^\times$ is the group of norm-1 elements of $F$ and $\cals_F := \abs{F^\times}$, i.e. the image of $F^\times$ in the valuation map, up to choice of uniformizer $\pi$ and homomorphic retraction $F^\times \to \calo_F^\times$. In the non-Archimedean case the group $\calo_F^\times$ is the literal group of units of the valuation ring, and in the Archimedean case we are abusing notation a little here. If $F = \rr$ then $\calo_F^\times := \set{\pm 1}$ and if $F = \cc$ then $\calo_F^\times := S^1$. This decomposition allows us to give a nice characterization of the multiplicative quasi-characters of a local field.

\begin{proposition}
    \label{prop: quasi-char of local field}
    For $F$ a local field, any quasi-character of $F^\times$ is of the form
    \begin{equation*}
        \chi: x \mapsto \mu(\tilde{x}) \abs{x}_F^s,
    \end{equation*}
    where $\mu$ is a character (i.e $S^1$-valued homomorphism) of $\calo_F^\times$, $\tilde{x}$ denotes the image of $x$ under the (choice of) homomorphic retraction $F^\times \to \calo_F^\times$, and $s \in \cc$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let $\mu$ be the restriction of $\chi$ to $\calo_F^\times$. Since the subgroup $\calo_F^\times$ is compact and $\cc^\times$ has no "small subgroups", it follows the image of $\mu$ must lie in $S^1$, and hence it must be a character. We note that the map
\begin{equation*}
    x \mapsto \chi(x)/\mu(\tilde{x})
\end{equation*}
is by construction trivial on $\calo_F^\times$, hence it must factor through the map
\begin{equation*}
    \abs{\cdot}_F : F^\times \surj \cals_F.
\end{equation*}
But the value group $\cals_F$ is either isomorphic to $\rr_{> 0}$ (if $F$ is Archimedean) or to $\zz$ (if $F$ is non-Archimedean), and the only quasi-characters on these groups are of the form $t \mapsto t^s$. Thus $\chi$ must decompose as described above.
\end{proof}


\begin{defn}
\label{def: exponent and unramified character}
    For any quasi-character  $\chi = \mu \abs{\cdot}^s$ of  $(F^\times)$, we call $\re{s}$ the \textbf{exponent} of $\chi$. Furthermore, we say $\chi$ is \textbf{unramified} if $\chi(\calo_F^\times) = 1$ (i.e if $\mu = 1$). We denote by $\fanx(F^\times)$ the total space of quasi-characters on $F^\times$.
\end{defn}
\begin{warning}
    Recall that for any $q \in \rr_{> 0}$ we have the formula:
    \begin{equation*}
        q^i = \cos(\log(q)) + i \sin(\log(q)).
    \end{equation*}
    In particular, in the non-Archimedean case the value of $s$ is \textit{not} uniquely determined (e.g $s + (2 \pi i N)/\log(q)$ give the same $\chi$ for all $N \in \zz$), which is why we define the exponent to be the real part, which \textit{is} uniquely determined. Thus we may abuse notation to write $\re{\chi}$ without confusion.
\end{warning}

We may consider the equivalence relation on $\fanx(F^\times)$ given by declaring two quasi-characters to be equivalent if their quotient is unramified. By our observations above, we see that any such equivalent class consists of quasi-characters of the form,
\begin{equation*}
    \chi(x) = \chi_0(x) \abs{x}_F^s,
\end{equation*}
where $\chi_0$ is a representative of the equivalence class, and $s \in \cc$ is variable. This then allows us to give each equivalence class the structure of a Riemann surface, since the variable $s$ determines the character. By our observation above, each $s \in \cc$ determines a distinct character when $F$ is Archimedean, and in the non-Archimedean case the character is only determined modulo $2 \pi i/\log(q)$. In particular, each equivalence class is isomorphic to $\cc$ if $F$ is Archimedean and to the infinite cylinder $\cc/(2 \pi i \zz/\log(q))$ otherwise. Thus our total space $\fanx(F^\times)$ is disjoint union of (at most countably many) Riemann surfaces, so for a function with such quasi-characters as an input we may reasonably discuss notions such as holomorphicity, convergence and analytic continuation, with the caveat that such details will have to be worked out for each connected component. 

\begin{remark}[Terminology]
The reasoning behind the term "unramified" character is as follows: if $F$ is a non-Archimedean local field with maximal abelian extension $F^{\ab}$, then any character $\chi$ of $\gal(F^{\ab}/F)$ factors through the Galois group of some finite abelian extension $L/F$. Naturally, the inertia subgroup $I_{F^{\ab}/F}$ of $\gal(F^{\ab}/F)$ maps onto the inertia subgroup of $\gal(L/F)$, which is trivial if and only if $L/F$ is an unramified field extension. Thus we may reasonably call $\chi$ unramified if $\chi \vert_{I_{F^{\ab}/F}} = 1$.

If $\theta: F^\times \inj \gal(F^{\ab}/F)$ denotes the local Artin homomorphism, then it is known that $\calo_F^\times$ maps isomorphically onto $I_{F^{\ab}/F}$. If we denote
\begin{equation*}
    \eta := \chi \circ \theta: F^\times \to \cc^\times,
\end{equation*}
it follows $\eta \vert_{\calo_F^\times} = 1$ if and only if $L/F$ is unramified. This is exposited in more detail in Chapter 7 of \cite{ChildressCFT}.
\end{remark}


We now state certain \textit{local} $L$-functions that will show up in the functional equation involving the local zeta integral, which will be described later.

\begin{enumerate}[(1)]
    \item In the non-Archimedean case, we set:
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eqn: non-Arch local L-factor}
        L(\chi) := \begin{cases}
            \inv{\brack{1 - \chi(\pi_F)}}, & \text{ if } \chi \text{ is unramified,}\\
            1, & \text{otherwise}.
        \end{cases}
    \end{equation}
    \item In the Archimedean case we recall by Gelfand-Mazur that $F = \cc$ or $\rr$. 
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item If $F = \cc$, note any $\chi$ is of the form:
        \begin{equation*}
            \chi_{s, n}: z \mapsto \abs{z}^s \brack{\frac{z}{\abs{z}}}^n
        \end{equation*}
         for $s \in \cc$ and $n \in \zz$ uniquely defined. Here we use the classical fact that the Pontryagin dual of $S^1$ is $\zz$. Also note by $\abs{z}$ here we mean the usual absolute value on $\cc$ ( i.e. not our normalized one!). We then set:
         \begin{equation}
        \label{eqn: complex local L-factor}
            L(\chi_{s, n}) := \Gamma_\cc(s + \frac{\abs{n}}{2}) := \brack{2 \pi}^{-\brack{s + \frac{\abs{n}}{2}}}\Gamma(s + \frac{\abs{n}}{2}).
         \end{equation} 
         \item If $F = \rr$ then $U_F := \set{\pm 1}$. Here any quasi-character is of the form $\chi = \mu \abs{\cdot}^s$, where $\mu$ and $s$ are uniquely defined,with $\mu$ either trivial or the sign homomorphism $\sgn: x \mapsto x/\abs{x}$. We then set:
         \begin{equation}
        \label{eqn: real local L-factor}
             L(\chi) := \begin{cases}
                 \Gamma_\rr(s) := \pi^{-s/2}\Gamma(s/2), & \mu = 1,\\
                 \Gamma_\rr(s+1), & \mu = \sgn.
             \end{cases}
         \end{equation}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}


\begin{defn}
\label{def: shifted dual and L(s, chi) notation}
    The functions $\chi \mapsto L(\chi)$ described in (\ref{eqn: real local L-factor}), (\ref{eqn: complex local L-factor}) and (\ref{eqn: non-Arch local L-factor}) are the \textbf{local $L$-factors} of their corresponding local fields. We also introduce the following convenient notation:
    \begin{equation*}
        L(s, \chi) := L(\chi \abs{\cdot}^s).
    \end{equation*}
    Moreover we define the \textbf{shifted dual} (or sometimes \textbf{twisted dual}) of $\chi$ by:
    \begin{equation*}
        \chi^\lor := \inv{\chi}\abs{\cdot}.
    \end{equation*}
\end{defn}

\begin{observation}
The twisted dual satisfies the following nice identities, which will come of use later:
\begin{equation*}
    \re{\chi^\lor} = 1 - \re{\chi},
\end{equation*}
and
\begin{equation*}
    L(s, \chi^\lor) = L(1-s, \inv{\chi}).
\end{equation*} 
\end{observation}

In order to discuss the local zeta integral, we must establish a sound measure-theoretic basis with which we will integrate. In Tate's thesis (which only treats number fields) he normalizes his Haar measurs on the local field to be self-dual relative to a choice of nontrivial additive character $\psi$, from which he obtains the nice classical Fourier identity $f(x) = \doublehat{f}(-x)$. Here instead we explore the functional equation's dependency on choice of additive character and Haar measure.

Given $\dx$ a Haar measure on $F$, define 
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn: measure on F^times}
    \d{^\times x} := c \cdot \frac{\dx}{\abs{x}}
\end{equation}
for some $c \in \rr_{> 0}$, which we always set to 1 in the Archimedean case. Then $\d{^\times x}$ is a Haar measure on $F^\times$. One may ask why we don't always set $c = 1$, and the reason for allowing ourselves this flexibility will become clear when we get to the computations of the global zeta functions in \S \ref{subsection: global zeta function}.

Fixing a nontrivial additive character $\psi \in \hat{F}$, for any $f \in \fans(F)$ we define its \textbf{Fourier transform} by:
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn: Fourier transform local-zeta-func}
    \hat{f}(y) := \int_F f(x)\psi(xy)\dx.
\end{equation}
The function $\hat{f}$ is well-defined and in fact again lies in $\fans(F)$, though it depends on our choice of $\psi$ and $\dx$. When $F = \rr$ or $\cc$ this fact is classical; when $F$ is non-Archimedean this is follows by a simple generalization of the proof of Proposition \ref{prop: self-dual local measures}. In particular, one can the fact that any such $f$ is compactly supported and locally constant (hence some finite linear combination of the function used for computation in that proof), and that changing the measure or additive character $\psi$ only affects things by some global scaling factor. We leave the (somewhat tedious) verification of the details to the reader.

We are now ready to define the (local!) zeta function.

\begin{defn}
\label{def: local zeta function}
    Given $f \in \fans(F)$ and $\chi \in \fanx(F^\times)$, we define the associated \textbf{local zeta integral} to be:
    \begin{equation*}
        Z(f, \chi) := \int_{F^\times} f(x)\chi(x)\d{^\times x}.
    \end{equation*} 
\end{defn}

The convenience of defining the space $\fanx(F^\times)$ in Definition \ref{def: exponent and unramified character} is hopefully clear with the $L$-factors and zeta functions defined, as it allows us to write zeta functions, a priori taking quasi-characters $\chi$ as inputs, as functions on $\cc$, which is frequently used in literature for ease of notation. The following is the main result of this subsection.

\begin{theorem}[The Local Functional Equation]
\label{thm: local zeta function convergence and func-eqn}
    For $F$ a local field, take  $f \in \fans(F)$ and $\chi = \mu \abs{\cdot}^s \in \fanx(F^\times)$ with exponent $\sigma = \re{s}$. Then:
    \begin{enumerate}[(a)]
        \item \label{thmitem: abs-convg of local zeta function} If $\sigma > 0$ then the local zeta integral $Z(f, \chi)$ is absolutely convergent and holomorphic in such $\chi$.
        \item \label{thmitem: functional-eqn of local zeta func} If $\sigma \in (0, 1)$ then the following functional equation holds:
        \begin{equation*}
            Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) = \gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx) Z(f, \chi)
        \end{equation*}
        for some $\gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx) \in \cc^\times$ independent of $f$, a priori defined on the strip $\sigma \in (0, 1)$ on each connected component of $\fanx(F^\times)$, and for all $\chi \in \fanx(F^\times)$ via meromorphic continuation.
        \item \label{thmitem: epsilon factor local-zeta-func} There exists $\epsilon(\chi, \psi, \dx) \in \cc^\times$ satisfying:
        \begin{equation*}
            \gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx ) = \epsilon(\chi, \psi, \dx) \frac{L(\chi^\lor)}{L(\chi)}
        \end{equation*}
        as a meromorphic function of $\chi \in \fanx(F^\times)$.
    \end{enumerate} 
\end{theorem}

\begin{observation}
Note that part \ref{thmitem: abs-convg of local zeta function} tells us that $Z(f, \chi)$ is absolutely convergent to the right of $\sigma = 0$ on each connected component of $\fanx(F^\times)$, from which it follows by construction that $Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor)$ is absolutely convergent to the left of $\sigma = 1$. Using this domain overlap along with part \ref{thmitem: functional-eqn of local zeta func}, we see that this theorem automatically yields a meromorphic continuation of $Z(f, \chi)$ to all $\chi \in \fanx(F^\times)$.
\end{observation}

\begin{proof}[Proof of part \ref{thmitem: abs-convg of local zeta function} in Theorem \ref{thm: local zeta function convergence and func-eqn}]
To show $Z(f, \chi)$ is absolutely convergent when $\sigma > 0$, using our relationship between our Haar measures in (\ref{eqn: measure on F^times}) and the fact that $\mu$ is unitary it suffices to show that:
\begin{equation*}
    I(f, \sigma) = c \int_{F \setminus \set{0}} \abs{f(x)} \abs{x}^{\sigma - 1} \dx < \infty,
\end{equation*}
which will also ensure holomorphicity in $\chi$. In the Archimedean case, finiteness of $I(f, \sigma)$ follows from the fact that $f$ is a Schwartz function, and thus rapidly decaying as $\abs{x}$ approaches infinity, and that $\abs{x}^{\sigma - 1}$ is integrable near 0\footnote{For $F = \cc$, we should be a bit careful here since the Haar measure and absolute value here are not the usual ones, but we note that we have $\frac{\abs{\dz \wedge \d{\bar{z}}}}{\abs{z}^{2(\sigma - 1)}} = \frac{\abs{\dz}}{\abs{z}^{\sigma - 1}} \times \frac{\d{\bar{z}}}{\abs{z}^{\sigma - 1}}$, so this integral remains finite.}, so we are happy.


In the non-Archimedean case, $f$ is locally constant with compact support, so it must factor through some finite group quotient of the form $\pp^n/\pp^m$, for some integers $m \leq n$ (here $\pp^k = \brangle{\pi^k}$ represents the fractional ideal of $\calo_F$ generated by the $k$-th power of its uniformizer). By linearity and translation-invariance of the Haar measure, it suffices to check finiteness of the integral for functions $f$ that are just the characteristic functions of the various ideals $\pp^j$.\\
Using unique factorizaition in any fractional ideal, we can decompose it into a disjoint union:
\begin{equation*}
    \pp^j \setminus \set{0} = \bigsqcup^\infty_{k=j} \pi^k \calo_F^\times.
\end{equation*}
Thus our integral becomes:
\begin{align*}
        I(f, \sigma) &= c \int_{F \setminus \set{0}} \abs{f(x)} \abs{x}^{\sigma - 1} \dx \\
        &= \int_{F^\times} \abs{f(x)} \abs{x}^\sigma \d{^\times x}\\
        &= \sum_{k \geq j} \int_{\pi^k \calo_F^\times} \abs{x}^\sigma \d{^\times x}\\
        &= \sum_{k \geq j}\vol(\calo_F^\times, \d{^\times x})q^{-k \sigma}\\
        &= \vol(\calo_F^\times, \d{^\times x}) \frac{q^{-j\sigma}}{1 - q^{- \sigma}}
\end{align*}
which is finite when $\sigma > 0$, as desired.
\end{proof}

To prove the next part of our main theorem, we use a lemma that is key in Tate's thesis. 
\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma: local-zeta-func func eqn}
    For $g \in \fans(F)$ and any quasi-character $\chi$ with exponent $\sigma \in (0, 1)$, we have:
    \begin{equation*}
        Z(f, \chi) Z(\hat{g}, \chi^\lor) = Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor)Z(g, \chi).
    \end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Expanded out, the left side of the Lemma is the following product of integrals:
\begin{equation*}
    Z(f, \chi) Z(\hat{g}, \chi^\lor) = \int_{F^\times} f(x) \chi(x) \d{^\times x} \cdot \int_{F^\times} \hat{g}(y) \inv{\chi}(y) \abs{y} \d{^\times  y},
\end{equation*}
both of which are absolutely convergent by the settle part \ref{thmitem: abs-convg of local zeta function} of Theorem \ref{thm: local zeta function convergence and func-eqn}. We can turn this into a double integral over the product space by:
\begin{equation*}
    \iint_{F^\times \times F^\times} f(x)\hat{g}(y) \chi(x \inv{y}) \abs{y} \d{^\times x} \d{^\times  y}.
\end{equation*}
Using invariance of the product measure under the "shearing" automorphism $(x, y) \mapsto (x, xy)$, we obtain:
\begin{equation*}
    \iint_{F^\times \times F^\times} f(x)\hat{g}(xy)\chi(\inv{y}) \abs{xy} \d{^\times x} \d{^\times  y},
\end{equation*}
which, by Fubini's theorem, is equal to:
\begin{equation*}
    \int_{F^\times} \brack{\int_{F^\times} f(x)\hat{g}(xy) \abs{x} \d{^\times x}} \chi(\inv{y}) \abs{y} \d{^\times  y}.
\end{equation*}
To prove the lemma it remains to show that the inner integral is symmetric in $f$ and $g$. Expanding out the Fourier transform of $g$ (recalling our convention in  (\ref{eqn: Fourier transform local-zeta-func}) and transferring it to an integral over $F$ using (\ref{eqn: measure on F^times}), we get that the inner integral is equal to:
\begin{equation*}
    c \iint_{F \times F} f(x) g(z) \psi(xyz) \dz \dx.
\end{equation*}
Note we replaced $F^\times$ with $F$, which is of no issue since $\set{0} \subseteq F$ is a set of measure zero. We switch the order of integration via Fubini to obtain:
\begin{equation*}
    c \iint_{F \times F} f(x) g(z) \psi(xyz) \dx \dz = c \int_F g(z) \hat{f}(zy) \dz = \int_{F^\times}  g(z) \hat{f}(zy) \abs{z} \d{^\times  z}
\end{equation*}
which gives us the desired result.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \ref{thmitem: functional-eqn of local zeta func} in \ref{thm: local zeta function convergence and func-eqn}]
Let $\chi$ be a representative of any connected component of $\fanx(F^\times)$. First suppose $Z(f, \chi) \neq 0$. Then our functional equation in Lemma \ref{lemma: local-zeta-func func eqn} tells us that:
\begin{equation*}
    \gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx) := \frac{Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor)}{Z(f, \chi)}
\end{equation*}
is independent of our choice of $f \in \fans(F)$, when $Z(f, \chi) \neq 0$. Note that:
\begin{equation*}
    \chi^\lor = \brack{\mu \abs{\cdot}^s}^\lor = \inv{\mu} \abs{\cdot}^{1-s}
\end{equation*}
By (the already proved!) part \ref{thmitem: abs-convg of local zeta function} of Theorem \ref{thm: local zeta function convergence and func-eqn}, we know $Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor)$ is absolutely convergent when $\sigma = \re{s} < 1$, and we have already seen that the denominator of $\gamma$ is absolutely convergent for all positive $\sigma$, so the functional equation in terms of $\gamma$ holds for all $\sigma \in (0, 1)$ when $Z(f, \chi) \neq 0$.

If $Z(f, \chi) = 0$, we want to show that $Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) = 0$ as well, which will follow from Lemma \ref{lemma: local-zeta-func func eqn} so long as we exhibit, for any $\chi$, the existence of a $g \in \fans(F)$ so that $Z(g, \chi) \neq 0$. For this, we turn to the proof of part \ref{thmitem: epsilon factor local-zeta-func} of the theorem, where we will compute explicit expressions for the zeta function and for the $\gamma$-factor which will directly imply these results. These are tabulated in Tables \ref{table: standard functions} and \ref{table: zeta functions and epsilon factors} in \S \ref{subsection: The Epsilon and Gamma factors}.

Finally, the fact that $\gamma$ admits meromorphic continuation to all of $\fanx(F^\times)$ will follow from the fact that it will be a familiar complex-valued function on the strip $\re{\chi} \in (0, 1)$ on each connected component, and thus will trivially admit a meromorphic continuation to all of $\fanx(F^\times)$. Again, these are tabulated and discussed further in \S \ref{subsection: The Epsilon and Gamma factors}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of part \ref{thmitem: epsilon factor local-zeta-func} in Theorem \ref{thm: local zeta function convergence and func-eqn} in the Archimedean case]
As shown in the proof of part \ref{thmitem: functional-eqn of local zeta func} of the theorem, the value of the $\gamma$ function is independent of our choice of $f \in \fans(F)$. Thus we may choose a special function, or family of functions, for each of the possible cases of $F$ to make our computations convenient. We will select a standard measure $\dx$ for each case, which is self-dual for an appropriate choice of $\psi$, and then analyze dependency on arbitrary $\dx$ and $\psi$ afterward, in \S \ref{subsection: The Epsilon and Gamma factors}.

\textbf{\underline{Case 1: $F = \rr$}}\\
We take $\dx$ to be the usual Lebesgue measure, and set our additive character to be:
\begin{equation*}
    \psi(x) = e^{- 2 \pi i x}.
\end{equation*}
As we noted in our discussion of the local $L$-factors, any $\chi \in \fanx(\rr^\times)$ is of the form $\abs{\cdot}^s$ or $\sgn \abs{\cdot}^s$.\\

\underline{Sub-Case (i): $\chi = \abs{\cdot}^s$}\\
Take $f(x) = e^{- \pi x^2}$, which is visibly in $\fans(\rr)$. Then recalling that we set the scaling constant in the measure transformation of (\ref{eqn: measure on F^times}) to 1, we get:
\begin{equation*}
    Z(f, \chi) = \int_{\rr^\times} e^{-\pi x^2} \abs{x}^s \d{^\times x} = 2 \int^\infty_0 e^{-\pi x^2} x^{s-1} \dx.
\end{equation*}
Using a substitution $u = \pi x^2$ (hence $\du = 2 \pi x \dx$),  the integral becomes:
\begin{equation*}
    \pi^{-s/2} \int^\infty_0 e^{-u} u^{s/2 - 1} \du = \pi^{-s/2} \Gamma(s/2).
\end{equation*}
and we see from (\ref{eqn: real local L-factor}) that this is exactly our local $L$-factor, so in fact $Z(f, \chi) = L(\chi)$ for all characters of this form. Classical Fourier analysis also tells us $f$ is self-dual via the Fourier transform, i.e.
\begin{equation*}
    \hat{f}(y) = \int_\rr e^{-\pi x^2} e^{-2 \pi i xy} \dx = f(x).
\end{equation*}
Thus, we have:
\begin{equation*}
    Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) = \int_{\rr^\times} \hat{f}(x) \chi^\lor(x) \d{^\times x} = \int_{\rr^\times} f(x) \chi^\lor(x) \d{^\times x} = Z(f, \chi^\lor)
\end{equation*}
which is equal to $L(\chi^\lor)$ by our previous calculation in this proof. Thus if $\chi = \abs{\cdot}^s$, we have:
\begin{align}
\label{eqn: real epsilon factor 1}
    \gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx) &= \frac{L(\chi^\lor)}{L(\chi)} \nonumber \\
    \implies \epsilon(\chi, \psi, \dx) &= 1.
\end{align}
Note here that division by $L(\chi) = \Gamma_\rr(s)$ is no issue, since the $\Gamma_\rr$ is (globally) non-vanishing and well-defined on the strip $\re{s} \in (0, 1)$.\\

\underline{Sub-Case (ii): $\chi = \sgn \abs{\cdot}^s$}\\
In this case we choose $f(x) = xe^{-\pi x^2}$, which is again immediately verifiable as rapidly decaying. Expanding the local zeta function we get:
\begin{align*}
    Z(f, \chi) &= \int_{\rr^\times} x e^{-\pi x^2} \frac{x}{\abs{x}} \abs{x}^s \d{^\times x}\\
    &= \int_{\rr^\times} e^{-\pi x^2} \abs{x}^{s+1} \d{^\times x}\\
    &= \pi^{-(s+1)/2} \Gamma\brack{\frac{s+1}{2}}.\\
    &= \Gamma_\rr(s+1)
\end{align*}
which we again recall from (\ref{eqn: real local L-factor}) as by definition our real local $L$-factor $L(\chi)$.\\
Classical Fourier duality on the Schwartz space tells us that multiplication by $i$ corresponds to multiplication by $x$, so we get the identity
\begin{equation*}
    \hat{f}(y) = i y e^{- \pi y^2 } = i f(y),
\end{equation*} 
from which it follows 
\begin{equation*}
    Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) = \int_{\rr^\times} i f(y) \chi^\lor \d{^\times x} = i L(\chi^\lor).
\end{equation*}
Thus, by a similar calculation to the last sub-case we deduce:
\begin{align}
\label{eqn: real epsilon factor 2}
    \epsilon(\chi, \psi, \dx) = i.
\end{align}

\textbf{\underline{Case 2: $F = \cc$}}\\
Here we let our measure on $\cc$ be $\abs{\dz  \wedge \d{\bar{z}}} = 2 \abs{\dx \wedge \dy}$, double the standard Lebesgue measure. In polar coordinates this is $2 r \abs{ \dr \wedge \dtheta}$, which also implies the Haar measure on $F^\times$ is $\d{^\times  z} = (2/r) \dr \dtheta$ (we divide by $r^2$ since our normalized absolute value on $\cc$ is the square of the usual one). We choose this as it is self-dual with respect to the additive quasi-character
\begin{equation*}
    \psi(z) := e^{- 2 \pi i (z + \bar{z})}.
\end{equation*} 
We also adjust the standard norm on $\cc$ to agree with the modulus, i.e
\begin{equation*}
    \abs{z} := z \bar{z}.
\end{equation*}
Since $\cc^\times \cong \rr_{> 0} \times S^1$, any multiplicative quasi-character must be of the form:
\begin{equation*}
    \chi_{s, n} := z \mapsto \abs{z}^s \brack{\frac{z}{\abs{z}}}^n
\end{equation*}
for $s \in \cc$ and $n \in \zz$ uniquely defined. We choose our class of functions to be:
\begin{equation*}
    f_n(z) := \begin{cases}
        \frac{1}{2\pi} \brack{\bar{z}}^n e^{- 2 \pi z \bar{z}}, & n \geq 0,\\
        \frac{1}{2\pi} z^{-n} e^{- 2 \pi z \bar{z}}, & n < 0.
    \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
We note that $f$ is visibly a Schwartz function for any $n$. Computing the local zeta function for non-negative $n$, we get:
\begin{align*}
    Z(f_n, \chi_{s, n}) &= \int_{\cc^\times} f_n(z) \chi_{s, n}(z)\d{^\times  z}\\
    &= \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_{\cc^\times}\brack{\bar{z}}^ne^{ - 2 \pi z \bar{z}}(z \bar{z})^s e^{i n \arg(z)}\d{^\times  z}\\
    &= \frac{1}{2\pi} \int^{2 \pi}_0 \int^\infty_0 (re^{-i \theta})^n e^{-2 \pi r^2} (r^2)^s e^{i n \theta} (2/r) \dr \dtheta\\
    &= \frac{1}{\pi} \int^{2\pi}_0 \int^\infty_0 r^{2s + n - 1} e^{-2 \pi r^2} \dr \dtheta\\
    &= (2 \pi)^{-\brack{s + \frac{n}{2}}} \int_0^\infty e^{- 2 \pi r^2} (2 \pi r)^{s + \frac{n}{2} - 1} 4 \pi r \dr\\
    &= (2 \pi)^{-\brack{s + \frac{n}{2}}} \int^\infty_0 e^{-t} t^{s + \frac{n}{2} - 1} \dt\\
    &= (2\pi)^{-\brack{s + \frac{n}{2}}} \Gamma(s + \frac{n}{2}) = L(\chi_{s, n})
\end{align*}
where we recall our definition of the local $L$-factor in (\ref{eqn: complex local L-factor}). A similar calculation also shows that $Z(f_n, \chi_{s, n}) = L(\chi_{s, n})$ when $n < 0$.

It remains to compute the "dual" local zeta function. Classical Fourier duality tells us that we have
\begin{equation*}
    \hat{f_n}(z) = i^{\abs{n}}f_{-n}(z)
\end{equation*}
for all $n$. Next, we notice that $\chi^\lor_{n, s} = \chi_{1-s, -n}$ for any $s$ and $n$. Using the fact that our computation was linear in the exponents $s$ and $n$, we deduce that
\begin{equation*}
    Z(\hat{f_n}, \chi^\lor_{s, n})  = i^{\abs{n}}L(\chi^\lor_{s, n}).
\end{equation*}
As a consequence we get that:
\begin{align}
    \label{eqn: complex epsilon factor}
    \gamma(\chi_{s, n}, \psi, \dx) &= i^{\abs{n}} \frac{L(\chi^\lor_{s, n})}{L(\chi_{s, n})} \nonumber \\
    \implies  \epsilon(\chi_{s, n}, \psi, \dx) &= i^{\abs{n}},
\end{align}
where again division by $L(\chi_{s, n})$ is okay since $\Gamma_\cc(s)$ is (globally) nonvanishing and well-defined on the strip $\re{s} \in (0, 1)$. This completes the complex case.\\

To conclude our primary analysis on the Archimedean local fields, we note by looking at all the (nowhere-vanishing!) local epsilon factors computed in (\ref{eqn: real epsilon factor 1}), (\ref{eqn: real epsilon factor 2}) and (\ref{eqn: complex epsilon factor}), and their corresponding local $L$-factors that $\gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx)$ is indeed meromorphic as a function of $\chi$, and that the $\epsilon$-factor has the desired properties. Though we showed this for a specific \textit{choice} of $\psi$ and $\dx$, in \S \ref{subsection: The Epsilon and Gamma factors} we will show that these hold for any $\psi$ and $\dx$.

Moreover, we exhibited, for each connected component of $\fanx(F^\times)$, a function $f \in \fans(F)$ for which $\chi \mapsto Z(f, \chi)$ on the strip $0 < \re{\chi} < 1$ on that component is visibly not identically zero, as required for the last part of the proof of part \ref{thmitem: functional-eqn of local zeta func} of Theorem \ref{thm: local zeta function convergence and func-eqn}.
\end{proof}

Before we proceed complete the proof of the final part of the theorem for the non-Archimedean case, we need to build a tool that will be vital in simplifying later expressions of the local zeta integrals.
\begin{defn}
    \label{def: Gauss sum}
    Given a locally compact topological ring $R$ with additive character $\psi: R \to S^1$, multiplicative character $\chi: R^\times \to S^1$, and Haar measure $\d{^\times u}$ on the unit group, we define the associated \textbf{Gauss sum} to be:
    \begin{equation*}
        g(\chi, \psi) := \int_{R^\times} \chi(u) \psi(u) \d{^\times  u}.
    \end{equation*}
    \end{defn}
    \begin{remark}
        Gauss sums turn out to be a hugely convenient notation that show up in a host of different applications, from theta-functions to cyclotomic fields. In fact, Weil made use of these Gauss-sums as a key step in his diagonal variety point-counting argument to motivate the Weil Conjectures in his seminal 1949 paper \cite{WeilOG_Paper}.
    \end{remark}

We will use this language and notation of conductors and Gauss sums to simplify the integrands of the local zeta functions via following key lemma.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma: gauss-sum conductor relations}
Let $\chi$ be a multiplicative character on $\calo_F^\times$ and $\psi$ be an additive character, with conductors $\pp^n$ and $\pp^m$ respectively. Define $\d{^\times x} := \d{x/\abs{x}}$, for $\dx$ a Haar measure on $F$, and let $c$ denote the scaling factor of the multiplicative measure $\d{^\times u}$, as discussed in (\ref{eqn: measure on F^times}). Then:
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
    \item If $m < n$ then $g(\chi, \psi) = 0$.
    \item If $m = n$ then $\abs{g(\chi, \psi)}^2 = c \vol(\calo_F, \dx) \vol(1 + \pp^n, \d{^\times x})$.
    \item If $m > n$ then $\abs{g(\chi, \psi)}^2 = c \vol(\calo_F, \dx) \brack{\vol(1 + \pp^n, \d{^\times x}) - \vol(1 + \pp^{m-1}, \d{^\times x})}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\underline{Case 1: $m < n$}\\
We decompose $\calo_F^\times$ into the disjoint union of (finitely many!) cosets of the unit subgroups $U_m := 1 + \pp^m$. Note for any $a, b \in \calo_F$ we have
\begin{equation*}
    \psi(a(1 + \pi^m b)) = \psi(a) \psi(a\pi^m b) = \psi(a),
\end{equation*}
by definition of the conductor $\pp^m = \brangle{\pi^m}$. Since $n \geq m$, we note $\chi$ is constant on cosets of $U_m$ in $\calo_F^\times$, the Gauss sum thus becomes:
\begin{equation*}
    g(\chi, \psi) = \sum_{\calo_F^\times/U_m} \brack{\psi(a) \chi(a) \int_{U_m} \chi(u) \d{^\times  u}}.
\end{equation*}
However since $m < n$, the subgroup $U_m$ is a strict superset of $U_n$, and so, by definition of the conductor, the character $\chi$ is non-trivial on $U_m$. Recall the integral of a nontrivial character over a compact subgroup is zero, which in turn makes the entire Gauss sum zero, as desired. This proves (a).

\underline{Case 2: $m \geq n$}\\
Expanding out the expression and making use of Fubini, we get:
\begin{align*}
    \abs{g(\chi, \psi)}^2 &= g(\chi, \psi) \bar{g(\chi, \psi)}\\
    &= \int_{\calo_F^\times} \chi(x) \psi(x) \d{^\times x} \cdot \bar{\int_{\calo_F^\times} \chi(y) \psi(y) \d{^\times  y}}\\
    &= \iint_{\calo_F^\times} \chi(x\inv{y})\psi(x-y)  \d{^\times x} \d{^\times  y}.
\end{align*}
Making the substitution $x\inv{y} \mapsto z$ the above integral becomes
\begin{equation*}
    \int_{\calo_F^\times} \chi(z) h(z) \d{^\times  z},
\end{equation*}
where 
\begin{equation*}
    h(z) := \int_{\calo_F^\times} \psi(yz - y)\d{^\times  y} = c \int_{\calo_F^\times} \psi(y(z-1)) \dy.
\end{equation*}
Note we can switch the Haar measure in the second equality using Equation \ref{eqn: measure on F^times} and the fact that $\abs{y} = 1$ for all $y \in \calo_F^\times$. Making use of the fact that $\calo_F$ is a local ring, we get:
\begin{equation*}
    h(z) = c \brack{\int_{\calo_F} \psi(y(z - 1)) \dy - \int_\pp \psi(y(z - 1)) \dy}.
\end{equation*}
Note that if $\nu_\pp(z-1)  <  m-1$ then in the above integrands $y(z-1)$ takes values outside of the conductor of $\psi$, and thus the integrands are non-trivial characters. If $\nu_\pp(z-1) = m - 1$ then the integrand is trivial over $\pp$, but not $\calo_F$, and otherwise it is trivial over all of $\calo_F$. These observations give us:
\begin{equation*}
    h(z) = \begin{cases}
        0, & \nu_\pp(z-1) < m-1\\
        -c\frac{\vol(\calo_F, \dx)}{q}, & \nu_\pp(z-1) = m-1\\
        c\vol(\calo_F, \dx)\brack{1 - \frac{1}{q}}, &   \nu_\pp(z-1) \geq m.
    \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
All that remains is to substitute these cases back into the integral $\int \chi(z)h(z) \d{^\times  z}$ to show the desired equalities in part (b) and part (c), which follows from direct computation, as the reader may verify.
\end{proof}


Now we have the necessary tools and results to prove the final part of Theorem \ref{thm: local zeta function convergence and func-eqn} in the non-Archimedean case. We first make a quick observation to classify the multiplicative characters we are working with. Recall from our discussion in the prelude to Definition \ref{def: exponent and unramified character} that any such character of $F^\times$ is of the form:
\begin{equation*}
    \chi_{s, n} := \brack{x \mapsto \abs{x}^s \omega \brack{\frac{x}{\abs{x}}}},
\end{equation*}
where $x \mapsto x/\abs{x}$ is some \textit{choice} of homomorphic retraction (which we may take to be the literal normalization map) to $\calo_F^\times \inj F^\times$ and $\omega$ is a unitary character on $\calo_F^\times$ with conductor $\pp^n$. Note the indices $s, n$ do not completely determine the character $\chi_{s, n}$, as there is some choice of $\omega$ to be made, but this notation will end up sufficing for the computations ahead. Note also that in the unramified case (i.e $n=0$), the unitary character $\omega$ is trivial. This fact will be used in the proof.

\begin{proof}[Proof of part \ref{thmitem: epsilon factor local-zeta-func} in Theorem \ref{thm: local zeta function convergence and func-eqn} in the non-Archimedean case]

Supposing our additive character $\psi$ has conductor $\pp^m$, we define:
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: non-arch f in Z(f, chi)}
    f(x) := \begin{cases}
        \psi(x), & x \in \pp^{m-n}\\
        0, & \text{ otherwise}.
    \end{cases}
\end{equation}
This is a Schwartz-Bruhat function as it is compactly-supported by definition, and also locally constant, since $\psi = \psi(0) = 1$ near 0, as $\cc^\times$ has "no small subgroups". We now compute the local zeta integrals $Z(f, \chi_{s, n})$ in two cases: $n$ zero, and $n$ positive.\\

\underline{\textbf{Case 1: $n = 0$}}\\
This means that $\omega$ is trivial, i.e $\chi_{s, 0}$ is an unramified character. We recall $\psi$ is trivial on $\pp^m$, and therefore $f$ is zero outside of the conductor of $\psi$. Using similar methods as in the proof of Lemma \ref{lemma: gauss-sum conductor relations}, we compute:
\begin{align}
\label{eqn: local zeta function non-Arch unramified}
    Z(f, \chi_{s, 0}) &= \int_{F^\times} f(x)\chi_{s, 0}(x) \d{^\times x} \nonumber \\
    &= \int_{\pp^m \setminus \set{0}} \psi(x) \abs{x}^s \omega(x/\abs{x}) \d{^\times x} \nonumber \\
    &= \int_{\pp^m \setminus \set{0}} \abs{x}^s \d{^\times x}\nonumber \\
    &= \sum_{k \geq m} \brack{\int_{\pi^k \calo_F^\times} \abs{x}^s \d{^\times x}}\nonumber \\
    &= \vol(\calo_F^\times, \d{^\times x}) \sum_{k \geq m} q^{-ks}\nonumber \\
    &= q^{-ms} \vol(\calo_F^\times, \d{^\times x} ) L(\chi_{s, 0}),
\end{align}
where we recall our definition of the local $L$-factor in the unramified case in (\ref{eqn: non-Arch local L-factor}).\\

\underline{\textbf{Case 2: $n > 0$}}\\
Recalling that $f$ is zero outside of $\pp^{m-n}$, by similar logic as in the $n=0$ case we get:
\begin{align*}
    Z(f, \chi_{s, n}) &= \sum_{k \geq m-n} \brack{ \int_{\pi^k \calo_F^\times} \psi(x) \abs{x}^s \omega(x/\abs{x}) \d{^\times x}}\\
    &= \sum_{k \geq m-n} \brack{ \int_{\calo_F^\times} \psi(\pi^k u)  \abs{\pi^k u}^s \omega(u)/\omega(\pi^k) \d{^\times  u}}\\
    &= \sum_{k \geq m-n} q^{-ks} \brack{\int_{\calo_F^\times} \psi(\pi^k u) \omega(u) \d{^\times  u}}\\
    &= \sum_{k \geq m-n}q^{-ks}g(\omega, \psi_{\pi^k}).
\end{align*}
In the last line the Gauss sum notation comes as a typographical godsave for us, where $\psi_{\pi^k}(x) := \psi(x \pi^k)$. We note that the conductor of $\psi_{\pi^k}$ is $m-k$, so in particular if $k > m - n$ then it contains the conductor of $\omega$. We thus get:
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn: local zeta function non-Arch ramified}
    Z(f, \chi_{s, n}) = q^{(n-m)s} g(\omega, \psi_{\pi^{m-n}}) = q^{(n-m)s} c \vol(\calo_F, \dx) \vol(1 + \pp^n, \d{^\times x}) \inv{(\bar{g(\omega, \psi_{\pi^{m -n}})})},
\end{equation}
where the first equality follows from part (a) of Lemma \ref{lemma: gauss-sum conductor relations} and the second from part (b) of the same lemma. Thus the zeta function, as a function of $s \in \cc$ restricted to the strip $0 < \re{s} < 1$, is a visibly holomorphic function with no zeroes or poles.


\begin{sanitycheck}
Note that to define the inverse of the Gauss sum and to ensure $Z(f, \chi)$ is not identically zero in \ref{eqn: local zeta function non-Arch ramified}, we must have that $g(\omega, \psi_{m-n}) \neq 0$. But examining Lemma \ref{lemma: gauss-sum conductor relations}, since the conductor of $\psi$ is $m-n < n$, (since $n \geq 0$), it follows that the Gauss sum is nonzero, so we may rest assured.
\end{sanitycheck}

Now we need to compute the zeta function of the dual, where here we define the Fourier transform also with respect to our additive character $\psi$ of conductor $\pp^m$, i.e.
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn: non-Arch Fourier transform integral}
    \hat{f}(y) := \int_F f(x)\psi(xy) \dx = \int_{\pp^{m-n}} \psi(x(y+1)) \dx. 
\end{equation}
We proceed with similar casework to before.\\

\underline{\textbf{Case 1: $n=0$}}\\
Since the conductor of $\psi$ is $\pp^m$, we observe from (\ref{eqn: non-Arch Fourier transform integral}) that $\hat{f}(y)$ is the integral of $\psi(x(y+1))$ taken over $\pp^m$. The character is non-trivial on $\pp^m$, and thus $\hat{f}(y)$ is zero, exactly when $y \notin \calo_F$. Thus in this case we have:
\begin{equation*}
    \hat{f}(y) = \begin{cases}
    \vol(\pp^m, \dx), & y \in \calo_F\\
    0, & \text{ otherwise},
    \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
i.e we have a scaled characteristic function of the ring of integers. Using this, we compute:
\begin{align}
\label{eqn: dual local zeta function non-Arch unramified case}
    Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor_{s, 0}) &= \int_{\calo_F \setminus \set{0}} \hat{f}(x) \chi^\lor_{s, 0}(x) \d{^\times x} \nonumber \\
    &= \vol(\pp^m, \dx) \sum_{k \geq 0} \brack{\int_{\calo_F^\times} \abs{\pi^k x}^{1-s} \d{^\times x}} \nonumber \\
    &= \vol(\pp^m, \dx) \vol(\calo_F^\times, \d{^\times x}) \frac{1}{1 - q^{(1-s)}} \nonumber \\
    &= \vol(\pp^m, \dx) \vol(\calo_F^\times, \d{^\times x}) L(\chi^\lor_{s, 0}).
\end{align}
Combing (\ref{eqn: local zeta function non-Arch unramified}) and (\ref{eqn: dual local zeta function non-Arch unramified case}), we get that for $\chi = \chi_{s, 0}$ we have:
\begin{align}
\label{eqn: local epsilon factor non-Arch unramified}
    \gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx) &= q^{ms} \vol(\pp^m, \dx) \frac{L(\chi^\lor)}{L(\chi)} \nonumber \\
    \implies \epsilon(\chi, \psi, \dx) &= q^{ms}\vol(\pp^m, \dx).
\end{align}

\textbf{\underline{Case 2: $n > 0$}}\\
Examining (\ref{eqn: Fourier transform local-zeta-func}) again, we see that the integrand $\psi(x(y+1)) = 1$ for all $x \in \pp^{m-n}$ exactly when $y + 1 \in \pp^n$, and otherwise it is a nontrivial character of $x$ and thus $\hat{f}(y) = 0$. We deduce then that $\hat{f}$ is the characteristic function of $\pp^n - 1$ scaled by the factor $\vol(\pp^{m-n}, \dx)$. Noting that $\pp^n - 1 \subseteq \calo_F^\times$ (since $n > 0$), we compute the dual local zeta function:
\begin{align}
\label{eqn: dual local zeta funtion non-Arch ramified}
    Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor_{s, n}) &= \int_{\pp^n - 1} \hat{f}(x) \chi^\lor_{s, n}(x) \d{^\times x} \nonumber \\
    &= \vol(\pp^{m-n}, \dx) \int_{\pp^n - 1} \abs{x}^{1-s} \bar{\omega}(x) \d{^\times x} \nonumber \\
    &= q^{n-m} \vol(\calo_F, \dx) \int_{1 + \pp^n} \bar{\omega}(-1)\bar{\omega}(x) \d{^\times x} \nonumber \\
    &= q^{n-m} \vol(\calo_F, \dx) \vol(1 + \pp^n, \d{^\times x}) \omega(-1),
\end{align}
where the last line follows from the fact that the conductor of $\omega$ is equal to the conductor of its conjugate, so it is trivial on all of $1 + \pp^n$. Combining (\ref{eqn: local zeta function non-Arch ramified}) and (\ref{eqn: dual local zeta funtion non-Arch ramified}) for $\chi = \chi_{s, n}$, we get:
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn: epsilon factor non-Arch ramified}
    \epsilon(\chi, \psi, \dx) = \gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx) = \frac{1}{c} q^{(m-n)(s-1) \omega(-1)} \bar{ g( \omega, \psi_{\pi^{m-n}})} = \frac{1}{c}q^{(m-n)(s-1)}g(\bar{\omega}, \psi_{\pi^{m-n}}).
\end{equation}

The final equality above made use of the following identity (the validity of which we leave the reader to verify) that, for any Gauss sum:
\begin{equation*}
    \bar{g(\chi, \psi)} = \chi(-1) g(\bar{\chi}, \psi).
\end{equation*}

We now conclude our analysis. The equality involving the epsilon factor and relevant local $L$-factors have been shown in (\ref{eqn: local epsilon factor non-Arch unramified}) and (\ref{eqn: epsilon factor non-Arch ramified}), from all of which $\gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx)$ is visibly meromorphic in $\chi$, completing our proof.
\end{proof}

Our work is not yet complete. We have implicitly shown the necessary conditions to allow the proofs of parts (b) and (c) to hold in our computations, in that we have demonstrated nontrivial zeta functions and holomorphicity of the $\epsilon$-factors on the strip $\re{\chi} \in (0, 1)$ on each connected component of $\fanx(F^\times$) to allow the $\gamma$-factor to be meromorphically continued to the entire space. This will be discussed this more explicitly in the following subsection. There we also analyze dependence of the $\gamma$-factors on choice of additive character and Haar measure, thus allowing the results to hold outside of the specific choices made in our computations.

\subsection{The Epsilon and Gamma Factors (with many tables)}
\label{subsection: The Epsilon and Gamma factors}

In the previous subsection we proved a functional equation of the zeta function on local fields, which involved the so-called gamma and epsilon factors, which were functions of our mulitplicative character $\chi$, our fixed additive character $\psi$, and our choice of Haar measure $\dx$ on our local field. In here we analyze the dependency of these factors on all three inputs, and prove some results about these $\epsilon$-factors in general, and their connection to the so-called root number. First, for ease of reference here we summarize all our computations of the previous subsection.\\


\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.5}

\begin{table}[h!]
    \resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{%
    \begin{tabular}{l|l|l|l|l|}
    \cline{2-5}
     & Multiplicative quasi-character $\chi$ & Additive character $\psi$ & Haar measure $\dx$ & Standard function $f$ \\ \hline
    \multicolumn{1}{|l|}{\multirow{2}{*}{$F = \rr$}} & $\chi = \abs{\cdot}^s$ & \multirow{2}{*}{$e^{-2 \pi i x}$} & \multirow{2}{*}{Lebesgue measure} & $e^{- \pi x^2}$ \\[5pt] \cline{2-2} \cline{5-5} 
    \multicolumn{1}{|l|}{} & $\chi = \sgn \abs{\cdot}^s$ &  &  & $xe^{- \pi x^2}$ \\[5pt] \hline
    \multicolumn{1}{|l|}{\multirow{2}{*}{$F = \cc$}} & \multirow{2}{*}{$\chi_{s, n} : z \mapsto \abs{z}^s \brack{\frac{z}{\abs{z}}}^n, \ n \in \zz$} & \multirow{2}{*}{$e^{- 2 \pi i (z + \bar{z})}$} & \multirow{2}{*}{$2 \abs{\dz \wedge \d{\bar{z}}}$} & $\frac{1}{2\pi} (\bar{z})^n e^{- 2 \pi z \bar{z}}, \ n \geq 0$ \\[5pt] \cline{5-5} 
    \multicolumn{1}{|l|}{} &  &  &  & $\frac{1}{2\pi}(z)^{-n} e^{- 2 \pi z \bar{z}}, \ n < 0$ \\[5pt] \hline
    \multicolumn{1}{|l|}{\multirow{2}{*}{$F$ non-Arch}} & \multirow{2}{*}{$\chi_{s, n} : x \mapsto \abs{x}^s \omega \brack{\frac{x}{\abs{x}}}, n = \text{conductor}(\omega) \in \zz_{\geq 0}$} & \multirow{2}{*}{Variable of conductor $\pp^m$} & \multirow{2}{*}{Variable} & \multirow{2}{*}{$\bone_{\pp^{m-n}}$} \\[5pt]
    \multicolumn{1}{|l|}{} &  &  &  &  \\ \hline
    \end{tabular}
    }
    \caption{The standard additive characters, measures and functions on $0 < \re{\chi} < 1$ on each connected component of $\fanx(F^\times)$ that the zeta integrals and epsilon factors were computed with respect to.}
    \label{table: standard functions}
\end{table}
