For each connected component of $\fanx(F^\times)$, our standard function $f \in \fans(F)$ was constructed so that $\chi \mapsto Z(f, \chi)$ on the strip $\re{\chi} \in (0, 1)$ is a nonvanishing holomorphic function, as can be seen in the second column of Table \ref{table: zeta functions and epsilon factors}. Note that in the case of $F$ being non-Archimedean and $\chi$ being unramified, the non-vanishing part rests on the non-vanishing of the Gauss sum, which always holds by Lemma \ref{lemma: gauss-sum conductor relations}, since the conductor of $\psi_{m-n}$ is never less than $n$ (the conductor of $\omega$). This makes the proof of part \ref{thmitem: functional-eqn of local zeta func} valid even in the case where $Z(f, \chi) = 0$. 

We also record here the $\epsilon$-factors corresponding each choice of $\chi, \psi$ and $\dx$, all of which are visibly holomorphic as functions of $s$.

% Please add the following required packages to your document preamble:
% \usepackage{multirow}
% \usepackage{graphicx}
\begin{table}[h!]
    \resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{%
    \begin{tabular}{l|l|l|l|}
    \cline{2-4}
     & Multiplicative quasi-character $\chi$ & Zeta function $Z(f, \chi)$ & $\epsilon(\chi, \psi, \dx)$ \\ \hline
    \multicolumn{1}{|l|}{\multirow{2}{*}{$F = \rr$}} & $\chi = \abs{\cdot}^s$ & $\Gamma_\rr(s)$ & 1 \\[5pt] \cline{2-4} 
    \multicolumn{1}{|l|}{} & $\chi = \sgn \abs{\cdot}^s$ & $\Gamma_\rr(s+1)$ & $i$ \\[5pt] \hline
    \multicolumn{1}{|l|}{$F = \cc$} & $\chi_{s, n}$ & $\Gamma_\cc \brack{s + \frac{\abs{n}}{2}}$ & $i^{\abs{n}}$ \\ [5pt]\hline
    \multicolumn{1}{|l|}{\multirow{2}{*}{$F$ non-Arch}} & $\chi_{s, n}, \ n = 0$ & $\frac{q^{-ms}}{1 - q^{-s}} \vol(\calo_F^\times, \d{^\times x})$ & $q^{ms} \vol(\pp^m, \dx)$ \\ [5pt] \cline{2-4} 
    \multicolumn{1}{|l|}{} & $\chi_{s, n}, n > 0$ & $q^{(n-m)s} c \vol(\calo_F, \dx) \vol(1 + \pp^n, \d{^\times x}) \inv{\bar{g(\omega, \psi_{\pi^{m-n}})}}$ & $\frac{1}{c}q^{(m-n)(s-1)}g(\bar{\omega}, \psi_{\pi^{m-n}})$ \\ [5pt] \hline
    \end{tabular}%
    }
    \caption{The zeta functions and epsilon factors with inputs from Table \ref{table: standard functions}, which we omit from this table for sake of space.}
    \label{table: zeta functions and epsilon factors}
\end{table}

Finally, we tabulate the local $L$-factors and gamma factors.

% Please add the following required packages to your document preamble:
% \usepackage{multirow}
% \usepackage{graphicx}
\begin{table}[h!]
\begin{center}
\resizebox{.8\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tabular}{l|l|l|l|}
\cline{2-4}
 & Multiplicative quasi-character $\chi$ & Local $L(\chi)$ & $\gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx)$ \\ [5pt] \hline
\multicolumn{1}{|l|}{\multirow{2}{*}{$F = \rr$}} & $\chi = \abs{\cdot}^s$ & $\Gamma_\rr(s)$ & $\Gamma_\rr(1-s)/\Gamma_\rr(s)$ \\[5pt] \cline{2-4} 
\multicolumn{1}{|l|}{} & $\chi = \sgn \abs{\cdot}^s$ & $\Gamma_\rr(s+1)$ & $i \Gamma_\rr(1-s)/\Gamma_\rr(s)$ \\ [5pt] \hline
\multicolumn{1}{|l|}{$F = \cc$} & $\chi_{s, n}$ & $\Gamma_\cc \brack{s + \frac{\abs{n}}{2}}$ & $i^{\abs{n}}\Gamma_\cc \brack{1-s + \frac{\abs{n}}{2}}/ \Gamma_\cc \brack{s + \frac{\abs{n}}{2}}$ \\[5pt] \hline
\multicolumn{1}{|l|}{\multirow{2}{*}{$F$ non-Arch}} & $\chi_{s, n}, \ n = 0$ (unramified) & $\inv{\brack{1 - \abs{\pi_F}^{-s}}}$ & $q^{ms}\vol(\pp^m, \dx) \frac{1 - \chi(\pi_F)}{1 - \chi^\lor(\pi_F)}$ \\ [5pt] \cline{2-4} 
\multicolumn{1}{|l|}{} & $\chi_{s, n}, n > 0$ (ramified) & 1 & $\frac{1}{c}q^{(m-n)(s-1)}g(\bar{\omega}, \psi_{\pi^{m-n}})$ \\ [5pt] \hline
\end{tabular}%
}
\caption{The local gamma factors with inputs from Table \ref{table: standard functions}, again omitted for sake of space. }
\label{table: gamma factors}
\end{center}
\end{table}

Table \ref{table: gamma factors} allows us to see at once that $\gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx)$ is non-vanishing and holomorphic on the strip $\re{\chi} \in (0, 1)$ for any representative $\chi$ of a connected component of $\fanx(F^\times)$, and thus it admits meromorphic continuation to all of $\fanx(F^\times)$.



We will now see how the $\gamma$-factor behaves as $\psi$ and $\dx$ vary. This will ensure our computations in the previous subsection easily imply the desired results for arbitrary choices of $\psi$ and $\dx$.

\begin{prop}
\label{prop: gamma-factor dependency}
Let $F$ be a local field, $\chi$ a nontrivial character of $F^\times$, $\psi$ a character of $F$ and $\dx$ a Haar measure. Then
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
    \item For every $t \in \rr_{> 0}$, 
    \begin{equation*}
        \gamma(\chi, \psi, t \cdot \dx) = t \cdot \gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx).
    \end{equation*}
    \item For any $a \in F^\times$, if $\psi_a: x \mapsto \psi(ax)$ then
    \begin{equation*}
        \gamma(\chi, \psi_a, \dx) = \chi(a) \inv{\abs{a}}_F \gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx).
    \end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
Since the Fourier transform of any $f \in \fans(F)$ is dependent on choice of $\psi, \dx$, we will write $\hat{f}(y, \psi, \dx)$ to denote this dependency (note if $(\psi, \dx)$ is not a self-dual pair then results like Fourier inversion need not hold, but this is no trouble for us presently). We thus have
\begin{equation*}
    \hat{f}(y, \psi, t \cdot \dx) := \int_F f(x) \psi(xy) t \dx = t \hat{f}(y, \psi, \dx).
\end{equation*}
We also note that $Z(f, \chi)$ has a dependence on $\dx$, if we define $\d{^\times x} := \dx/\abs{x}_F$. In particular the scaling of $\d{^\times x}$ is precisely the amount we scale $\dx$ by. It is also clear from definition that $Z(f, \chi, t \cdot \dx) = t Z(f, \chi, \dx)$. As we have seen, we may pick some $f \in \fans(F)$ so that $Z(f, \chi, \dx)$ is not identically 0 as a meromorphic function of $\chi$ on its connected component in $\fanx(F^\times)$, so we get:
\begin{align*}
\frac{Z(\hat{f}(y, \psi, \dx), \chi^\lor, t \cdot \dx)}{Z(f, \chi, t \cdot \dx)} &= \frac{t^2}{t} \frac{Z(\hat{f}(y, \psi, \dx), \chi)}{Z(f, \chi, \dx)}\\
\implies \gamma(\chi, \psi, t \cdot \dx) &= t \gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx),
\end{align*}
thus proving part (a).\\
Similarly, we have
\begin{equation*}
    \hat{f}(y, \psi_a, \dx) := \int_F f(x) \psi(axy) \dx = \hat{f}(ay, \psi, \dx).
\end{equation*}
Thus, we have
\begin{align*}
    Z(\hat{f}(x, \psi_a, \dx), \chi^\lor) &= \int_{F^\times} \hat{f}(ax, \psi, \dx) \chi^\lor(x)\d{^\times x} \\
    &= \int_{F^\times} \hat{f}(x, \psi, \dx)\chi^\lor(\inv{a} x) \d{^\times x}\\
    &= \chi^\lor(\inv{a}) Z(\hat{f}(x, \psi, \dx), \chi^\lor)\\
    &= \chi(a) \inv{\abs{a}_F}Z(\hat{f}(x, \psi, \dx), \chi^\lor),
\end{align*}
from which it easily follows that
\begin{equation*}
    \gamma(\chi, \psi_a, \dx) = \chi(a) \inv{\abs{a}_F} \gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx),
\end{equation*}
proving part (b).
\end{proof}

Since any possible Haar measure on $F$ is obtained by scaling some chosen $\dx$ by some nonzero positive constant, and (as Proposition \ref{thm: self-duality of local fields} tells us) any additive character is of the form $\psi_a$ for some fixed $\psi$, the above result generalizes our casework in the previous subsection completely. Thus we may (finally!) put our minds to rest on the validity of the proof of Theorem \ref{thm: local zeta function convergence and func-eqn}.\\

For the remainder of the subsections we sketch out some intermediary results that will come up later in \S \ref{section: Examples and Applications} when we look at applications of Tate's thesis to explicit $L$-function computations.\\

In the case that the pair $(\psi, \dx)$ \textit{is} self-dual, we also have nice results about the interaction of the $\gamma$-factors with the multiplicative character $\chi$.

\begin{prop}
    \label{prop: gamma factors relationship}
    Let $F$ be a local field, Suppose the pair $(\psi, \dx)$ is self-dual on $F$ (i.e the Fourier inversion formula with respect to the additive character $\psi$ and measure $\dx$ holds on $F$). Then the gamma factors satisfy:
    \begin{enumerate}[(a)]
        \item $\gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx) \gamma(\chi^\lor, \psi, \dx) = \chi(-1)$.
        \item $\gamma(\bar{\chi}, \psi, \dx) = \chi(-1) \bar{\gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx)}$.
    \end{enumerate}
    \end{prop}
    \begin{proof}
    \underline{Part (a)}: By Fourier inversion, classical Fourier theory that \ $\doublehat{f}(x) = f(-x)$. Moreover, we make the observation that taking the shifted dual is an involution on the space of multiplicative characters, i.e.  $(\chi^\lor)^\lor = \chi$. Putting these two facts together, we get:
    \begin{align*}
        Z(\doublehat{f}, (\chi^\lor)^\lor) = \int_{F^\times} f(-x)\chi(x)\d{^\times x} = \chi(-1)  \int_{F^\times} f(-x)\chi(-x)\d{^\times x} = \chi(-1) Z(f, \chi).
    \end{align*} 
    Next, recalling the main functional equation in Theorem \ref{thm: local zeta function convergence and func-eqn} from the local theory and applying it twice, with the above equality, we get:
    \begin{align*}
        \chi(-1) Z(f, \chi) &= Z(\doublehat{f}, (\chi^\lor)^\lor)\\
        &= \gamma(\chi^\lor, \psi, \dx) Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor)\\
        &= \gamma(\chi^\lor, \psi, \dx) \gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx) Z(f, \chi). 
    \end{align*}
    For any connected component of $\fanx(F^\times)$ we may look in Table \ref{table: standard functions} to find a function $f \in \fans(F)$ such that Table \ref{table: zeta functions and epsilon factors} will assure us that $\chi \mapsto Z(f, \chi)$ is not identically zero on the chosen connected component. Thus, we may cancel the $Z(f, \chi)$ without worry to obtain the desired equality.\\
    
    \underline{Part (b)}:  First, using the fact that $\psi(xy) = \bar{\psi(-xy)}$, since $\psi$ is additive, for any $f \in \fans(F)$ we have:
    \begin{align*}
        \hat{\bar{f}}(y) &:= \int_{F} \bar{f(x)} \psi(xy) \dx\\
        &= \int_{F} \bar{f(x)\psi(-xy)} \dx = \bar{\hat{f}}(-y).
    \end{align*}
    This gives:
    \begin{align*}
        Z(\hat{\bar{f}}, \chi) &:= \int_{F^\times} \hat{\bar{f}}(x) \chi(x)\d{^\times x}\\
        &= \int_{F^\times} \bar{\hat{f}}(-x)\chi(x) \d{^\times x}\\
        &= \chi(-1) Z(\bar{\hat{f}}, \chi).
    \end{align*}
    Next we make the observation that complex conjugation commutes with taking the shifted dual, i.e. $\bar{\chi}^\lor = \bar{\chi^\lor}$. Applying this fact, along with the above deduced identity and the functional equation of Theorem \ref{thm: local zeta function convergence and func-eqn}, we get:
    \begin{align}
        \label{eqn: local zeta func. gamma factor factorization}
        \bar{Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor)} = Z(\bar{\hat{f}}, \bar{\chi^\lor}) &= \chi(-1)Z(\hat{\bar{f}}, \bar{\chi}^\lor) \nonumber \\
        &= \chi(-1) \gamma(\bar{\chi}, \psi, \dx) Z(\bar{f}, \bar{\chi}).
    \end{align}
    
    However, we also have:
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eqn: conjugate of local zeta func.}
        \bar{Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor)} = \bar{\gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx) Z(f, \chi)} = \bar{\gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx)} Z(\bar{f}, \bar{\chi}).
    \end{equation}
    
    Combining (\ref{eqn: local zeta func. gamma factor factorization}) and (\ref{eqn: conjugate of local zeta func.}), and once again looking in our tables to pick $f$ so that $\chi \mapsto Z(\bar{f}, \bar{\chi})$ is not identically zero on a chosen component of $\fanx(F^\times)$, we get desired equality.
    \end{proof}
    
    \begin{corollary}
        \label{cor: epsilon factors relationship}
        The statements of Propositions \ref{prop: gamma-factor dependency} and \ref{prop: gamma factors relationship} hold true if we replace $\gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx)$ with $\epsilon(\chi, \psi, \dx)$.
    \end{corollary}
    \begin{proof}
    This is immediate from the relationship between the epsilon factor and the gamma factor (as meromorphic functions of $\chi$ on $\fanx(F^\times)$) as defined in part (c) of Theorem \ref{thm: local zeta function convergence and func-eqn}, which we repeat below for convenience:
    \begin{equation*}
        \epsilon(\chi, \psi, \dx) = \gamma(\chi, \psi, \dx) \frac{L(\chi)}{L(\chi^\lor)}.
    \end{equation*}
    Here we also implicitly use the observation (which the reader may choose to rigorously verify should they desire) that $\bar{L(\chi)} = L(\bar{\chi})$ for all the local $L$-factors and all inputs $\chi$. 
\end{proof}




The following definition was motivated by several classical computations in analytic number theory, in which $L$-functions have certain complex factors of norm 1 attached to them known as root numbers. Our local zeta functions give us a nice characterizations of this factor.
\begin{defn}
\label{def: root number}
    Let $\chi$ be a multiplicative character (i.e \textit{$S^1$-valued}) of a local field $F$, and let $(\psi, \dx)$ be a self-dual pair of an additive character and Haar measure on $F$. Then define the \textbf{root number} $W(\chi)$ by
    \begin{equation*}
        W(\chi) := \epsilon(\chi \abs{\cdot}^{1/2}, \psi, \dx).
    \end{equation*}
\end{defn}

\begin{remark}
At first glance this definition, in particular the choice of appending the factor of $\abs{\cdot}^{1/2}$ to $\chi$, may seem rather arbitrary. However, we will see that ensuring the input of the $\epsilon$-factor is always a quasi-character of exponent 1/2 is crucial to make the root number well-defined.
\end{remark}
 



\begin{corollary}
    \label{cor: root number has norm 1}
    For any multiplicative character $\chi$ of a local field, the root number $W(\chi)$ is well-defined independent of choice of $\psi$ and $\dx$, and moreover $\abs{W(\chi)} = 1$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
First we analyze dependence (or lack-thereof) on choice of additive character and Haar measure; let $(\psi, \dx)$ be a self-dual pair. By Theorem \ref{thm: self-duality of local fields}, any other additive character looks like $\psi_a = \psi(ax)$, for some $a \in F^\times$. One then knows (from classical Fourier theory in the Archimedean case and from examination of our proof of Theorem \ref{thm: self-duality of local fields} in the non-Archimedean case) that $\abs{a}^{1/2} \dx$ is the Haar measure that gives rise to a self-dual pair with $\psi_a$. Applying Proposition \ref{prop: gamma-factor dependency} (using Corollary \ref{cor: epsilon factors relationship} to use it for $\epsilon$-factors), we get
\begin{align*}
\epsilon(\chi \abs{\cdot}^{1/2}, \psi_a, \abs{a}^{1/2} \dx) = \abs{a}^{1/2} (\chi(a) \abs{a}^{1/2}) \inv{\abs{a}} \epsilon(\chi \abs{\cdot}^{1/2}, \psi, \dx) = \epsilon(\chi \abs{\cdot}^{1/2}, \psi, \dx).
\end{align*}
Thus $W(\chi)$ is well-defined.

For the second assertion, all this is really saying is that any quasi-character of exponent 1/2 has epsilon factor with norm 1. Note for an arbitrary quasi-character $\chi'$ we always have $\chi'(x) (\chi')^\lor(x) = \abs{x}$. If we impose an exponent of 1/2 then we also have $\chi'(x) \bar{\chi'}(x) = \abs{x}$. Combining these identities together and cancelling out $\chi'(x)$ we get $\bar{\chi'} = (\chi')^\lor$.\\
Using this fact and combining statements (a) and (b) of Corollary \ref{cor: epsilon factors relationship} (really Proposition \ref{prop: gamma factors relationship}), we obtain 
\begin{equation*}
    \inv{\epsilon(\chi', \psi, \dx)} = \bar{\epsilon(\chi', \psi, \dx)},
\end{equation*}
and thus the epsilon factor of any exponent 1/2 quasi-character has norm 1, proving our desired result.
\end{proof}


\newpage
\section{The Global Theory}
\label{section: The Global Theory}

\subsection{Adelic Fourier Theory (with some Riemann-Roch)}
\label{subsection: Adelic Fourier Theory}

We first explore the theory of integration and Fourier transforms on general restricted products. Essentially we just want to confirm that all the results and identities one expects to hold actually do. Continuing with the notation of \S \ref{subsection: Intro Adeles and Ideles}, we let $\set{G_v}$ be a collection of locally compact Hausdorff groups, cofinitely many of which are equipped with a respective compact subgroup $H_v$, a Haar measure $\d{g_v}$ with the property that $\vol(H_v, \d{g_v}) = 1$ for almost all $v$, and let $G$ denote their restricted product. We use $\dg$ to denote the induced Haar measure on the restricted product, as in Definition \ref{def: resprod measure}.

\begin{observation} 
    For $f$ an integrable function on $G$, we have the following identity:
    \begin{equation*}
        \int_G f(g) \dg = \lim_S \int_{G_S}f(g_S) \d{g_S},
    \end{equation*}
    where the limit is naturally taken over an increasing directed union of places. This is a direct consequence of the fact that we may compute and integral via a limit of any compact exhaustion, and the fact that any compact is contained in some $G_S$. Conversely, the dominated convergence theorem tells us that if this limit exists for $\abs{f}$ (for a Borel function $f$), then $f$ must be integrable. In particular $f \in L^1(G)$ if and only if $\abs{f} \in L^1(G)$.
\end{observation}

Let $S_0$ be a finite set of indices containing $J_\infty$ and the (finitely many!) finite $v$ for which $\vol(H_v, \d{g_v}) \neq 1$. Suppose for each $v$ we have a continuous integrable $f_v$ on $G_v$ such that $f_v \mid_{H_v} = 1$ for all $v \notin S_0$. For $g = (g_v) \in G$, define
\begin{equation*}
    f(g) = \prod_v f_v(g_v)
\end{equation*}
\begin{prop}
\label{prop: Product of integrals is Integral in restricted product}
With the notation above, the function $f$ is well-defined and continuous. For any $S \supseteq S_0$, we have
\begin{equation*}
    \int_{G_S}f(g_S)\d{g_S} = \prod_{v \in S} \int_{G_v}f_v(g_v)\d{g_v}
\end{equation*}
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
Since $g = (g_v)$ is in the restricted direct product, then $g_v \in H_v$ for all but finitely many $v$. Combining this with the fact that $f_v \mid_{H_v} = 1$ for almost all $v$, we deduce $\prod f_v(g_v)$ is a finite product, and thus well-defined.\\
To show $f$ is continuous, consider a base for $G$ consisting of sets of the form
\begin{equation*}
    \prod U_v \times \prod H_v
\end{equation*}
where the first product contains all the (finitely many!) finite $v$ such that $f \vert_{H_v}$ is nontrivial. Thus $f$ can be computed locally as a finite product of continuous functions, and is thus locally continuous, and thus continuous.\\
The fact that the integral behaves well with products is a consequence of definitions and Fubini's theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
\label{cor: Product integral restricted product Corollary}
    With the notation as in the above result, if $f$ is integrable then 
    \begin{equation*}
        \int_G f(g) \dg = \prod_v \int_{G_v}f_v(g_v) \d{g_v}
    \end{equation*}
    and if the right side exists for $\abs{f}$ then $f$ is integrable.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
By our earlier observation, we know $f$ is integrable if and only if $\lim_S \int_{G_S}\abs{f}(g_S)\d{g_S}$ exists, which is true if and only if the limit
\begin{equation*}
    \lim_S \prod_{v \in S}\int_{G_v}\abs{f_v}(g_v) \d{g_v} = \prod_{v} \int_{G_v} \abs{f_v}(g_v) \d{g_v} < \infty,
\end{equation*}
exists, by Proposition \ref{prop: Product of integrals is Integral in restricted product}.
\end{proof}


\begin{prop}
\label{prop: Fourier Transform on restricted product is integrable}
Suppose all the $G_v$ are abelian, and suppose the continuous functions $\{f_v\}$ on $G_v$ are the characteristic functions for $H_v$ for all but finitely many $v$. Then $f$ is integrable, with Fourier transform $\hat{f}$ continuous and given by
\begin{equation*}
    \hat{f}(\chi) = \prod_v \hat{f}_v(\chi_v),
\end{equation*}
for $\chi = (\chi_v)_v \in \hat{G} = \rprod_v \hat{G_v}$.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
Firstly note that we have
\begin{equation*}
    \hat{f}(\chi) = \int_G f(g) \chi(g) \dg = \prod_v \int_{G_v}f_v(g_v) \chi_v(g_v) \d{g_v} = \prod_{v \in S} \int_{G_v} f_v(g_v) \chi_v(g_v) \d{g_v} = \prod_{v \in S} \hat{f_v}(\chi_v)
\end{equation*}
for some finite set $S$, since $f_v$ is the characteristic function of $H_v$ and $\chi_v \vert_{H_v} = 1$ for cofinitely many $v$. Thus we have a finite product of continuous functions of $\chi$, as desired.
\end{proof}

From now on assume all groups are abelian. We want to build a Haar measure on the Pontryagin dual $\hat{G}$ that is dual to $\dg$ in the sense of the Fourier inversion theorem. For each $v$, let
\begin{equation*}
    \d{\chi_v} = \hat{\d{g_v}}
\end{equation*}
denote the dual measure to $\d{g_v}$ on $\hat{G_v}$. Note for any $f_v \in L^1(G_v)$, we have that
\begin{equation*}
    \hat{f_v}(\chi_v) = \int_{G_v} f_v(g_v)\bar{\chi_v}(g_v)\d{g_v}
\end{equation*}
by definition. If $f_v$ is the characteristic function of $H_v$ (which is visibly integrable and nonnegative) then 
\begin{equation*}
    \hat{f_v}(\chi_v) = \int_{H_v} \chi_v(g_v)\d{g_v} = \begin{cases}
        \vol(H_v, \d{g_v}), & \chi_v \vert_{H_v} = 1\\
        0, & \text{otherwise,}
    \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
since the integral of a non-trivial character over a compact group is zero. In other words, if $H_v^\perp$ is the subgroup of $\hat{G_v}$ of characters trivial on $H_v$, then $\hat{f_v}(\chi_v)$ is the characteristic function of $H^\perp_v$ scaled by $\vol(H_v, \d{g_v})$. Thus, by Fourier inversion, for $y \in H_v$ we have:
\begin{align*}
     \hat{f_v}(y) &= \int_{\hat{G_v}} \hat{f_v}(\chi_v) \chi_v(y) \d{\chi_v}\\ &= \vol(H_v, \d{g_v}) \int_{H^\perp_v} \d{\chi_v} \\ &= \vol(H_v, \d{g_v}) \vol(H^\perp_v, \d{\chi_v})
\end{align*}
where the first equality follows from our choice of $f_v$ as the characteristic function of $H_v$. Recall in our construction of $\dg$ that $\d{g_v}$ assigns volume 1 to $H_v$ for all but finitely many $v$. The above equation then tells us $\vol(H_v^\perp, \d{\chi_v}) = 1$ almost everywhere as well, and so we can define the restricted product Haar measure
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: dual measure on restricted product}
    \d{\chi} := \rprod_v \d{\chi_v}
\end{equation}
as in (\ref{eqn: measure on restricted product}), and this is also well-defined.

\begin{prop}
\label{prop: duality of measure on restricted product}
The measure $\d{\chi}$, as defined in (\ref{eqn: dual measure on restricted product}), is dual to $\dg$, defined in (\ref{eqn: measure on restricted product}), in the sense that:
\begin{equation*}
    f(g) = \int_{\hat{G}} \hat{f}(\chi) \chi(\inv{g}) \d{\chi}
\end{equation*}
for all continuous $f \in L^1(G)$ such that $\hat{f} \in L^1(\hat{G})$.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
    For our purposes, since we know a dual measure to $\dg$ exists and is unique, it suffices to check on some nonzero continuous $f \in L^1(G)$, which we may choose to be a product function of the form
    \begin{equation*}
        f = \prod f_v,
    \end{equation*}
    where $f_v$ is the characteristic function of $H_v$ for almost all $v$. For any input $g$, the left side above is a locally finite product $\prod f_v(g_v)$ by definition, and using the fact that each $\d{\chi_v}$ is dual to $\d{g_v}$, we get:
    \begin{equation*}
        f_v(g_v) = \int_{\hat{G_v}} \hat{f_v}(\chi_v) \chi_v(\inv{g_v}) \d{\chi_v}.
    \end{equation*}
    Multiplying them together (while applying Fubini) gives us our desired result.
\end{proof}

With the relevant desired properties verified, we are now ready to proceed to some actual adelic harmonic analysis.

\begin{defn}
    \label{def: adelic SB func}
    For $K$ a global field, if for every place $v$ we have some $f_v \in \fans(K_v)$ such that $f_v \vert_{\calo_v} = 1$ for almost every $v$, then the "tensor product"
\begin{align*}
\bigtensor_v f_v =: f : \aa_K &\to \cc\\
(x_v) & \mapsto \prod f_v(x_v)
\end{align*}
is locally a finite product, and we call any $\cc$-linear combination of such functions an \textbf{adelic Schwartz-Bruhat function}. We denote by $\fans(\aa_K)$ the complex vector space of all such functions.
\end{defn}

Similar to classical Fourier analysis, it is an exercise in simple-function approximation to see that $\fans(\aa_K)$ is dense in $L^2(\aa_K)$ and $L^1(\aa_K)$. Our goal will be to derive an adelic version of the Poisson summation formula of classical Fourier analysis.\\

First off, to define our Fourier transform we fix $\psi$ to be a standard additive character on the adeles, i.e any character trivial on $K \inj \aa_K$. Moreover we let $\dx$ be the (self-dual!) Tamagawa measure on $\aa_K$, defined in \ref{def: Tamagawa measure}. Then for any $f \in \fans(\aa_K)$, the Fourier transform 
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: fourier transform on A_K}
    \hat{f}(x) := \int_{\aa_K} f(y)\psi(xy) \dy 
\end{equation}
makes sense.

\begin{prop}
    \label{prop: fourier isometry of S(A_K)}
The Fourier transform $f \mapsto \hat{f}$ is an $L^2$-isometry of $\fans(\aa_K)$. 
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
    This follows immediately from the fact that on each component it is an isometry (from classical Fourier analysis at the Archimedean places, and from our proof of Theorem \ref{thm: self-duality of local fields} at the non-Archimedean places), and from the multiplicative definition of the idelic norm in Definition \ref{def: idelic absolute value}. We leave it to the reader to verify the (rather tedious) details should they want to do so.
\end{proof}

We will also particularly be interested in continuous integrable functions on $\aa_K$ that are \textit{$K$-periodic}, i.e $f(x) = f(x + \alpha)$ for every $\alpha \in K$. In these cases we may view $f$ as a function on the quotient $\aa_K/K$. In particular, we may consider its Fourier transform on $ \hat{\aa_K/K}$ (canonically identified with $K$ by Proposition \ref{prop: K = dual of A_K/K}, up to some global choice of nonzero $\omega \in \Omega^1_{K/\ff_q}$ in the function field case)
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: fourier transform on A_K/K}
    \hat{f}(\alpha) := \frac{1}{\vol(\aa_K/K, \dx)} \int_{\aa_K/K} f(y) \psi(\alpha y) \dy,
\end{equation}
for $\alpha \in K$.
\begin{warning}[Notational Abuse]
    There are a few slight abuses of notation here; we have already mentioned the one with our notation of $f$. We also abuse notation for the measure $\dy$ in (\ref{eqn: fourier transform on A_K/K}) is unique measure induced on the quotient by the self-dual Tamagawa $\dx$ on $\aa_K$ and the counting measure on $K$. We also multiply by $1/(\vol(\aa_K/K))$ since this is the normalized measure on the quotient that is dual of the counting measure on $K$. Theorem \ref{thm: volume of A_K/K = 1} tells us this volume is 1, but we will actually deduce this as a consequence of the Poisson summation formula. 
\end{warning}
Fourier inversion then tells us that if $\hat{f} \in L^1(K)$ then
\begin{equation*}
    f(x) = \sum_{\alpha \in K} \hat{f}(\alpha) \bar{\psi}(x \alpha)
\end{equation*}
for $x \in \aa_K/K$, since a convergent integral with respect to a counting measure is just an absolutely convergent sum.
\begin{defn}
A $\cc$-valued continuous integrable function $f$ on $\aa_K$ is \textbf{admissible} if both
\begin{equation*}
    \sum_{\gamma \in K} f(x + \gamma), \quad \sum_{\gamma \in K} \hat{f}(x + \gamma)
\end{equation*}
are absolutely and uniformly convergent on compact sets.
\end{defn}

Admissibility will be a vital property for us, as we will need to use it in the proofs of the upcoming results of Poissoin Summation and Riemann-Roch for the adeles, so we had better be sure everything we are working with is indeed admissible.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma: adelic SB-func is admissible}
Any $f \in \fans(\aa_K)$ is admissible.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Since the Fourier transform $f \mapsto \hat{f}$ carries $\fans(\aa_K)$ into itself, it suffices to just show the necessary convergence for an arbitrary adelic Schwartz-Bruhat function. By part (b)  of Proposition \ref{prop: restricted product properties}, any compact subset of $\aa_K$ is contained in some set of the form
\begin{equation*}
    C := \prod_{v \in S} C_v \times \prod_{v \notin S} \calo_v
\end{equation*}
for $S$ a finite index set containing the infinite places and $C_v \subseteq K_v$ compact. Thus it suffices to show (absolute and uniform) convergence on a set of the form $C$. Recall that, as a $\cc$-vector space, $\fans(\aa_K)$ is generated by product functions built from characteristic functions of fractional ideals at all the non-Archimedean places $v$, and some Schwartz function at all the Archimedean completions. Showing convergence of any such generating function suffices, as it will imply convergence of any finite $\cc$-linear combination of them.

For any such "product function" $f$ we note that at any non-Archimedean place $v$ there exists some $\delta_v \in \rr_{> 0}$ such that
\begin{equation*}
\abs{\gamma}_v > \delta_v \implies f_v(x_v + \gamma) = 0 \  \forall x_v \in C_v.
\end{equation*}
We can use $\delta_v = 1$ for almost all $v$, and so there is some maximum $\delta_v$ across all finite places.

In the positive characteristic case, since our $K$ is some function field over a finite field and every place is non-Archimedean, this tells us the sum $\sum_{\gamma \in K}f(x + \gamma)$ is just a finite sum on  $C$, and thus naturally absolutely and uniformly convergent.

In the number field case, our summation is restricted to some fractional ideal $J$ of $\calo_K$ whose prime factorization corresponds exactly to all the finite places where $\delta_v \neq 1$. However note that this is a discrete subset of 
\begin{equation*}
    K_\infty := \prod_{v \mid \infty} K_v,
\end{equation*}
 which we can treat as a subset of a lattice in $\rr^n$. Note that, as we take an exhaustion of closed balls, the number of elements in the lattice contained in a shell of radius $R$ and thicknes $\Delta$ (relatively small compared to $R$) grows like a fixed polynomial in $R$. However, since $f_v \in \fans(K_v)$ decreases faster than any polynomial, it will go to 0 much faster than the number of $\gamma$ in the sum can grow. This gives us our desired result.
\end{proof}

Now that we know our summation does give us a well-defined value, the statement of the following theorem makes sensse.

\begin{theorem}[Adelic Poisson Summation]
    \label{thm: Adelic Poisson Summation}
For $f \in \fans(\aa_K)$,
\begin{equation*}
    \sum_{\gamma \in K}f(\gamma) = \frac{1}{\vol(\aa_K/K, \dx)} \sum_{\gamma \in K} \hat{f}(\gamma)
\end{equation*}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For notational convenience, define
\begin{equation*}
    F(x) := \sum_{\gamma \in K} f(x + \gamma).
\end{equation*}
Note that since $F(x)$ is $K$-periodic we can view it as a continuous function on $\aa_K/K$. By Pontryagin duality considerations we then know its Fourier transform $\hat{F}$ can be viewed as a function $K \to \cc$. For any $\alpha \in K$, we have:
\begin{align*}
\hat{F}(\alpha) &= \frac{1}{\vol(\aa_K/K, \dx)}\int_{\aa_K/K} \sum_{\gamma \in K} f(x + \gamma) \psi(\alpha x) \dx\\
&=  \frac{1}{\vol(\aa_K/K, \dx)} \int_{\aa_K/K} \sum_{\gamma \in K}  f(x + \gamma) \psi(\alpha(x + \gamma)) \dx \\
&= \frac{1}{\vol(\aa_K/K, \dx)} \int_{\aa_K} f(x) \psi(\alpha x) \dx\\
&= \frac{1}{\vol(\aa_K/K, \dx)} \hat{f}(\alpha),
\end{align*}
where the second equality follows from the fact that $\psi$ is trivial on $K$ and the third follows from the compatability of our measure on $\aa_K/K$ with the counting measure on $K$. Since $\hat{f} \in \fans(\aa_K)$, the above equality also tells us that $\hat{F} \in L^1(K)$, so we may apply Fourier inversion to get
\begin{align*}
 F(x) := \sum_{\gamma \in K} f(x + \gamma) &= \sum_{\gamma \in K} \hat{F}(\gamma) \bar{\psi(x \gamma)}\\
 &= \frac{1}{\vol(\aa_K/K, \dx)} \sum_{\gamma \in K} \hat{f}(\gamma) \bar{\psi(x \gamma)}.
\end{align*}
Setting $x = 0$ gives us our desired result.
\end{proof}

This result allows us to give a computation-free proof of the fact that the Tamagawa measure assigns the quotient $\aa_K/K$ volume 1.

\begin{corollary}
    \label{cor: volume of A_K/K is 1}
    The volume of $\aa_K/K$, induced by the Tamagawa measure on $\aa_K$ and the counting measure on $K$, is 1.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    By Theorem \ref{thm: Adelic Poisson Summation}, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \sum_{\gamma \in K}\hat{f}(\gamma) = \vol(\aa_K/K, \dx) \sum_{\gamma \in K} f(\gamma).
    \end{equation*}
    Applying the Fourier transform again to $\hat{f}$ and using Fourier inversion, we have:
    \begin{align*}
    \sum_{\gamma \in K} f(\gamma) &= \sum_{\gamma \in K} f(-\gamma)\\
     &= \sum_{\gamma \in K} \ \doublehat{f}(\gamma)\\
    &= \vol(\aa_K/K, \dx) \sum_{\gamma \in K} \hat{f}(\gamma)\\
    &= \vol(\aa_K/K, \dx)^2 \sum_{\gamma \in K} f(\gamma).
    \end{align*}
    If we can find some $f \in \fans(\aa_K)$ such that $\sum_{\gamma \in K} f(\gamma) \neq 0$, the above expression would then imply $\vol(\aa_K/K, \dx)^2 = 1$, so the volume must in fact be equal to 1! It remains to construct such a Schwartz-Bruhat function.

    When $K$ is a global function field, we may simply take the adelic "tensor product" of the characteristic function of the local valuation rings at all places, i.e
    \begin{equation*}
        f := \bigtensor_{v \in \fanp_K} \bone_{\calo_v}.
    \end{equation*}
    To be integral at all places a rational function $\gamma \in K$ must have no poles and come from a global function on the corresponding curve over $\ff_q$; this is satisfied only by constant functions, so we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \sum_{\gamma \in K} f(\gamma) = \abs{\ff_q} = q \neq 0,
    \end{equation*}
    as needed.

    When $K$ is a number field, at all the infinite places we may set $f_v$ to be a non-negative nonzero bump function compactly supported in some neighborhood of 0, and at all the finite places we may set $f_v = \bone_{\calo_v}$, and set $f$ to be the adelic "tensor" product of them. Since $\cap_{v < \infty} \calo_v = \calo_K$, it follows that the sum
    \begin{equation*}
        \sum_{\gamma \in K} f(\gamma)
    \end{equation*}
    is nonzero and positive at some finite number of elements of $\calo_K$, specifically the ones that lie in the support of $f_v$ under all the embeddings (up to complex conjugation) $K \inj \cc$. By construction this includes $0 \in \calo_K$, so in particular this sum is nonzero. This completes our proof.
\end{proof}

We will also need a multiplicative version of the Poisson summation formula, relating to the average
\begin{equation*}
    \sum_{\gamma \in K} f(x \gamma),
\end{equation*}
for $x \in \ii_K$. This will be analogous to the Riemann-Roch theorem in a way that we will discuss later in this subsection.

\begin{theorem}[Adelic Riemann-Roch]
    \label{thm: adelic riemann-roch}
For any $x \in \ii_K$ and $f \in \fans(\aa_K)$, 
\begin{equation*}
    \sum_{\gamma \in K} f(\gamma x) = \frac{1}{\abs{x}_{\ii_K}} \sum_{ \gamma \in K} \hat{f}(\gamma \inv{x})
\end{equation*}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix some $x \in \ii_K$, and set $g(z) :=  f(xz)$, for any $z \in \aa_K$. Note that $g \in \fans(\aa_K)$ as well, and thus Poisson summation (\ref{thm: Adelic Poisson Summation}) applies to give us:
\begin{align*}
    \sum_{\gamma \in K} g(\gamma) &= \sum_{\gamma \in K} \hat{g}(\gamma)\\
    &= \sum_{\gamma \in K} \int_{\aa_K} f(xz) \psi(\gamma z) \dz\\
    &= \sum_{\gamma \in K} \int_{\aa_K} \frac{1}{\abs{x}_{\ii_K}} f(z) \psi(\gamma z \inv{x}) \dz\\
    &= \frac{1}{\abs{x}_{\ii_K}} \sum_{\gamma \in K} \hat{f}(\gamma \inv{x}),
\end{align*}
completing the proof.
\end{proof}

At first glance, one might wonder why the above Theorem is named Riemann-Roch; indeed, there seems to be no compact Riemann surfaces anywhere in the picture. Even in Tate's exposition he does not expound upon this connection apart from declaring that it is a "number-theoretic analogue" of Riemann-Roch. In general, the theory of local and global fields in positive characteristic cannot be fully fleshed out and understood without the language of algebraic geometry, and to that end we indulge in a short interlude for the remainder of this subsection to explain this connection. Due the limits of space and time however we will not be able to describe nearly as many connections to broader concepts as we should; for a more detailed exposition we refer the reader to the second chapter of \cite{serre2012algebraic}, or the more number-theory-oriented (yet denser) sixth chapter of \cite{weil1974basic}.\\

The goal here will be to view $K$ as the function field of a smooth proper curve $X$ that is geometrically connected over the finite field of constants $\ff_q \subseteq K$, and reformulate Theorem \ref{thm: adelic riemann-roch} in geometric terms that will correspond to the classical theorem of Riemann-Roch.
\begin{defn}
\label{def: divisors and degree}
Let $K$ be a global field of positive characteristic. A \textbf{divisor} on $K$ is a finite formal linear combination
\begin{equation*}
    D = \sum_{v \in \fanp_K} n_v \cdot v, \quad n_v \in \zz .
\end{equation*}
We denote by $\Div(K)$ the group of divisors on $K$ (which can be thought of as the free abelian group generated by the places of $K$).\\
The \textbf{degree} of a place $\deg(v)$ is the degree of the residue field corresponding to $v$ over the field of constants $\ff_q$. For a divisor $D$ we define its degree by
\begin{equation*}
    \deg(D) := \sum_{v \in \fanp_K} n_v \deg(v).
\end{equation*} 
We denote by $\Div^0(K)$ the subgroup of degree-zero divisors.
\end{defn} 


For any $f \in K^\times$, we note the formal linear combination
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: principal divisor example}
    \smalldiv(f) := \sum_{v \in \fanp_K} \ord_v(f) v
\end{equation}
is a finite linear combination, since $f$ and $1/f$ are both regular functions on a dense open in $X$. Hence this is a divisor, and moreover we note that
\begin{equation*}
    \smalldiv(f) + \smalldiv(g) = \smalldiv(fg)
\end{equation*}
Furthermore, recall the product formula (\ref{thm: Adele product formula}) tells us for any $f \in K^\times$ that
\begin{align*}
1 = \prod_{v \in \fanp_K} \abs{f}_v &= \prod_{v \in \fanp_K} q_v^{\ord_v(f)}\\
&= q^{\sum_{v \in \fanp_k} \deg(v) \ord_v(f)}\\
\implies \deg(\smalldiv(f)) &= \sum_{v \in \fanp_k} \deg(v) \ord_v(f) = 0.
\end{align*}
Thus any such divisor induced by an element of $K^\times$ lies in $\Div^0(K)$, which motivates the following definitions.
\begin{defn}
\label{def: principal divisors and pic}
We denote by $\smalldiv(K^\times)$ the group of all \textbf{principal divisors} of the form $\smalldiv(f)$ for $f \in K^\times$. The quotient $\Div(K)/\smalldiv(K^\times)$ is denoted $\pic(K)$ and called the \textbf{Picard group} of $K$, whose elements are called \textbf{divisor classes}. We also define the \textbf{Picard group of degree zero}, denoted $\pic^0(K)$, by the quotient $\Div^0(K)/\smalldiv(K^\times)$.
\end{defn}

We note that if $\smalldiv(f) = \smalldiv(g)$ then $\smalldiv(f/g) = 0$, which means $f/g \in \bigcap_{v \in \fanp_K} \calo_v \implies f/g \in \ff_q^\times$. All of this tells us we have the following exact sequence:
\begin{equation*}
    1 \to \ff_q^\times \to K^\times \to \Div^0(K) \to \pic^0(K) \to 0.
\end{equation*}


\begin{defn}
    \label{def: linear system}
We say a divisor $D = \sum_{v \in \fanp_K} n_v v$ is \textbf{effective} if $n_v \geq 0$ for all $v$.\\
The \textbf{linear system} $L(D)$ of a divisor $D$ is the union of $\set{0}$ and set of all $f \in K^\times$ such that $D + \smalldiv(f)$ is effective.
\end{defn}
Geometrically, the notion of an effective divisor corresponds to some (codimension-one) subvariety cut out by regular functions, not just rational ones. Analytically one could also think of it as the vanishing locus of a holomorphic function. This concept also allows us to "compare" divisors to one another. In particular, we say
\begin{equation*}
    D \geq D' \iff D - D' \text{ is effective} \iff n_v \geq n'_v \ \forall v \in \fanp_K.
\end{equation*}

\begin{observation}
The "functions" $f \in K^\times$ that are in $L(D)$, together with 0, form an $\ff_q$-vector space, since $f \in L(D)$ if and only if $\ord_v(f) \geq -n_v$ for all $v \in \fanp_K$.  We make the observation that if if $\deg(D) < 0$ then $\dim(L(D)) = 0$. In the more general case, we have the following proposition.
\end{observation}

\begin{prop}
\label{prop: L(D) is fin-dim}
For any divisor $D$, the $\ff_q$-vector space $L(D)$ is finite-dimensional.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
The proof here uses the idelic formulation, which will be used to relate Theorem \ref{thm: adelic riemann-roch} to the Riemann-Roch theorem on $X$. First we define a \textit{divisor map} on $\ii_K$ by
\begin{align}
    \label{eqn: divisor map on ideles}
    \fand : \ii_K &\to \bigdiv(K) \nonumber \\
    (x_v)_v &\mapsto \sum_v \ord_v(x_v) \cdot v.
\end{align}
This map is visibly a surjective homomorphism, so to each divisor $D \in \bigdiv(K)$ we may denote an idele $x(D) = (x(D)_v)_v$, i.e $\ord_v(x(D)_v) = \ord_v(D)$. Letting $f_v$ be the characteristic function of $\calo_v$ for each $v$, we may define $ f := \bigtensor_v f_v \in \fans(\aa_K)$. Then by construction, for any $\gamma \in K^\times$, we have:
\begin{equation*}
    f(\gamma x(D)) = \begin{cases}
    1, & \text{ if $\ord_v(\gamma x(D)_v) \geq 0 \ \forall v$,}\\
    0, & \text{ otherwise}.
    \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
In other words, $f(\gamma x(D)) = 1$ if and only if $\fand(\gamma x(D))$ is an effective divisor, which in turn is true if and only if $\gamma \in L(D)$. Thus
\begin{equation*}
    \sum_{\gamma \in K} f(\gamma x(D)) = \abs{L(D)}.
\end{equation*}
Note $f$ is admissible by construction and so the adelic Riemann-Roch theorem applies to tell us the above sum converges, and thus $L(D)$ has finite cardinality, hence is finite dimensional.
\end{proof}  

\begin{remark}
The proof above is rather non-standard, for example a more classical approach taken in Chapter II of \cite{serre2012algebraic} was to show that the sheaf $\call(D)$ -- defined by having its non-zero sections over any nonempty open $U \subseteq X$ consist of regular functions satisfying $\smalldiv_U(f) \geq D \cap U$ -- is coherent. Then one can use the well-known result that the global sections of any coherent sheaf on a projective variety are finite-dimensional. However, our proof method is important for two reasons: (1) as mentioned, it will be used later in the proof of the Riemann-Roch theorem, and (2) it demonstrates the versatility of the adelic language. 
\end{remark}

It will also be of later use to us to note some basic properties of the divisor map, all of which follow directly from definitions. We leave the reader to verify the following equalities, should they wish.
\begin{prop}
    \label{prop: properties of the divisor map}
    If $\fand: \ii_K \to \bigdiv(K)$ denotes the divisor map, then the following properties hold:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item $\ker(\fand) = \prod_v \calo_v^\times = I_{K, \emptyset}$.
        \item $\fand(\ii^1_K) = \bigdiv^0(K)$.
        \item $\ii_K/(K^\times \cdot \ii_{K, \emptyset}) = \pic(K)$.
        \item $\ii^1_K/(K^\times \cdot \ii_{K, \emptyset}) = \pic^0(K)$.
    \end{itemize}
\end{prop}


\begin{remark}
    The nontrivial map $\deg: \pic(K) \to \zz$ not only has finite index, but is in fact \textit{surjective}. This arises as a consequence of the fact that the greatest common divisor of $\deg(v)$ over all $v \in \fanp_K$ is 1. This is discussed further in the proof of Proposition \ref{prop: idelic norm image generated by q} and the remark following it.
\end{remark}


We are now ready for the formulation of the Riemann-Roch theorem.

\begin{theorem}[Geometric Riemann-Roch]
\label{thm: geometric riemann-roch}
    Let $K$ be a global field of positive characteristic. Then there exists an integer $g \geq 0$, called the \textbf{genus} of $K$, and a divisor $\fank$ of degree $2g-2$, called the \textbf{canonical divisor} of $K$, such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \dim(L(D)) - \dim(L(\fank-D)) = \deg(D) - g + 1
    \end{equation*}
    for all $D \in \bigdiv(K)$.
\end{theorem}

The proof of this theorem relies on the extension of the notion of a divisor to that of characters as well. Let $\psi$ be a non-trivial character of $\aa_K$ trivial on $K$; we know this exists by Proposition \ref{prop: exists non-trivial adelic character}. Moreover, the Breaking Down Lemma (\ref{lemma: characters on restricted products}) tells us that the induced characters $\psi_v$ on each are trivial on $\calo_v$ at cofinitely many $v$. Thus, if we define $\ord_v(\psi_v) := \ord_{\pp_v}(\text{Conductor of } \psi_v)$, we get that the divisor
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: divisor of a character}
    \smalldiv(\psi) := - \sum_v \ord_v(\psi_v) \cdot v
\end{equation}
is well-defined. Fixing any such $\psi$, Proposition \ref{prop: K = dual of A_K/K} tells us that any other such character of $\aa_K$ trivial on $K$ is obtained by $x \mapsto \psi(\alpha x)$ for some $\alpha \in K^\times \subseteq \ii_K$. In other words, the set of all divisors induced by characters of $\aa_K/K$ is given by a single divisor class modulo the group of principal divisors, i.e some element $\Psi \in \pic(K)$. This is the key to the proof.

\begin{proof}[Proof of \ref{thm: geometric riemann-roch}]
Pick any $\fank \in \Psi$ defined above corresponding to a standard character $\psi$ of $\aa_K$ trivial on $K$, constructed in the proof of Proposition \ref{prop: exists non-trivial adelic character} via a choice of nonzero global $\omega \in \Omega^1_{K/\ff_q}$. As seen in that proof, each local $\psi_v$ has conductor $\pp_v^{-d_v}$, where $d_v$ is the order of vanishing of $\omega$ in at the stalk $\Omega^1_{X, v}$. By construction, this conductor is equal to $\pp_v^{\deg(v) \ord_v(\fank)}$.

Now define
\begin{equation*}
    g := 1 + \frac{\deg(\fank)}{2} \in \frac{1}{2} \zz.
\end{equation*}
Letting $f := \bigtensor_v f_v \in \fans(\aa_K)$ be defined as the tensor product of all the characteristic functions of $\calo_v$ at each place, we have seen from the proof of Proposition \ref{prop: L(D) is fin-dim} that 
\begin{equation*}
    q^{\dim(L(D))} = \sum_{\gamma \in K} f(\gamma x(D))
\end{equation*} 
for any divisor $D$. By the adelic Riemann-Roch theorem (Theorem \ref{thm: adelic riemann-roch}), we therefore get that
\begin{equation*}
    q^{\dim(L(D))} = \frac{1}{\abs{x(D)}_{\ii_K}} \sum_{\gamma \in K} \hat{f}(\gamma \inv{x(D)}).
\end{equation*}
Note that
\begin{equation*}
    \abs{x(D)}_{\ii_K} = \prod_v q_v^{-\ord_v(D)} = q^{- \deg(D)},
\end{equation*}
so it only remains to show that 
\begin{equation*}
    \sum_{\gamma \in K} \hat{f}(\gamma \inv{x(D)}) = q^{1 - g + \dim(L(\fank - D))}
\end{equation*}
and that $g$ is a non-negative integer. Recalling our computation of the local Fourier transforms at non-Archimedean places in the proof of Proposition \ref{prop: self-dual local measures}, we see that
\begin{equation*}
    \hat{f_v} = N(\text{Conductor of } \psi_v)^{1/2} \bone_{\text{Conductor of } \psi_v} = q^{\deg(v)\ord_v(\fank)/2} \bone_{\text{Conductor of } \psi_v},
\end{equation*}
from which it follows that
\begin{equation*}
    \prod_v \hat{f}_v = q^{-\deg(\fank)/2} \bigtensor_v \bone_{\text{Conductor of } \psi_v} = q^{1-g} \bigtensor_v \bone_{\text{Conductor of } \psi_v}.
\end{equation*}
Thus, for any $\gamma \in K^\times$ we have
\begin{equation*}
    \hat{f}(\gamma \inv{x(D)}) = \begin{cases}
    q^{1-g}, & \ord_v(\gamma) \geq \ord_v(D) + \ord_v(\psi_v) \ \forall v\\
    0, & \text{ otherwise}.
    \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
In particular, $\hat{f}(\gamma \inv{x(D)})$ is nonzero if and only if $\smalldiv(\gamma) \in L(\fank - D))$, whence the desired equality follows. This also shows that $g$ must be equal to $\dim(L(\fank))$, and hence must be a non-negative integer.
\end{proof}



\subsection{The Global Functional Equation}
\label{subsection: global zeta function}

\begin{defn}
\label{def: idele-class character}
An \textbf{idele-class quasi-character} is a continuous homomorphism $C_K \to \cc^\times$. Equivalently, it is a quasi-character of $\ii_K$ trivial on $K^\times$. We will frequently abuse notation between these two.
\end{defn}

\begin{remark}
Some texts also use the term \textbf{Hecke character} or \textbf{Gro\ss \"encharaktere}, though we stick to the more explicitly descriptive term.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[Idelic Lifts of Dirichlet Characters]
\label{example: Dirichlet char as idele-class character}
Recall a \textbf{Dirichlet character} of modulus $m$ is a homomorphism $\chi: (\zz/m\zz)^\times \to \cc^\times$. We claim this can be lifted to an idele-class character. Recalling our decomposition of the rational idele group from Example \ref{example: decomposition of I_Q}, one sees immediately that
\begin{equation*}
    \ii_\qq/\qq^\times \cong \rr_{> 0} \times \hat{\zz}^\times \surj \hat{\zz}^\times \surj (\zz/m\zz)^\times \overset{\chi}{\to} \cc^\times
\end{equation*}
gives us a character of $C_\qq$. A perhaps more non-obvious observation is that \textit{every} quasi-character of $C_\qq$ arises in this fashion, up to some complex power of the idelic absolute value. To see this, note we have the exact sequence
\begin{equation*}
    0 \to C^1_\qq \to C_\qq \to \abs{\ii_\qq}_{\aa_\qq} \to 0, 
\end{equation*}
and recall by Theorem \ref{thm: C^1_K is compact} that $C^1_\qq$ is compact. We can decompose any idele-class quasi-character $\chi$ into $\mu \eta$, with $\mu: C_\qq \to S^1$ and $\eta: C_\qq \to \rr_{> 0}$. Since $\hat{\zz}^\times$ is compact and totally disconnected, any complex quasi-character of it must have finite image, by a standard no-small-subgroups argument.\\
In the case of $\eta$ which has its image in the positive reals, this means it must be trivial on $\hat{\zz}^\times$ and therefore factors through the idelic norm $\abs{\cdot}_{\ii_\qq} : \ii_\qq \surj \rr_{> 0}$. Since any continuous endomorphism of $\rr_{> 0}$ is an exponential, we deduce $\eta$ must be of the form
\begin{equation*}
    \eta(x) = \brack{\abs{x}_{\ii_\qq}}^a,
\end{equation*}
for some $a \in \rr$.\\
In the case of $\mu$ whose values are in $S^1$, it has finite image, and so on $\hat{\zz}^\times$ it must be a lifted Dirichlet character $\lift{\gamma}$. Moreover its value on $\rr_{> 0}$ must be some imaginary power of the idelic norm, by a similar argument as with $\eta$, using the fact that any character of $\rr_{> 0}$ corresponds to imaginary exponentiation. Thus we deduce
\begin{equation*}
    \mu(x) = \brack{\abs{x}_{\ii_\qq}}^{bi} \lift{\gamma}(x),
\end{equation*}
for $b \in \rr$ and $\gamma$ a Dirichlet character. Multiplying them together we get that 
\begin{equation*}
    \chi(x) = \brack{\abs{x}_{\ii_K}}^s \lift{\gamma},
\end{equation*}
for $s = a + bi \in \cc$.
\end{example}


Before we make a definition for our global zeta integral, we must fix a multiplicative measure on the ideles. Recall from  (\ref{eqn: measure on F^times}), before our analysis on the local theory that, for each completion $K_v$, our Haar measure $\d{^\times x_v}$ on the multiplicative group is $\d{x_v}/\abs{x_v}$ scaled by some constant $c_v$. We will now see the reason we gave ourselves this flexibility. If we simply chose the easy route setting $c_v = 1$ for all $v$, then we'd fail to have $\vol(\calo_v^\times, \d{^\times x_v}) = 1$ for almost all $v$. More explicitly, the induced measure $\d{^\times x} = \prod_v \d{^\times x_v}$ on the ideles (in the sense of (\ref{eqn: measure on restricted product})) is defective in the following sense: the measure of any basic open set $\prod_{v \in S} U_v \times \prod_{v \notin S} \calo_v^\times$ would be
\begin{equation*}
    \brack{\prod_{v \in S} \int_{U_v} \d{^\times x_v}} \cdot \brack{\prod_{v \notin S} \int_{\calo_v^\times} \d{^\times x_v}} = \brack{\prod_{v \in S} \vol(U_v, \d{^\times x_v})} \cdot \brack{\prod_{v \notin S} (1 - \inv{N(\pp_v)})},   
\end{equation*}
and the second term in the product converges to 0. To amend this, we set our scaling constant (at all the finite places, keeping the infinite ones the same) to 
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: global scaling constant}
    c_v = N(\pp_v)/(N(\pp_v) - 1).
\end{equation}
Since our standard measure $\d{x_v}$ was normalized so that $\vol(\calo_v, \d{x_v}) = N(\frakD_v)^{1/2}$, we have:
\begin{equation*}
    \vol(\calo_v, \d{x_v/\abs{x_v}}) = (N(\pp_v) - 1)/N(\pp_v) \implies \vol(\calo_v, c_v \d{x_v}/\abs{x_v}) = N(\frakD_v)^{1/2}
\end{equation*}
We may then define $\d{^\times x}$ on $\ii_K$ as the restricted product of all the appropriately scaled local measures, which makes sense since $\calo_v$ is given measure 1 at all the (cofinitely many!) finite unramified places $v$.

\begin{defn}
\label{def: global zeta function}
Given $f \in \fans(\aa_K)$ and $\chi$ an idele-class quasi-character, we define the \textbf{global zeta integral} to be
\begin{equation*}
    Z(f, \chi) := \int_{\ii_K} f(x) \chi(x) \d{^\times x}
\end{equation*}
\end{defn}

The argument in Example \ref{example: Dirichlet char as idele-class character} may be easily generalized to characterize the idele-class quasi-characters for any global field $K$. Firstly note that any quasi-character trivial on $\ii^1_K$ factors through the map $\abs{\cdot}_{\ii_K}: \ii_K \to \rr_{> 0}$, and thus is of the form $\abs{\cdot}^s$ (since any quasi-character on $\rr_{> 0}$ or $q^\zz$ are of the form $t \mapsto t^s$). Moreover, by the imposition of triviality on $K^\times$, any quasi-character on $\ii^1_K$ factors through the compact group $C^1_K$, and thus by a "no-small subgroups" argument is a character (i.e $S^1$-valued.) Thus any idele-class quasi-character is of the form
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: idele-class quasi-character}
    \chi = \mu \abs{\cdot}_{\ii_K}^s
\end{equation}
for $\mu$ a character on $\ii^1_K$ and $s \in \cc$. As in \S\ref{subsection: the local func eqn}, we denote by $\sigma$ its exponent (which is intrinsic to $\chi$ even though $s \in \cc$ is not) and $\chi^\lor$ the shifted dual (see Definitions \ref{def: exponent and unramified character} and \ref{def: shifted dual and L(s, chi) notation}). 

As we did in the local theory, by declaring two idele-class quasi-characters to be equivalent if their quotient is trivial on $\ii^1_K$, we see an equivalence class consists of quasi-characters of the form 
\begin{equation*}
    \chi = \chi_0 \abs{\cdot}^s,
\end{equation*}
for $\chi_0$ some representative of the equivalence class and $s \in \cc$ uniquely determined. Thus we may view the zeta integral as having inputs on the Riemann surface $\fanx(C_K)$ of idele-class quasi-characters, with local complex coordinate $s$ on each connected component. We now are ready for the crux of Tate's thesis.

\begin{theorem}[The Global Functional Equation]
\label{thm: global func eqn}
Let $K$ be a global field. Then for any $f \in \fans(\aa_K)$ and idele-class quasi-character $\chi$ with exponent $\sigma = \re{\chi}$  the following hold:
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
    \item \label{thmitem: global zeta func absolutely converges} $Z(f, \chi)$ is holomorphic in $\chi$ and absolutely converges when $\sigma > 1$, uniformly on $\sigma \geq 1 + \epsilon$, for all $\epsilon > 0$.
    \item \label{thmitem: global zeta func meromorphic cont.}$Z(f, \chi)$ extends to a meromorphic function on all of $\fanx(C_K)$, with simple poles when $\chi = 1$ and $\chi = \abs{\cdot}_{\ii_K}$, with corresponding residues given by $- \vol(C^1_K)f(0)$ and $\vol(C^1_K)\hat{f}(0)$.
    \item \label{thmitem: global zeta func eqn} The functional equation $Z(f, \chi) = Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor)$ is satisfied.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
In item (b) of the above Theorem, we have not explicitly what measure we are taking the volume of $C^1_K$ with respect to. The answer is what one might expect: first we induce the measure on $\ii^1_K$ by the exact sequence
\begin{equation*}
    1 \to \ii^1_K \to \ii_K \to \abs{\ii_K}_{\ii_K} \to 1,
\end{equation*}
where $\abs{\ii_K}_{\ii_K}$ is given the standard measure $\inv{\abs{x}}\dx$ if it is $\rr_{> 0}$ and the counting measure if it is (some finite-index subgroup of) $q^\zz$, and $\ii_K$ is given the measure defined above. The reason for this will become clear in the computations involved in the proof of the above theorem. Then, using that measure on $\ii^1_K$ and the counting measure on $K^\times$, and the exact sequence 
\begin{equation*}
    1 \to K^\times \to \ii^1_K \to C^1_K \to 1,
\end{equation*}
we get the measure with respect to which we should compute the volume of $C^1_K$.
Weil, in \cite{weil1974basic} normalizes the measure to make $\vol(C^1_K) = 1$; this is essentially akin to the old dilemma of "where to put the factor of $2\pi$?" that one encounters in classical Fourier analysis. We elect not to do this and to compute its volume later in terms of the measure scaled locally by the factors described in (\ref{eqn: global scaling constant}).
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \ref{thmitem: global zeta func absolutely converges} of \ref{thm: global func eqn}]
The assertions are $\cc$-linear in $f$, so it suffices to verify them for the case where $f = \tensor_v f_v$, where $f_v$ is the characteristic function of $\calo_v$ for almost all $v$. Similarly we write $\chi_v$ for the restriction of $\chi$ the $v$-th component. Then Corollary \ref{cor: Product integral restricted product Corollary} allows us decompose our zeta function into a product of the form: 
\begin{equation*}
    Z(f, \chi) = \prod_{v \in \fanp_K} Z(f_v, \chi_v).
\end{equation*}
It suffices to show absolute convergence (and uniformity on $\sigma \geq 1 + \epsilon$) of the product of $Z(\bone_{\calo_v}, \abs{\cdot}^\sigma_{\aa_K}) = \inv{\brack{1 - \abs{\pi_v}_v^\sigma}}$ over all the finite places $v$, since the remaining components will only contribute a finite product.

When $K$ is a number field of degree $d$ over $\qq$, we have (when $\sigma > 1$):
\begin{equation*}
    1 \leq \prod_{v \nmid \infty} \frac{1}{1 - \abs{\pi_v}_v^\sigma} \leq \prod_{p \text{ prime}} \brack{\frac{1}{1 - 1/p^{\sigma}}}^d = \brack{\sum_{n \geq 1} \frac{1}{n^\sigma}}^d,
\end{equation*}
so we set absolutely convergence with uniformity in this case.

When $K$ has positive characteristic $p$, expressing it as a finite separable extension of $\ff_q(t)$, the relevant product to check for convergence is
\begin{equation*}
    \prod_{n \geq 1} \brack{\frac{1}{1 - 1/q^{n\sigma}}}^{N_n} 
\end{equation*}
where $q$ is the size of the algebraic closure of $\ff_p$ in $K$ and $N_n$ is the number of monic irreducibles of degree $n$ in $\ff_q[t]$. The reasoning behind this is that for every monic irreducible of $\ff_q[t]$ (we may drop all the places of $K$ above the infinite one on $\ff_q(t)$ for the purposes of verifying convergence) there are $\leq [K : \ff_q(t)]$ finite places of $K$ over that prime. We know there are $\leq q^n$ monic irreducibles of degree $n$, so it suffices to check convergence of 
\begin{equation*}
    \prod_{n \geq 1} \brack{\frac{1}{1 - 1/q^{n\sigma}}}^{q^n}. 
\end{equation*}
Taking the formal logarithm gives us 
\begin{equation*}
    \log \brack{\prod_{n \geq 1} \brack{\frac{1}{1 - 1/q^{n\sigma}}}^{q^n}} = \sum_{n \geq 1} q^n \log \brack{\frac{1}{1 - q^{-n \sigma}}}.
\end{equation*}
A classical Taylor series expansion tells us $\log(1/(1-x)) \leq x$ (for $x \in (0, 1/2)$, at least, which suffices for our purposes), so the above converges (uniformly on $\sigma \geq 1 + \epsilon$) if the following expression does:
\begin{equation*}
    \sum_{n \geq 1} q^n q^{-n \sigma} = \sum_{n \geq 1} q^{n(1 - \sigma)}.
\end{equation*}
This is visibly convergent when $\sigma > 1$, so thus our original expression is also convergent in this domain, as desired.

Holomorphicity of the function $\chi \mapsto Z(f, \chi)$ then follows by the fact that uniform limits of complex analytic functions are analytic.
\end{proof}

To prove the other parts of the theorem, we employ a classic integration trick: splitting up our integral by norm. We thus write our zeta function as the double integral
\begin{equation*}
    Z(f, \chi) = \begin{cases}
    \int_{\rr_{> 0}} \int_{\ii^1_K}f(tx) \chi(tx)\d{^\times x} \frac{\dt}{t}, & \char(K) = 0\\
    \sum_{t \in q^\zz} \int_{\ii^1_K}f(tx) \chi(tx)\d{^\times x}, & \char(K) > 0
    \end{cases} 
\end{equation*}
where by "$tx$" we are abusing notation to mean multiplying $x$ by some choice of idele with norm $t$.

\begin{warning}
When $\char(K) > 0$, we have not yet shown that the image of the idelic norm is in fact all of $q^{\zz}$; this will arrive as a consequence of the proof of our main theorem on the global zeta function! However, we know it is some finite index subgroup of this, so for the purposes of the remainder of the proof we may abuse notation a little and take $q$ to be a \textit{generator} of this subgroup.
\end{warning}

The path to proving our desired statements will be via proving a functional equation for the inner integrand
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: t-local adelic zeta function}
    Z_t(f, \chi) := \int_{\ii_K^1} f(tx) \chi(tx) \d{^\times x} = \int_{\ii_K^t}f(x)\chi(x) \d{^\times x},
\end{equation}
where by $\ii_K^t$ we mean the ideles of norm $t$ (i.e a coset of $\ii^1_K$). The key lemma is as follows.


\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma: Z_t(f, chi) functional equation}
For any idele-class quasi-character $\chi$ and adelic Schwartz-Bruhat function $f$, the $t$-local zeta function $Z_t(f, \chi)$ defined in (\ref{eqn: t-local adelic zeta function}) satisfies
\begin{equation*}
    Z_t(f, \chi) + f(0) \int_{C^1_K} \chi(tx)\d{^\times x} = Z_{\inv{t}}(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) + \hat{f}(0) \int_{C_K^1} \chi^\lor(x\inv{t}) \d{^\times x}. 
\end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{warning}[Notational Abuse]
We are writing $\d{^\times x}$ when are actually integrating with respect to the natural quotient measure on $C^1_K$ discussed in the remark following the Theorem statement of \ref{thm: global func eqn}.
\end{warning}
\begin{proof}
Since $\ii_K^1 /K^\times = C^1_K $ and $\chi$ is trivial on $K^\times$, we have that
\begin{equation*}
    Z_t(f, \chi) = \int_{C^1_K} \brack{\sum_{\alpha \in K^\times} f(\alpha t x)}\chi(tx)\d{^\times x}.
\end{equation*}
Here we use discreteness of $K^\times$ in $\ii_K$ (Corollary \ref{cor: K^times discrete in I_K}) so that a Haar measure on $K^\times$ is given by the counting measure, then using Fubini to separate the uniform summation from the integral. Again, recall "$tx$" here simply means multiplying $x$ by some norm-$t$ idele. By Lemma \ref{lemma: adelic SB-func is admissible}, we know any $f \in \fans(\aa_K)$ is admissible, so the sum within the integral is (absolutely and uniformly!) convergent. Since we are integrating over a compact set, it follows $Z_t(f, \chi)$ is in fact convergent for any choices of $f, \chi$. This naturally leads us to the following identity of the left side of Lemma \ref{lemma: adelic SB-func is admissible}:
\begin{equation*}
    Z_t(f, \chi) + f(0) \int_{C^1_K} \chi(tx)\d{^\times x} = \int_{C^1_K} \sum_{\alpha \in K}f(\alpha t x) \chi(tx)\d{^\times x} . 
\end{equation*}
The vital step now is to apply Theorem \ref{thm: adelic riemann-roch} -- the adelic Riemann-Roch -- to the summation over $K$. More precisely it tells us that $\sum_{\alpha \in K}f(\alpha tx) = 1/\abs{tx}_{\ii_K} \sum_{\alpha \in K} \hat{f}(\alpha \inv{(xt)})$. Substituing this in, we get
\begin{align*}
    Z_t(f, \chi) + f(0) \int_{C^1_K} \chi(tx)\d{^\times x} &= \int_{C^1_K} \chi(tx) \frac{\chi(tx)}{\abs{tx}_{\ii_K}} \sum_{\alpha \in K}\hat{f}(\alpha \inv{(xt)}) \d{^\times x}\\
    &= \int_{C^1_K} \abs{\inv{t}x}_{\ii_K}\chi(t \inv{x}) \sum_{\alpha \in K}\hat{f}(\alpha x \inv{t}) \d{^\times x}\\
    &= \int_{\ii_K^1} \inv{\chi}(x\inv{t})\abs{x \inv{t}}_{\ii_K} \hat{f}(x \inv{t}) \d{^\times x} + \hat{f}(0) \int_{C^1_K} \inv{\chi}(x\inv{t})\abs{x \inv{t}}_{\ii_K} \d{^\times x}\\
    &= Z_{\inv{t}}(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) + \hat{f}(0) \int_{C^1_K}\chi^\lor(x \inv{t})\d{^\times x},
\end{align*}
where the second line follows from a change of variables $x$ to $\inv{x}$. This proves the functional equation.
\end{proof}

Before continuing, we write out the zeta function as an integral over the $t$-local zeta functions, splitting it into an integral over the ideles of norm greater than 1, and over the ideles of norm less than 1. This will come in useful in the remainder of the proof.

When $K$ is a number field, we have:
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: splitting up Z(f, chi) number field}
  Z(f, \chi) = \int_0^1 Z_t(f, \chi)\frac{\dt}{t} + \int_1^\infty Z_t(f, \chi) \frac{\dt}{t}.
\end{equation}
When $K$ is a function field with residue field of order $q$, we have:
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: splitting up Z(f, chi) function field}
    Z(f, \chi) = \brack{ \frac{1}{2}Z_1(f, \chi) + \sum_{n \in \zz_{< 0}} Z_{q^n}(f, \chi)} + \brack{\frac{1}{2} Z_1(f, \chi) + \sum_{n \in \zz_{> 0}} Z_{q^n}(f, \chi)}.
\end{equation}
We are now ready for the proof of the remainder of Theorem \ref{thm: global func eqn}. Though the proofs in the number field and function field case are practically identical modulo notation, for clarity we divide them up. 

\begin{proof}[Proof of parts \ref{thmitem: global zeta func meromorphic cont.} and \ref{thmitem: global zeta func eqn} of \ref{thm: global func eqn} in characteristic zero]
We first examine the integral over of the ideles of norm greater than (or equal to) 1, i.e:
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: convergent half of global zeta function number field}
    \int_1^\infty Z_t(f, \chi) \frac{\dt}{t} = \int_{x \in \ii_K : \abs{x}_{\ii_K} \geq 1} f(x) \chi(x) \d{^\times x}.
\end{equation}
By the settled part \ref{thmitem: global zeta func absolutely converges} of our theorem, we know that this converges when $\sigma = \re{\chi} > 1$. However since all the inputs $x$ of this integral have norm $\geq 1$ convergence actually improves when our exponent $\sigma$ shrinks (recall $f$ is a Schwartz-Bruhat function!). Thus we deduce that (\ref{eqn: convergent half of global zeta function number field}) is convergent everywhere.

It remains to analyse the first half of (\ref{eqn: splitting up Z(f, chi) number field}) . By our "$t$-local" functional equation from Lemma \ref{lemma: Z_t(f, chi) functional equation}, we note that
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: splitting up integral of Z_t from 0 to 1}
    \int^1_0 Z_t(f, \chi) \frac{\dt}{t} = \int_0^1 Z_{\inv{t}}(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) \frac{\dt}{t} + \fanr(f, \chi),
\end{equation}
where $\fanr$ is the "remainder" given by the functional equation of the lemma, i.e
\begin{equation*}
    \fanr(f, \chi) := \int^1_{0} \brack{\hat{f}(0) \int_{C^1_K}\chi^\lor(x \inv{t})\d{^\times x} - f(0) \int_{C^1_K}\chi(tx)\d{^\times x} } \frac{\dt}{t}.
\end{equation*}
 We note that by replacing $t$ with $\inv{t}$, we get that
 \begin{equation*}
    \int^1_0 Z_{\inv{t}}(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) \frac{\dt}{t} = \int^\infty_1 Z_t(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) \frac{\dt}{t}.
 \end{equation*}
 By Proposition \ref{prop: fourier isometry of S(A_K)}, $\hat{f} \in \fans(\aa_K)$ and $\chi^\lor$ is by construction also an idele-class quasi-character, so the same argument as for  (\ref{eqn: convergent half of global zeta function number field}) tells us that this is also convergent everywhere. Thus we have to analyse $\fanr(f, \chi)$.

 If $\chi$ is nontrivial on the norm-1 ideles (i.e $\chi$ is not in the connected component of $1 \in \fanx(C_K)$), then integrating either $\chi$ or $\chi^\lor$ over $C^1_K$ (a compact group!) yields zero, that is to say, $\fanr(f, \chi) = 0$ in this case for any $f$. Combined with our work done so far, we deduce that in this case $Z(f, \chi)$ is in fact an entire function! In other words, on every connected component of $\fanx(C_K)$ except the one on which the characters are trivial on $\ii^1_K$, the the global zeta integral is holomorphic.

 Thus in subsequent analysis we assume that $\chi$ is trivial on the norm-1 ideles, or " everywhere unramified", in an analogous sense to Definition \ref{def: exponent and unramified character}. That is to say, $\chi = \abs{\cdot}_{\ii_K}^s$, for some $s \in \cc$. Thus, we get:
 \begin{equation*}
    \int_{C^1_K} \chi(tx) \d{^\times x} = \int_{C^1_K} \abs{xt}^s_{\aa_K} \d{^\times x} = \vol(C^1_K) t^s.
 \end{equation*}
 Substituting the above into our remainder factor we get:
 \begin{align*}
    \fanr(f, \chi) &= \int^1_0 \brack{ \hat{f}(0) \vol(C^1_K) (\inv{t})^{1-s} - f(0)  \vol(C^1_K) t^s} \frac{\dt}{t}.\\
    &= \vol(C^1_K) \int^1_0 \brack{\hat{f}(0)t^{s-1} - f(0)t^s} \frac{\dt}{t}.
 \end{align*}
 Applying the standard power rule of integration, we get that
 \begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: number-field remainder zeta function}
    \fanr(f, \chi) = \vol(C^1_K) \brack{\frac{\hat{f}(0)}{s-1} - \frac{f(0)}{s}}.
 \end{equation}
 This is visibly meromorphic on $\cc$ with the appropriate residues at the appropriate simple poles as mentioned in part \ref{thmitem: global zeta func meromorphic cont.} in the number field case.
Finally we need to show the functional equation. The two key identities needed for this are
\begin{equation*}
    \doublehat{f}(x) = f(-x) \ \text{ and } \ (\chi^\lor)^\lor = \chi,
\end{equation*}
where the second follows by definition and the first follows by self duality of $\aa_K$ with respect to the Tamagawa measure (Corollary \ref{cor: self-duality of A_K}). Using this observation and combining the equations in (\ref{eqn: splitting up Z(f, chi) number field}) and (\ref{eqn: splitting up integral of Z_t from 0 to 1}), we get
\begin{align*}
Z(f, \chi) &= \int_1^\infty Z_t(f, \chi) \frac{\dt}{t} + \int_1^\infty Z_t(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) \frac{\dt}{t} + \fanr(f, \chi)\\
&= \int_1^\infty \int_{\ii^1_K} \ \doublehat{f}(-tx)(\chi^\lor)^\lor (tx) \d{^\times x} \frac{\dt}{t} + \int_1^\infty Z_t(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) \frac{\dt}{t} + \fanr(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor)\\
&= \int_1^\infty Z_t(\ \doublehat{f}, (\chi^\lor)^\lor) + \int_1^\infty Z_t(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) \frac{\dt}{t} + \fanr(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor)\\
&= Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor),
\end{align*}
where the second line follows from our explicit computation in (\ref{eqn: number-field remainder zeta function}) showing that $\fanr(f, \chi)$ is invariant under replacing $f$ with $\hat{f}$ and $\chi$ with $\chi^\lor$, and the third line follows from the substitution $x = -x$ in the inner integrand (implicitly using that $\chi(x) = \chi(-x)$, as $\chi$ is trivial on $\ii^1_K$).  This completes the proof of the functional equation, and in the words of Tate's thesis, \textit{"the Main Theorem is proved!"}
\end{proof} 

We finish by proving the same result in the case of $K$ a function field in positive characteristic.

\begin{proof}[Proof of parts \ref{thmitem: global zeta func meromorphic cont.} and \ref{thmitem: global zeta func eqn} of \ref{thm: global func eqn} in positive characteristic]
The proof is essentially the same, so we omit some repetitive details. By an identical argument to that used for the equation in (\ref{eqn: convergent half of global zeta function number field}), we see that
\begin{equation*}
    \frac{1}{2}Z_1(f, \chi) + \sum_{n \in \zz_{< 0}} Z_{q^n}(f, \chi)
\end{equation*}
(i.e the sum over ideles of norm greater than 1, along with half of $Z_1$) is convergent everywhere.  If $t_n$ denotes a representative of the coset of $\ii^1_K$ of norm $q^n$ the $t_n$-local functional equation gives us
\begin{align*}
    \frac{1}{2} Z_1(f, \chi) + \sum_{n \in \zz_{> 0}} Z_{q^n}(f, \chi) &= \frac{1}{2}Z_1(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) + \sum_{n \in \zz_{< 0}} Z_{q^n}(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) + \fanr(f, \chi),
\end{align*} 
where
 \begin{align*}
    \label{eqn: function field remainder zeta function}
    \fanr(f, \chi) &= \sum_{n = 1}^\infty \brack{\hat{f}(0) \chi^\lor(\inv{t_n}) \int_{C^1_K}\chi^\lor(x)\d{^\times x} - f(0) \chi(t_n) \int_{C^1_K} \chi(x)\d{^\times x}}\nonumber \\ 
    & \quad \quad  + \frac{1}{2} \brack{\hat{f}(0) \int_{C^1_K} \chi^\lor(x) \d{^\times x} - f(0) \int_{C^1_K} \chi(x) \d{^\times x}}.
 \end{align*}
This follows from our setup of splitting the integral along the norm-one ideles into half, and the observation that $\chi^\lor(\inv{t_0}) = \chi(t_0) = 1$ for any $t_0 \in \ii^1_K$, since $\chi$ is an idele-class quasi-character trivial on $\ii^1_K$ (note triviality on the norm-1 ideles may be assumed by an identical argument to that of the number field case; that the remainder term is zero for any $\chi$ nontrivial on this subgroup!). Using the standard geometric series identity then gives us
 \begin{align}
   \fanr(f, \chi) &= \frac{\vol(C^1_K)(\hat{f}(0) - f(0))}{2} + \sum_{n = 1}^\infty  \vol(C^1_K)\brack{\hat{f}(0) \brack{q^{-n}}^{s-1} - f(0) \brack{q^{-n}}^{s}} \nonumber \\
    &= \vol(C^1_K) \brack{\frac{\hat{f}(0)}{1 - q^{1-s}} - \frac{f(0)}{1 - q^s} + \frac{\hat{f}(0) - f(0)}{2}}.
 \end{align} 
Here we also see we have the desired residues at the relevant (simple!) poles, thus proving part \ref{thmitem: global zeta func meromorphic cont.} of the theorem in the function field case.
Finally, to show the functional equation we again use the fact that $\doublehat{f}(x) = f(-x)$ and $(\chi^\lor)^\lor = \chi$, and the same setup as in the number field case to get:
\begin{align*}
Z(f, \chi) &= \frac{1}{2}Z_1(f, \chi) + \sum_{n \in \zz_{< 0}}Z_{q^n}(f, \chi) + \frac{1}{2}Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) + \sum_{n \in \zz_{< 0}} Z_{q^n}(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) + \fanr(f, \chi)\\
&= \frac{1}{2}Z_1( \ \doublehat{f}, (\chi^\lor)^\lor) + \sum_{n \in \zz_{< 0}}Z_{q^n}( \ \doublehat{f}, (\chi^\lor)^\lor) + \frac{1}{2}Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) + \sum_{n \in \zz_{< 0}} Z_{q^n}(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor) + \fanr(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor)\\
&= Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor).
\end{align*}
Now we may once yet again proclaim \textit{"the Main Theorem is proved!"}

\end{proof}




\newpage



\newpage
\section{Examples and Applications}
\label{section: Examples and Applications}

\subsection{Immediate Corollaries of the Main Theorem}
\label{subsection: Immediate corollaries}

Here, and in the subsequent sections we go through a number of immediate results that follow from the main result of the prior section. Throughout, $K$ denotes a global field.\\

\begin{prop}
\label{prop: Series finite set of places away}
Let $S$ be a finite set of places of $K$ containing the Archimedean ones (i.e possibly empty when $\char(K) > 0$). Then the product
\begin{equation*}
    Z_S(s) := \prod_{v \notin S} \frac{1}{1 - N(\pp_v)^{-s}}
\end{equation*}
is absolutely convergent for $\re{s} > 1$, and moreover $(s-1) Z_S(s)$ tends to a finite positive limit as $s \to 1$.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
For absolute convergence, it suffices to verify the product
\begin{equation*}
    \prod_{v < \infty} \frac{1}{1 - \abs{\pi_v}_v^{\re{s}}}
\end{equation*}
is convergent for all $\re{s} > 1$. But this is precisely what we showed in order to prove part \ref{thmitem: global zeta func absolutely converges} of Theorem \ref{thm: global func eqn}! For the second assertion, pick some $f \in \fans(\aa_K)$ so that $f_v = \bone_{\calo_v}$ for all $v \notin S$ and $f_v$ is the standard function (as tabulated in the fourth column of Table \ref{table: standard functions}) for all $v \in S$. Then, on the domain $\re{s} > 1$, we have the decomposition
\begin{equation*}
    Z(f, \abs{\cdot}^s_{\ii_K}) = \prod_{v \in \fanp_K} Z(f_v, \abs{\cdot}_v^{s}) = Z_S(s) \prod_{v \in S} Z(f_v, \abs{\cdot}_v^s).
\end{equation*}
The local theory (specifically part \ref{thmitem: abs-convg of local zeta function} of Theorem \ref{thm: local zeta function convergence and func-eqn}) tells us that the local zeta functions are in fact absolutely convergent on $\re{s} > 0$. Moreover, part \ref{thmitem: global zeta func meromorphic cont.} of Theorem \ref{thm: global func eqn} tells us that the global zeta integral on the left side of the above equality has a simple pole at $s = 1$ with residue $\vol(C^1_K) \hat{f}(0)$. Thus, we get
\begin{equation*}
    \lim_{s \to 1} (s-1) Z_S(s) = \lim_{s \to 1} (s-1) \frac{Z(f, \abs{\cdot}^s_{\ii_K})}{\prod_{v \in S} Z(f_v, \abs{\cdot}_v^s)} = \frac{\vol(C^1_K) \hat{f}(0)}{\prod_{v \in S} Z(f_v, \abs{\cdot}_v)}
\end{equation*}
Examining Table \ref{table: standard functions}, one sees that $\hat{f}_v(0) > 0$ for all standard $f_v$, and so $\hat{f}(0) > 0$, and moreover the second column of Table \ref{table: zeta functions and epsilon factors} assures us that the local zeta functions with respect to the standard functions are all positive and nonvanishing functions for all $\re{s} > 0$, and in particular for $s = 1$. Thus we get our desired finite positive limit.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Hasse-Weil Zeta function]
    \label{example: Hasse-Weil zeta func}
In postive characteristic we have seen the places of $K$ correspond to closed points of the smooth proper curve $X$ geometrically connected over $\ff_q$ with function field $K$. Then the product
\begin{equation*}
    Z_X(s) := \prod_{v \in X} \frac{1}{1 - \abs{\pi_v}_v^{s}}
\end{equation*}
is absolutely convergent on the right half plane $\re{s} > 1$ by Proposition \ref{prop: Series finite set of places away}. The above is actually equal to
\begin{equation*}
    Z_X(s) = \exp \brack{\sum_{d=1}^\infty \frac{\abs{X(\ff_{q^d})} q^{-ds}}{d}},
\end{equation*}
where $\abs{X(\ff_{q^d})}$ denotes the number of $\ff_{q^d}$-rational points on $X$. We don't prove this equality here, but this particular generating series, the \textbf{Hasse-Weil zeta function}, is of utmost importance, as conjectures about it (first outlined in \cite{WeilOG_Paper}) and subsequent efforts towards an eventual proof led to the formation of much of modern algebraic geometry. We outline these results here for a general smooth projective variety over $\ff_q$.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \underline{Rationality}: 
    For $t := q^{-s}$, 
    \begin{equation*}
        \zeta_X(t) = \frac{P_1(t) P_3(t) \cdots P_{2n-1}(t)}{P_0(t) P_2(t) \cdots P_{2n}(t)},
    \end{equation*}
    where each $P_i(t) \in 1 + t \zz[t]$ for all $i$.
    \item \underline{Functional equation}:
    \[ \zeta_X(q^{-n}t^{-1}) = \pm q^{n\chi(X)/2}t^{\chi(X)} \zeta_X(t)\] 
    where $\chi(X)$ is the Euler characteristic of $X$.
    \item \underline{"Riemann hypothesis"}: For each reciprocal root $\alpha_{i, j}$ of $P_i(t)$, we have $|\alpha_{i, j}| = q^{i/2}$.
    \item \underline{Betti numbers}: If $X$ arises as a "good reduction mod $p$" of a complex variety, then $\deg P_i = \dim H_i(X)$.
\end{enumerate}
We purposefully occlude some details for brevity and clarity, but we will later see ( in \S \ref{subsection: dedekind zeta function}) that the adelic language can be used to prove the Weil conjectures (modulo the Riemann hypothesis) in the case of $X$ a smooth proper geometrically connected curve.
\end{example}

We can use the above proposition and example to prove something we have hinted at all the way back since Chapter \ref{chapter: Hello, Adele}; that the value group of the idelic norm of $K$ in positive characteristic is in fact generated by $q$

\begin{prop}
    \label{prop: idelic norm image generated by q}
For $K$ a global field of positive characteristic and field of constants $q$, the image of $\ii_K$ under the (continuous!) idelic norm is exactly $q^\zz$.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
It is clearly some finite index subgroup; it remains to show that we can generate an idele of norm $q$. Equivalently, if $\kappa_v$ denotes the residue field of $K_v$, we want to show that
\begin{equation*}
    \gcd(\set{[\kappa_v: \ff_q]: v \in \fanp_K}) = 1.
\end{equation*}
Let this $\gcd$ be denoted $d$. Then each $\kappa_v$ contains a copy of $\ff' := \ff_{q^d}$ over $\ff_q$. Let $X$ be the geometrically connected smooth projective curve with function field $K$, and consider
\begin{equation*}
    X' := X \tensor_{\ff_q} \ff',
\end{equation*}
which is a finite etale connected cover of $X$ that is geometrically connected over $\ff'$ and has $d$ points $v_1, \ldots v_d$ over each point $v \in X$. This follows from the fact that
\begin{equation*}
    \kappa_v \tensor_{\ff_q} \ff' = \prod_{\sigma: \ff' \inj \kappa_v} \kappa_v,
\end{equation*}
where the product is taken over all $\ff_q$-linear embeddings of $\ff'$ into $\kappa_v$, of which there are exactly $[\ff' : \ff_q] = d$. Thus we have, in the notation of Example \ref{example: Hasse-Weil zeta func},
\begin{equation*}
    Z_{X'}(s) = \brack{Z_X(s)}^d.
\end{equation*}
