If $f = \bigotimes_{v \in \fanp_K} \bone_{\calo_v}$ and $\chi = \abs{\cdot}^s_{\ii_K}$, using the equality discussed in Example \ref{example: Hasse-Weil zeta func} gives us:
\begin{equation*}
    Z(f, \chi) = Z_X(s).
\end{equation*}
If $K'$ denotes the function field of $X'$ (also a global field), then putting all our observations together tells us that any pole of the zeta function corresponding to $K'$ has order $d$ times the zeta function corresponding to $K$. However, Theorem \ref{thm: global func eqn} tells us the only poles of the (analytically continued) zeta function of a global field are simple, so we must have $d = 1$!
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Interestingly, the above result (at least the way we proved it) can also be used to show the following fact: for $K$ a global field of positive characteristic and $S \subseteq \fanp_K$ any finite set of places, there exists a divisor $D = \sum n_v \cdot v$ of degree 1 such that $n_v = 0$ for all $v \in S$. We refer the reader to \S 5 of Chapter VII in \cite{weil1974basic} for a proof of this.
\end{remark}


\subsection{Hecke $L$-functions and Global Epsilon Factors}
\label{subsection: L-function and epsilon factor}
The general theory of $L$-functions on a global field $K$ is of great interest in number theory. One of the primary merits in Tate's approach is the insight into gamma factors and global root numbers of generalized ideal class characters that becomes available via the adelic language. To explore this we will first need to "globalize" the $\epsilon$-factors discussed in Section \ref{section: Local Theory}.\\

In the following propositions, as we did in previous sections, we consider the global $\epsilon$-factor and $L$-function as having inputs on the Riemann surface $\fanx(C_K)$ of idele-class quasi-characters.

\begin{defn}
\label{def: global epsilon factor}
Let $\brack{\psi = \rprod \psi_v, \dx = \rprod \d{x_v}}$ be a self-dual pair on $\aa_K$. Then we define the \textbf{global epsilon factor} to be
\begin{equation*}
    \epsilon(\chi) := \prod_{v \in \fanp_K} \epsilon(\chi_v, \psi_v, \d{x_v}).
\end{equation*}
\end{defn}


\begin{prop}
    \label{prop: global epsilon factor independence}
    The global $\epsilon$-factor in Definition \ref{def: global epsilon factor} is a nonvanishing and holomorphic function on $\fanx(C_K)$, independent of choice of self-dual pair $(\psi, \dx)$.
\end{prop}
\begin{proof}
    We first discuss independence of choice of such a pair of character and measure. Pick a nontrivial adelic character trivial on $K$ and Haar measure
    \begin{equation*}
        \psi = \rprod_{v \in \fanp_K} \psi_v, \quad \dx = \rprod_{v \in \fanp_K} \d{x_v},
    \end{equation*}
    where we recall from \S \ref{subsection: Characters and Measures} that $\psi_v$ is trivial on $\calo_v$ and $\d{^\times x_v}$ assigns $\calo_v$ measure 1 for almost all places $v$.\\ 
    Recall Proposition \ref{prop: K = dual of A_K/K} tells us $\hat{\aa_K/K} \cong K$, so any other additive character has the form $\psi_b = \psi(bx)$, for some $b \in K^\times$. At each local component, viewing $b$ as an element of $K_v^\times$ under the canonical inclusion, we have seen that the self-dual local Haar measure relative to $\psi_{v, b}$ is given by $\abs{b}_v^{1/2} \d{x_v}$. By Proposition \ref{prop: gamma-factor dependency} (using Corollary \ref{cor: epsilon factors relationship} to apply it to the $\epsilon$-factors), we get:
    \begin{equation*}
        \epsilon(\chi_v, \psi_{v, b}, \abs{b}_v^{1/2}\d{x_v}) = \chi_v(b) \abs{b}_v^{-1/2} \epsilon(\chi_v, \psi_v, \d{x_v}).
    \end{equation*}
    Applying this over the product (which also verifies that the restricted product of these modified local measures is a valid measure globally) gives us:
    \begin{align*}
        \prod_{v \in \fanp_K} \epsilon(\chi_v, \psi_{v, b}, \abs{b}_v^{1/2} \d{x_v}) &= \prod_{v \in \fanp_K}  \chi_v(b) \abs{b}_v^{-1/2} \epsilon(\chi_v, \psi_v, \d{x_v})\\
        &= \chi(b) \abs{b}^{-1/2}_{\ii_K} \prod_{v \in \fanp_K} \epsilon(\chi_v, \psi_v, \d{x_v})\\
        &= \prod_{v \in \fanp_K} \epsilon(\chi_v, \psi_v, \d{x_v}),
    \end{align*}
    where the final equality follows from the fact that $\chi$ is trivial on $K^\times$ and the product formula (\ref{thm: Adele product formula}) telling us $\abs{b}_{\ii_K} = 1$. Thus, $\epsilon(\chi)$ is independent of choice of self-dual pair.

    For nonvanishing and holomorphicity, examining our record of all local $\epsilon$-factors in Table \ref{table: zeta functions and epsilon factors}, (combined with Proposition \ref{prop: gamma-factor dependency}) we see that they are non-vanishing and holomorphic for any choice of local $\chi_v, \psi_v, \d{x_v}$, so it remains only to check convergence.

    Any $\chi \in \fanx(C_K)$ is unramified at almost all places, so examining the fourth row of Table \ref{table: zeta functions and epsilon factors} tells us that at cofinitely many $v$ the local epsilon factor is of the form
    \begin{equation*}
        q^{\abs{m_v} s} \vol(\pp_v^{m_v}, \d{x_v}),
    \end{equation*}
    where $\pp_v^{m_v}$ is the conductor of $\psi_v$. However, we also recall  that any adelic character must have trivial conductor (i.e $m_v = 0$) at almost all places, and that any adelic measure must assign $\calo_v$ volume 1 at almost all places. Thus, for any $\chi$, the product $\epsilon(\chi)$ reduces (locally, on $\fanx(C_K)$) to a finite product of non-vanishing holomorphic functions, thereby proving our claim. 
\end{proof}

We also introduce here the notion of a \textit{global} root number.

\begin{defn}
\label{def: global root number}
Given an idele-class quasi-character $\chi$ of exponent 1/2, its \textbf{global root number} is the complex number given by
\begin{equation*}
    W(\chi) := \prod_{v \in \fanp_K} W(\chi_v),
\end{equation*}
where each $W(\chi_v)$ is the local root number as in Definition \ref{def: root number}.
\end{defn}

\begin{sanitycheck}
The definition above makes sense, since the exponent of each local $\chi_v$ will also be 1/2, as was required in Definition \ref{def: root number}, and at almost all places we will have $W(\chi_v) = 1$, which we leave the reader to verify. Again, we also omit choice of adelic character or self-dual measure, since the root number is independent of them by construction. Moreover, since all the local root numbers have norm 1, so does the global root number.
\end{sanitycheck}

This is the factor that comes up in the functional equation of the classical Dirichlet $L$-function, which we will see later in Example \ref{example: Dirichlet L-function func-eqn}. Tate's adelic formulation gives a meaningful decomposition of this factor.

With the $\epsilon$-factors and root numbers behind us, we may proceed to globalize the $L$-function. Take some idele-class quasi-character $\chi \in \hat{C_K}$, and -- viewing it as a character on $\ii_K$ -- recall we can consider the local restrictions $\chi_v$ for each place $v$. We can then define the \textbf{global $L$-function} by taking the product of all the local factors, i.e.
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: global L-function}
    L(\chi) := \prod_{v \in \fanp_K} L(\chi_v).
\end{equation}

It will be useful to record here the local $L$-factors and compare their relation to local zeta functions for some choice of local standard function $f_v$. We recall here the "specialized" Gamma functions:
\begin{equation*}
    \Gamma_\cc(s) := (2\pi)^{-s} \Gamma(s), \quad \Gamma_\rr(s) := \pi^{-s/2} \Gamma(s/2),
\end{equation*}
where $\Gamma$ is the classical analytic extension of the factorial function.

\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{2}
\begin{table}[hpt]
\resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tabular}{l|l|l|l|l|}
\cline{2-5}
 & Multiplicative $\chi$ & Local $L(\chi)$ & Local zeta function $Z(f, \chi)$ & Quotient $Z(f, \chi) / L(\chi)$ \\ \hline
\multicolumn{1}{|l|}{\multirow{2}{*}{$F = \rr$}} & $\chi = \abs{\cdot}^s$ & $\Gamma_\rr(s)$ & $\Gamma_\rr(s)$ & 1 \\ \cline{2-5} 
\multicolumn{1}{|l|}{} & $\chi = \sgn \abs{\cdot}^s$ & $\Gamma_\rr(s+1)$ & $\Gamma_\rr(s+1)$ & 1 \\ \hline
\multicolumn{1}{|l|}{$F = \cc$} & $\chi_{s, n}$ & $\Gamma_\cc \brack{s + \frac{\abs{n}}{2}}$ & $\Gamma_\cc \brack{s + \frac{\abs{n}}{2}}$ & 1 \\ \hline
\multicolumn{1}{|l|}{\multirow{2}{*}{$F$ non-Arch}} & $\chi_{s, n}, \ n = 0$ (unramified) & $\inv{\brack{1 - \abs{\pi_F}^{-s}}}$ & $\frac{q^{-ms}}{1 - q^{-s}} \vol(\calo_F^\times, \d{^\times x})$ & $q^{-ms} \vol(\calo_F^\times, \d{^\times x})$ \\ \cline{2-5} 
\multicolumn{1}{|l|}{} & $\chi_{s, n}, n > 0$ (ramified) & 1 & $q^{(n-m)s} c \vol(\calo_F, \dx) \vol(1 + \pp^n, \d{^\times x}) \inv{\bar{g(\omega, \psi_{\pi^{m-n}})}}$ & $Z(f, \chi)$. \\ \hline
\end{tabular}%
}
\caption{On the strip  $0 < \re{\chi} < 1$ of each connected component of $\fanx(F^\times)$ the zeta integral is computed with respect to the corresponding additive character, measure and standard function outlined in Table \ref{table: standard functions}. The zeta integrals themselves were tabulated in Table \ref{table: zeta functions and epsilon factors}. At non-Archimedean $F$, the $q$ denotes the size of its residue field, $m$ denotes the conductor of the (variable) additive character $\psi$, and $\omega$ denotes the unramified part of $\chi$ (which depends on choice of homomorphic retraction of $F^\times$ to $\calo_F^\times$).}
\label{table: L-factors and zeta factors}
\end{table}


Before stating the main theorem on these $L$-functions we recall the \textbf{different ideal} $\frakd_K$ of a global field $K$. In the number field case, this is a proper ideal of $\calo_K$ whose norm recovers $\abs{\disc(\calo_K)}$. In the function field case, the different is better thought of as a \textit{divisor} (since there is no canonical ring of integers) dependent on our choice of standard adelic character $\psi$, which itself was dependent on some choice of nonzero global meromorphic 1-form $\omega \in \Omega^1_{K/\ff_q}$. The degree of this divisor $d_v$ at each place $v$ is the order of $\omega$ at $v$. Moreover, in positive characteristic by the \textbf{different} $\frakD_v$ of $K_v$ we mean the inverse fractional ideal of conductor of $\psi_v$, which is precisely the ideal $\pp_v^{-d_v}$.

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm: global L-func}
For an idele-class quasi-character $\chi$ the global $L$-function $L(\chi)$ converges (absolutely and uniformly on $\re{\chi} \geq 1 + \epsilon$ for every $\epsilon > 0$) when $\re{\chi} > 1$, and has a meromorphic continuation to all of $\fanx(C_K)$, holomorphic outside of $\chi= 1$ and $\chi = \abs{\cdot}_{\ii_K}$, where it has respective residues $- (2\pi)^{-r_2} \vol(C^1_K)$ and $(2 \pi)^{-r_2} \vol(C^1_K)$.
\end{theorem} 

\begin{proof}
All following computations in this proof will be done using self-dual pair $\brack{\psi, \dx}$ of the standard character and Tamagawa measure with respect to which we defined the Fourier transform, all the way back in (\ref{eqn: fourier transform on A_K}). We will see that this is no loss of generality.

For convergence, examining Table \ref{table: L-factors and zeta factors} shows us that $L(\chi_v)$ is a well-defined (and nonvanishing!) on the domain $\re{\chi} > 1$ at all the Archimedean places, and all the ramified non-Archimedean ones (of which there are only finitely many). Thus it suffices to verify that the product
\begin{equation*}
    \prod_{v < \infty} \frac{1}{1 - \chi_v(\pi_v)^{\re{\chi}}}
\end{equation*}
is convergent on the domain $\re{\chi} > 1$; this is precisely the statement of Proposition \ref{prop: Series finite set of places away} with $S = S_\infty$.

To show meromorphic continuation, it will suffice to produce a function $f \in \fans(\aa_K)$ and $G_f$ on $\fanx(C_K)$ non-vanishing and holomorphic on $\re{\chi} > 1$ so that
\begin{equation*}
    L(\chi) =  Z(f, \chi)/G_f(\chi)
\end{equation*}
for $\re{\chi} > 1$, since Theorem \ref{thmitem: global zeta func absolutely converges} of \ref{thm: global func eqn} assures us $Z(f, \chi)$ is convergent on this domain and part \ref{thmitem: global zeta func meromorphic cont.} tells us it admits meromorphic continuation to all of $\fanx(C_K)$.\\
First we construct $f$ as the "adelic tensor product" of all the standard functions $f_v$ described in Table \ref{table: standard functions}. In particular we have
\begin{itemize}
    \item $f_v = e^{- \pi x^2}$, for $v$ real.
    \item $f_v = 1/(2\pi) e^{- 2 \pi z \bar{z}}$, for $v$ complex.
    \item $f_v = \bone_{\pp_v^{m_v}}$, for $v$ non-Archimedean (recall $m_v$ is th conductor of $\psi_v$).
\end{itemize}
This is valid since each $f_v \in \fans(K_v)$, and moreover using the fact that the conductors of $\psi_v, \chi_v$ are trivial at almost all places, we get that $f_v = \bone_{\calo_v}$ for cofinitely many $v$. Hence $\bigotimes_{v} f_v \in \fans(\aa_K)$.

Furthermore, we can explicitly construct $G_f$ by defining its local components $G_{f, v}$, and the fourth-column of Table \ref{table: L-factors and zeta factors} tells us exactly what they should be. In particular the local components of $G_f$ are given by:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $G_{f, v} =1$, for $v$ Archimedean
    \item $G_{f, v} = q^{-ms} \vol(\calo_F^\times \d{^\times x})$, for non-Archimedean $v$ where $\chi_v$ is unramified.
    \item $G_{f, v} = Z(f_v, \chi_v)$, for non-Archimedean $v$ where $\chi_v$ is ramified.
\end{itemize}
By construction then we get $L(\chi_v) G_{f, v} = Z(f_v, \chi_v)$ and moreover each $G_{f, v}$ is visibly nonvanishing and holomorphic on all of $\fanx(K_v^\times)$. We also recall that multiplicative $\chi$ is unramified at cofinitely many places. Of those, (at the unramified \textit{places} of $K$) cofinitely many have trivial conductor for the local additive character $\psi_v$. In other words, $m_v = 0$ at almost all $v$, and recall our local scaling constant for the measures on $K_v^\times$ in (\ref{eqn: global scaling constant}) assigns each $\calo_v^\times$ measure $N(\frakD_v)^{-1/2} = 1$ at all these places as well. It follows the function
\begin{equation*}
    G_f(\chi) := \prod_{v \in \fanp_K} G_{f, v}(\chi_v)
\end{equation*} 
is well-defined, nonvanishing and holomorphic on all of $\fanx(C_K)$, and by construction satisfies
\begin{equation*}
    L(\chi) G_f(\chi) = Z(f, \chi)
\end{equation*}
on this domain. Thus, $L(\chi)$ admits meromorphic continuation to all of $\fanx(C_K)$.

It remains to verify the poles and residues. Since we have $L(\chi) = Z(f, \chi)/G_f(\chi)$, with $G_f$ non-vanishing and holomorphic, the poles of $L(\chi)$ are exactly those of $Z(f, \chi)$, and the residue at any $\chi_0$ is that of $Z(f, \chi_0)$ multiplied by $1/G_f(\chi_0)$. Theorem \ref{thm: global func eqn} tells us $Z(f, \chi)$ has simple poles at $\chi = 1$ and $\chi = \abs{\cdot}_{\ii_K}$, with respective residues $- \vol(C^1_K) f(0)$ and $\vol(C^1_K) \hat{f}(0)$. Noting that that both these poles are unramified characters and examining Table \ref{table: standard functions}, we see that $f_v(0) = 1$ for all non-complex places, and thus $f(0) = (2\pi)^{-r_2}$. Moreover at $\chi = 1$ we have $1/G_f(\chi) = 1$. Thus, we get
\begin{equation*}
    \res_{\chi = 1} L(\chi) = -(2 \pi)^{-r_2} \vol(C^1_K),
\end{equation*}

To compute the residue at $\chi = \abs{\cdot}_{\ii_K}$, we will need to compute the Fourier transform of the standard function $f$. We recall from construction the the standard $\psi_v$ has conductor exactly $\inv{\frakD}_v$ (for function fields this is true by definition) for all non-Archimedean $v$. Mimicking our proof of Proposition \ref{prop: self-dual local measures}, we have
\begin{align*}
\hat{f_v}(y) &= \int_{\inv{\frakD_v}} \bar{\psi_v(xy)} \d{x_v}\\
&= \begin{cases}
\vol(\inv{\frakD_v}, \d{x_v}), & y \in \frakD_v,\\
0, & y \notin \frakD_v.
\end{cases}
\end{align*}
Recall from construction of the Tamagawa measure (see the local components after Definition \ref{def: resprod measure}) that $\vol(\calo_v, \d{x_v}) = N(\frakD_v)^{-1/2}$, whence it follows taking the volume of the codifferent gives us $N(\frakD_v^{1/2})$. With this and the classical results in the Archimedean cases, we have:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $\hat{f_v} = f_v$, for $v$ real.
    \item $\hat{f_v} = f_v$, for $v$ complex.
    \item $\hat{f_v} = (N \frakD_v)^{1/2} \bone_{\frakD_v}$, for $v$ non-Archimedean.
\end{itemize} 
Multiplying them all out together gives us
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: fourier transform of standard func at 0}
    \hat{f}(0) = \prod_{v \in \fanp_K} \hat{f_v}(0) = (2 \pi)^{-r_2} \prod_{v < \infty \text{ ramified}} (N \frakD_v)^{1/2} = (2\pi)^{- r_2} \abs{\frakd_K}^{1/2}.
\end{equation}
 At $\chi = \abs{\cdot}_{\ii_K}$, the local factors are all trivial except at the non-Archimedean places, where
\begin{equation*}
    G_{f, v}(\abs{\cdot}_{\ii_K}) = q_v^{-m_v} \vol(\calo_v^\times, \d{^\times x_v}).
\end{equation*}
Again we use the fact that at all non-Archimedean places we have the conductor of $\psi_v$ is $\pp_v^{m_v} = \inv{\frakD_v}$ and that $\d{x_v}$ assigns $\calo_v$ the value $(N \frakD_v)^{-1/2} = q_v^{m_v/2}$. Thus,  
\begin{equation*}
    G_{f, v}(\abs{\cdot}_{\ii_K}) = q_v^{-m_v} q_v^{m_v/2} = q_v^{-m_v/2} = (N \frakD_v)^{1/2} 
\end{equation*}
Multiplying out all the local components (again using the fact that $m_v = 0$ if and only if $v$ is unramified) therefore gives us
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: Gf residue at 1}
    \frac{1}{G_f(\abs{\cdot}_{\ii_K})} = \prod_{v < \infty \text{ ramified}} (N\frakD_v)^{-1/2} = \abs{\frakd_K}^{-1/2} 
\end{equation}
Putting  (\ref{eqn: fourier transform of standard func at 0}) and (\ref{eqn: Gf residue at 1})  together we get
\begin{equation*}
     \res_{\chi = \abs{\cdot}_{\ii_K}} L(\chi) =  (2 \pi)^{- r_2} \vol(C^1_K),
\end{equation*}
completing our proof.
\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}
\label{thm: L-function functional eqn}
For any idele-class quasi-character $\chi$, we have
\begin{equation*}
    L(\chi) = \epsilon(\chi) L(\chi^\lor).
\end{equation*}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
In all proceeding computations, we let $(\psi, \dx)$ be the self-dual pair of the restricted product of the characters and measures outlined in Table \ref{table: standard functions}. Proposition \ref{prop: global epsilon factor independence} assures us this is no loss of generality.\\
Take any $\chi \in \fanx(C_K)$. Examining the fourth column of Table \ref{table: L-factors and zeta factors}, we see that, at all the (cofinitely!) many places where $\chi_v$ is unramified \textit{and} $\psi_v$ has conductor $\calo_v$, we have
\begin{equation*}
    L(\chi_v) = Z(f_v, \chi_v) \quad \text{and} \quad L(\chi_v^\lor) = Z(\hat{f_v}, \chi_v^\lor).
\end{equation*}
Let $S$ be the finite set of places where this is \textit{not} the case, and define
\begin{equation*}
    L_S(\chi) = \prod_{v \notin S} L(\chi_v).
\end{equation*}
When $\re{\chi} > 1$, we then have the following decomposition:
\begin{equation*}
    Z(f, \chi) = L_S(\chi) \prod_{v \in S} Z(f_v, \chi_v).
\end{equation*}
Noting that all the local $Z(f_v, \chi_v)$ are nonvanishing holomorphic functions on $\re{\chi_v} > 0$, since the right side of the above expression admits a meromorphic continuation to all of $\fanx(C_K)$ (part \ref{thmitem: global zeta func meromorphic cont.} of \ref{thm: global func eqn}), so does $L_S(\chi)$.

Now let us work on the domain $\re{\chi} < 0$ (so $\re{\chi^\lor} > 1$). Part \ref{thmitem: global zeta func eqn} of Theorem \ref{thm: global func eqn} tells us we have
\begin{equation*}
    Z(f, \chi) = Z(\hat{f}, \chi^\lor),
\end{equation*}
with the left side being the meromorphic continuation of the zeta function and the right side decomposing into an absolutely convergent product. Thus, we get:
\begin{align*}
L_S(\chi) \prod_{v \in S} Z(f_v, \chi_v) &= \prod_{v \in \fanp_K} Z(\hat{f_v}, \chi_v^\lor)\\
&= L_S(\chi^\lor) \prod_{v \in S} \epsilon(\chi_v) \frac{L(\chi_v^\lor)}{L(\chi_v)} Z(f_v, \chi_v)\\
\implies L_S(\chi) &= L(\chi^\lor) \prod_{v \in S} \frac{\epsilon(\chi_v)}{L(\chi_v)}\\
\implies L(\chi) &= \epsilon(\chi) L(\chi^\lor),
\end{align*}
where the final equality follows from our work in the proof of Proposition \ref{prop: global epsilon factor independence} that showed $\epsilon(\chi_v, \psi_v, \d{x_v}) = 1$ at all places $v$ where $\chi_v$ is unramified and $\psi_v$ has trivial conductor, i.e all $v \notin S$, thus implying $\epsilon(\chi) = \prod_{v \in S} \epsilon(\chi_v)$. This completes our proof.
\end{proof}

Classically, the $L$-function is written in the manner given in the following definition.

\begin{defn}
    \label{def: Hecke-L function}
For $\chi$ an idele-class quasi-character, the \textbf{Hecke $L$-function} is
\begin{equation*}
    L(s, \chi) := L(\chi \abs{\cdot}^s).
\end{equation*}
\end{defn}


In the more classical notation of the Hecke  the functional equation proved in \ref{thm: global L-func} is given by
\begin{equation*}
    L(s, \chi) = \epsilon(s, \chi) L(1-s, \inv{\chi}),
\end{equation*}
where naturally we define $\epsilon(s, \chi) := \epsilon(\chi \abs{\cdot}^s)$.
It was this result (though not in this exact formulation) that Tate concluded his thesis with, to display how the adelic formulation gave rise to elegant arguments for such classically known results.

\begin{example}[Functional equation of the classical Dirichlet $L$-function]
\label{example: Dirichlet L-function func-eqn}
Suppose $K = \qq$, and let $\chi_0$ be a \textit{primitive} Dirichlet character with conductor $N > 1$; we have seen from Example \ref{example: Dirichlet char as idele-class character} that we may pick an idelic lift $\chi$ of $\chi_0$ so that $\chi$ has exponent $0$, which would tell us that $\inv{\chi} = \bar{\chi}$.
By Theorem \ref{thm: L-function functional eqn}, we deduce that
\begin{equation*}
    L(s, \chi) = \epsilon(s, \chi) L(1-s, \bar{\chi}).
\end{equation*}
Our objective here will be to compute $\epsilon(s, \chi)$. On all the local factors we use the standard characters from Definition \ref{def: standard character} (i.e has trivial conductor at all the finite places) and the self-dual local measure with respect to these characters (i.e Lebesgue on the infinite place and and assigning volume 1 to all $\zz_p$). If $N_p := \ord_p(n)$, we make the observation that $\chi_p$ has conductor $p^{N_p} \zz_p$ at all the finite places. Thus examining Table \ref{table: zeta functions and epsilon factors}, we see that
\begin{align*}
    \epsilon(\chi_\infty) &= i^{\delta}\\
    \epsilon(\chi_p) &= \begin{cases}
    1, & p \nmid N\\
    p^{-N_p(s-1)} g(\bar{\omega_p}, \psi_{p^{-N_p}}), & p \mid N
    \end{cases},
\end{align*}
where here $\delta = 0$ if $\chi_0(-1) = 1$ and 1 otherwise. Expanding out the Gauss sum at all the primes $p$ where $\chi_p$ is ramified (i.e precisely the primes dividing $N$) we have:
\begin{align*}
    g(\bar{\omega_p}, \psi_{p^{-N_p}}) &= \omega_p(-1)g(\omega_p,  \psi_{p^{-N_p}})\\
    &= \omega_p(-1) \int_{\zz_p^\times} \omega_p(u) \psi(u p^{-N_p}) \d{^\times u}.
\end{align*} 
Since $\chi$ has conductor $N_p$, it factors through the quotient $\zz_p^\times/U_{N_p} \cong (\zz/p^{N_p} \zz)^\times$, where 
\begin{equation*}
    U_{N_p} := 1 + p^{N_p} \zz_p
\end{equation*}
Recalling our standard character on $\qq_p$ defined in (\ref{eqn: standard character on Q_p}), and writing $\bar{u}$ for the image of $u \in \zz_p^\times$ under the quotient map, we see that the above is equal to:
\begin{align*}
\omega_p(-1) \sum_{\bar{u} \in (\zz/p^{N_p} \zz)^\times} \omega_p(\bar{u}) e^{2 \pi i \bar{u} p^{-N_p}} \brack{\int_{U_{N_p}} \psi(u p^{-N_p}) \d{^\times u}}
\end{align*}

Note for any $ u = 1 + u_0p^{N_p} \in U_{N_p}$ we have $p^{N_p} u = p^{-N_p} + u_0 \in \zz_p^\times$. Since the conductor of each local additive character is $\zz_p$, we have that $\psi_{p^{-N_p}}(u) = 1$ for all $u \in U_{N_p}$,  so we have:
\begin{align*}
    g(\bar{\omega_p}, \psi_{p^{-N_p}}) &= \omega_p(-1) \sum_{\bar{u} \in (\zz/p^{N_p} \zz)^\times} \omega_p(\bar{u}) e^{2 \pi i \bar{u} p^{-N_p}} \vol(U_{N_p}, \d{^\times u})\\
    &= \omega_p(-1) p^{-N_p} \sum_{\bar{u} \in (\zz/p^{N_p} \zz)^\times} \chi_0(\bar{u}) e^{- 2 \pi i \bar{u}p^{-N_p}}
\end{align*}
If we define $\tau_p(\chi_0) := \sum_{\bar{u} \in (\zz/p^{N_p} \zz)^\times} \chi_0(\bar{u}) e^{- 2 \pi i \bar{u}p^{-N_p}}$, then by primitivity of $\chi_0$ we get:
\begin{equation*}
    \prod_{p \mid N} \tau_p(\chi_0) = \sum^N_{k=1} \chi_0(k) e^{- 2 \pi i k/N} =: \tau(\chi_0),
\end{equation*}
where here $\tau(\chi_0)$ denotes the classical \textbf{Gauss sum} of a Dirichlet character. Using this fact and multiplying all the local $\epsilon$-factors together, we get:
\begin{align*}
    \epsilon(s, \chi) &= i^\delta \prod_{p \mid N}p^{-N_p(s - 1)} \omega_p(-1) p^{-N_p} \tau_p(\chi_0)\\
    &= i^\delta \chi_0(-1) N^{-s} \tau(\chi_0).
\end{align*}
Using the observation that $\chi(-1) i^\delta = (-i)^\delta$ and substituting the above expression into the functional equation gives us:
\begin{equation*}
    L(s, \chi) = N^{-s} (-i)^{\delta} \tau(\chi_0) L(1-s, \bar{\chi}).
\end{equation*}
Note that the factors of $(-i^\delta)$ and $\tau(\chi_0)$ are independent of $s$, so in particular if $s = 1/2$ we would have $\epsilon(1/2, \chi) = W(\chi)$, as in Definition \ref{def: global root number}. Thus, we can write
\begin{equation*}
    L(s, \chi) = N^{-s + 1/2} W(\chi) L(1-s, \bar{\chi}).
\end{equation*}
Multiplying both sides by $N^{s/2}$ and setting $\Lambda(s, \chi) := N^{s/2} L(s, \chi)$, we get:
\begin{equation*}
    \Lambda(s, \chi) = W(\chi) \Lambda(1-s, \bar{\chi}),
\end{equation*}
which is precisely the functional equation of the completed Dirichlet $L$-function!
\end{example}

\begin{sanitycheck}
In the above example, we should check that $\Lambda(s, \chi)$ and $W(\chi)$ deserve their notation, in the sense that our adelic definitions align with the classical ones. For the root number, our adelic language gave us
\begin{equation*}
    W(\chi) = N^{-1/2} (-i)^\delta \tau(\chi_0),
\end{equation*}
but this is precisely the classical definition of the root number! At all the primes coprime to $N$ the local character is unramified, we literally have $L(s, \chi_p) = \inv{(1 - \chi(p)p^{-s})}$, and at all the primes dividing $N$ we the local $L$-factor is trivial, so we get the classical (non-completed) Euler product
\begin{equation*}
    L(s, \chi^f) = \prod_p L(s, \chi_p),
\end{equation*}
where here $L(s, \chi^f)$ denotes the classical Dirichlet $L$-function.
At the (real) infinite place, we note that
\begin{equation*}
    L(s, \chi_\infty) = \pi^{(s + \delta)/2} \Gamma \brack{\frac{s + \delta}{2}},
\end{equation*}
And so we see
\begin{equation*}
    \Lambda(s, \chi) := N^{s/2} L(s, \chi) = N^{s/2} \pi^{(s + \delta)/2} \Gamma \brack{\frac{s + \delta}{2}} L(s, \chi^f),
\end{equation*} 
which we see agrees exactly with the classical completion of the Dirichlet $L$-function.
\end{sanitycheck}


\subsection{Dedekind Zeta Functions}
\label{subsection: dedekind zeta function}


\begin{defn}
\label{def: dedekind zeta function}
For $K$ a global field with trivial idele-class quasi-character $\chi = 1$, the product of all the non-Archimedean local $L$-factors is called the \textbf{Dedekind zeta function}, and denoted
\begin{equation*}
    \zeta_K(s) = \prod_{v \text{ non-Arch.}} \frac{1}{1 - \abs{\pi_v}_v^s}.
\end{equation*}
\end{defn}


In other words, when $K$ is a number field the product is taken over all the finite places, and when $K$ is a global function field it is taken over all places.  We note that unique prime factorization of ideals in a Dedekind domains tells us, via a computation similar to classical Euler expansion,
\begin{equation}
        \label{eqn: Dedekind zeta func Euler expansion}
        \zeta_K(s) = \prod_{v \text{ non-Arch.}} \frac{1}{1 - (N(\pp_v))^{-s}} = \prod_{v \text{ finite}} \brack{\sum_{n \in \nn} \frac{1}{N(\pp_v)^{ns}}} = \sum_{\fraka \ideal \calo_K} \frac{1}{N(\fraka)^s}. 
\end{equation}



\begin{example}
    \label{example: riemann zeta function}
When $K = \qq$ then (\ref{eqn: Dedekind zeta func Euler expansion}) in the above observation tells us 
\begin{equation*}
    \zeta_\qq(s) = \sum_{n\zz \ideal \zz} \frac{1}{\abs{\zz / n \zz}} = \sum_{n \geq 1} \frac{1}{n^s},
\end{equation*}
i.e. we recover the classical Riemann zeta function.
\end{example}




Our goal will be to give explicit characterizations of the Dedekind zeta function for both the number field and function field case. We begin with the case of the number fields. Before we do so, however, we need to recall a few objects from algebraic number theory.

Suppose $K$ has $r_1$ real embeddings and $r_2$ complex embeddings (up to complex conjugation), each corresponding to an infinite place $v \in S_\infty := \fanp_{K, \infty}$. By Dirichlet's unit theorem (Example \ref{example: dirichlet's unit thm}, which itself is a consequence of the generalized $S$-unit theorem), the unit group $\calo_K^\times$ has rank $r - 1$, where $r := r_1 + r_2$.

\begin{defn}
\label{def: regulator}
For $K$ a number field, let $u_1, \ldots u_{r-1}$ be a set of generators for $\calo_{K}^\times/\mu(K)$, and for each $v \in S_\infty$, let $N_v$ be 1 if $v$ is real and 2 if it is complex. Consider the $(r-1) \times r$ matrix 
\begin{equation*}
    M := \sqbrack{N_v \log(\abs{u_i}_v)}_{v \in S_\infty, i \in [r-1]}
\end{equation*}
and let $M'$ be the $(r-1) \times (r-1)$ matrix obtained by deleting any one column of $M$. Then the \textbf{regulator} of $K$ is defined as $\abs{\det(M)}$.
\end{defn}

Essentially, the regulator is a measure of the "relative size" of the group of units of a number field. The larger the regulator, the "fewer" units there are. That the regulator is well-defined is a consequence of the fact that the sum of any row is zero, which itself is a consequence of Lemma \ref{lemma: idelic log map} which is used in the proof of Theorem \ref{thm: dedekind zeta func in char 0}. Geometrically, what is happening is we are taking embedding the group of units as a lattice in a hyperplane in $\rr^{r_1 + r_2}$, and then taking its "covolume", i.e. the volume of the hyerplane quotiented out by the lattice in standard Euclidean Haar measure.



\begin{theorem}
\label{thm: dedekind zeta func in char 0}
Let $K$ be a number field with $r_1$ real places, $r_2$ complex places, root of unity group $\mu(K)$, regulator $R_K$, class number $h_K$, and discriminant $\frakd_K$. Then the function $\zeta_K(s)$ is holomorphic except for simple poles at $s = 0$ and 1 with respective residues $- (2\pi)^{-r_2}c_K$ and $ c_K$, where
\begin{equation*}
    c_K := \frac{2^{r_1} (2\pi)^{r_2} h_k R_k}{\abs{\mu(K)} \abs{\frakd_K}^{1/2}}.
\end{equation*}
\end{theorem}

For convenience we again recall here the specialized Gamma factors:
\begin{equation*}
    \Gamma_\cc(s) := (2\pi)^{-s} \Gamma(s), \quad \Gamma_\rr(s) := \pi^{-s/2} \Gamma(s/2).
\end{equation*}
Note that
\begin{equation*}
    L(\abs{\cdot}^s_{\ii_K}) = L(s, 1) = \brack{\Gamma_\rr(s)}^{r_1} \brack{\Gamma_\cc(s)}^{r_2} \zeta_K(s).
\end{equation*}
 By Theorem \ref{thm: global L-func}, this has residue $- (2 \pi)^{r_2} \vol(C^1_K)$ at $s = 0$ and $(2\pi)^{-r_2} \vol(C^1_K)$ at $s = 1$. We see that
 \begin{equation*}
    \Gamma_\cc(0) = \Gamma_\rr(0) = 1,
 \end{equation*}
 so these factors don't affect the residue at $s = 0$, i.e the residue of $L(s, 1)$ at 0 must be equal to that of $\zeta_K(s)$. At 1, we see
 \begin{equation*}
    \Gamma_\cc(1) = (2\pi)^{-1}, \quad \Gamma_\rr(1) = 1.
 \end{equation*}
 Thus the complex gamma factors contribute a residue of $(2 \pi)^{-r_2}$ to the global $L$-function, so it follows $\zeta_K(s)$ must have residue  $\vol(C^1_K)$ at $s = 1$. Hence, all we have left to do is show that $\vol(C^1_K)$ (with respect to the measure induced appropriately scaled $\d{^\times x}$ on $\ii_K$ and the counting measure on $K$) is equal to $c_K$.\\ 

Let $S$ denote any finite set of places of $K$ containing the infinite ones, and recall from Definition \ref{def: S-versions} the subgroup of $S$-ideles $\ii_{K, S}$. We define the \textit{S-idelic logarithm map} by
\begin{align}
    \label{eqn: idelic logarithm map}
    \fanl_S : \ii^1_{K, S} &\to \rr^{\abs{S}} \nonumber \\
    (x_v)_v & \mapsto (\log\abs{x_v}_v)_{v \in S}.
\end{align}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lemma: idelic log map}
 When $S = S_\infty$, define the hyperplane
 \begin{equation*}
    H_S := \set{(t_v)_{v \in S} : \sum_{v \text{ real}} t_v + 2 \sum_{v \text{ complex}} t_v = 0}.
 \end{equation*}
 Then $\image(\fanl_S) = H_S$ and $\ker(\fanl_S) = \ii_{K, \emptyset}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By definition, $\prod_{v \in S} \abs{x_v}_v = 1$, so thus $\sum_{v \in S} \log(\abs{x_v}_v) = 0$, which is precisely the equation defining the hyperplane $H_S$ (recall our normalized absolute value on $\cc$ defined in Definition \ref{def: normalized absolute values} is the square of the standard one). Moreover given any $(t_v)_v \in H$ we can construct an element of $\ii^1_{K, S}$ by setting $x_v$ of absolute value $e^{t_v}$ for $v \in S$ and picking some $x \in \calo_v^\times$ for all the $v \notin S$. This proves the image of the logarithm is exactly $H_S$.\\
For the kernel, note $(x_v)_v \in \ker(\fanl_S)$ if and only if $\abs{x_v}_v = 1$ (which is true if and only if $x_v \in \calo_v^\times)$ for all $v \in S$. Thus, $(x_v)_v \in \prod_{v} \calo_v^\times = \ii^1_{K, \emptyset} = \ii_{K, \emptyset}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    For the reasons discussed following Definition \ref{def: regulator}, Ramakrishnan \cite{ramakrishnan1998fourier} called the restriction of $\fanl_{S_\infty}$ to $\calo_K^\times$ the \textit{regulator map}.
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem \ref{thm: dedekind zeta func in char 0}]
Let $S = S_\infty$ be the (nonempty, since we are in characteristic zero) set of Archimedean places of $K$. Recall the $S$-class group $C_{K, S}$, which, from the observation following Definiton \ref{def: S-class group}, using the fact that $S$ is nonempty we may write 
\begin{equation*}
    C_{K, S} = \ii^1_K/(K^\times \cdot \ii^1_{K, S}).
\end{equation*}
 We make the observation that the kernel of the projection $C^1_K \surj C_{K, S}$ is precisely $\ii^1_{K, S}/(K^* \cap \ii^1_{K, S})$. By Proposition, \ref{prop: C_K, S is finite}, since $S$ is nonempty we deduce $C_{K, S}$ is a finite group, hence we have that
 \begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: C^1_K vol computation}
    \vol(C^1_K) = \abs{C_{K, S}} \cdot \vol(\ii^1_{K, S}/(K^* \cap \ii^1_{K, S})).
 \end{equation}
Since $S = S_\infty$, we may recall that Example \ref{example: ideal class group} showed $\abs{C_{K, S}} = h_K$, where $h_K$ is the class number of $K$ (i.e the size of the ideal class group). It remains to compute the volume of the second factor.

Note that $\calo_K^\times$ can be viewed as the diagonal embedding of $K^\times$ into $\ii^1_{K, S}$. By Lemma \ref{lemma: idelic log map}, we see that
\begin{equation*}
    \ker(\fanl_{S} \vert_{\calo_K^\times}) = \calo_{K, \emptyset}^\times = \mu(K),
\end{equation*}
where the second equality follows from the generalized $S$-unit theorem (Theorem \ref{thm: S-unit theorem}). This gives us the exact sequence
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: C^1_K vol SES1}
    1 \to \mu(K) \to \calo_K^\times \to \fanl_{S}(\calo_K^\times) \to 1.
\end{equation}
Since $K$ embeds discretely into any $\ii^1_{K, S}$, it follows that $\fanl_S(\calo_K^\times)$ is a discrete subgroup of $\fanl_{S}(\ii^1_{K, S}) = H_S$, as defined in Lemma \ref{lemma: idelic log map}. Moreover recall from Lemma \ref{lemma: O_K, S is cocompact} that $\ii^1_{K, S}/\calo_{K, S}$ is compact, and since $H_S, \fanl_S(\calo_K^\times)$ are the continuous images of $\ii^1_{K, S}$ and $\calo_{K, S} (= \calo_K^\times)$ respectively, we deduce the quotient $H_S/ \fanl_S(\calo_K^\times)$ is also compact. This gives us the exact sequence
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: C^1_K vol SES2}
    0 \to \fanl_S(\calo_K^\times) \to H_S \to H_S/\fanl_S(\calo_K^\times) \to 0.
\end{equation}
Combining (\ref{eqn: C^1_K vol SES1}) and (\ref{eqn: C^1_K vol SES2}) together along with all of our prior observations, we get the following grid of exact sequences:
\begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
        & 1 \arrow[d] & 1 \arrow[d] & 0 \arrow[d] & \\
        1\arrow[r]  & \mu(K) \arrow[r] \arrow[d]  & \calo_K^\times (= K \cap \ii^1_{K, S}) \arrow[r] \arrow[d] & \fanl_S(\calo_K^\times) \arrow[r] \arrow[d] & 1\\
        1 \arrow[r] & \ii^1_{K, \emptyset} \arrow[d] \arrow[r] & \ii^1_{K, S} \arrow[r] \arrow[d] & H_S \arrow[r] \arrow[d] & 0\\
        1\arrow[r] & \ii^1_{K, \emptyset}/\mu(K) \arrow[r] \arrow[d] & \ii^1_{K, S}/(K \cap \ii^1_{K, S}) \arrow[r] \arrow[d] & H_S/\fanl_S(\calo_K^\times) \arrow[r] \arrow[d] & 0\\
        & 1 & 1 & 0
    \end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
It follows that the desired volume we want is given by:
\begin{equation*}
    \vol(\ii^1_{K, S}/(K \cap \ii^1_{K, S})) = \vol(H_S/\fanl_S(\calo_K^\times)) \cdot \vol(\ii^1_{K, \emptyset}/\mu(K))
\end{equation*}
Examining the definition of the regulator and the $S$-idelic logarithm map, we see that $\fanl_S(\calo_K^\times)$ is precisely the embedding of the unit group as a lattice into $H_S$, and that (the absolute value of) the determinant of the matrix in Definition \ref{def: regulator} is equivalent to the covolume of this lattice. Hence,
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: volume is regulator}
    \vol(H_S/\fanl_S(\calo_K^\times)) = R_K.
\end{equation}
To compute the measure of $\ii^1_{K, \emptyset}/ \mu(K)$, we use the fact that $\mu(K)$ is a finite subgroup to see that the measure of the quotient is simply given by $\vol(\ii^1_{K, \emptyset})/\abs{\mu(K)}$. It thus remains to compute this volume. For $v \in S_\infty$, define $U_v := \set{\pm 1}$ if $v$ is real and $S^1$ if $v$ is complex. Note then that we have
\begin{equation*}
    \ii^1_{K, \emptyset} = \prod_{v \in S_\infty} U_v \times \prod_{v < \infty} \calo_v^\times  \subseteq \ii^1_{K, \infty} \subseteq \ii_{K, \infty}.
\end{equation*}
First we determine the measure on $\ii_{K, \infty}$ (not just for $\ii^1_{K, \infty}$!). Recall that our measure we placed on the finite components of the ideles using the scaling constant of (\ref{eqn: global scaling constant}) that gives the unit group volume 1 at all the unramified finite places. Thus if we let $T$ be the set of all infinite and finite ramified (over $\qq$) places, if $\d{^\times x}$ denotes the standard restricted product measure on the ideles, it follows that:
\begin{equation*}
    \d{^\times x} \vert_{\ii_{K, \infty}}  = \d{^\times x_T} = \prod_{v \in T} \d{^\times x_v}.
\end{equation*}
In other words, the Haar measure on $\ii_{K, \infty}$ is literally just the product of the normalized measure at all the components in $T$.  At all the finite places ramified over $\qq$, we have
\begin{equation*}
    \prod_{v < \infty \text{ ramified}} \vol(\calo_v^\times, \d{^\times x_v}) = \prod_{v < \infty \text{ ramified}} N(\pp_v)^{-1/2} = \abs{\frakd_K}^{-1/2}.
\end{equation*} 
For the infinite places, the Haar measure on $U_v$ should be precisely the one that recovers the measure on $\ii_{K, \infty}$ when integrating along $\abs{\ii_K}_{\ii_K} = \rr_{> 0}$, in the sense of Fubini. Since the measure on $\ii_{K, \infty}$ was seen to be the product of the normalized measures on each component, picking an infinite place $v_0 \in S_\infty$, and noting that $K_v^\times = U_v \times \rr_{> 0}$, we want to impose the measure on $U_v$ such that
\begin{equation*}
    \int_{K_v^\times} f(x_v) \d{^\times x_v}  = \int_{U_v} \int_{\rr_{> 0}} f(x_v) \frac{\dt}{t} \du = \int_{\rr_{> 0}} \int_{U_v} f(x_v) \du \frac{\dt}{t}
\end{equation*}
for any $f \in L^1(K_v^\times)$. Since this is the standard Lebesgue measure when $K_v^\times = \rr^\times$, we have to give $U_v = \set{\pm 1}$ the counting measure at the real places (we equally add up the volume along the positive and negative half of the real line), and since this is twice the Lebesgue measure when $K_v^\times = \cc^\times$, we have to give $U_v = S^1$ measure $2 \pi$ (the standard Lebesgue measure gives a circle area $\pi r^2$). Thus $\vol(U_v) = 2$ if $v$ is real and $2 \pi$ if $v$ is complex. Combining these we get
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: volume of I_K emptyset}
    \vol(\ii^1_{K, \emptyset}) = \vol \brack{ \prod_{v \in S_\infty} U_v \times \prod_{v < \infty \text{ ramified}} } = (2)^{r_1} (2 \pi)^{r_2} \abs{\frakd_K}^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}
Putting together our observations and computations in  (\ref{eqn: C^1_K vol computation}), (\ref{eqn: volume is regulator}) and (\ref{eqn: volume of I_K emptyset}), we get  
\begin{equation*}
    \vol(C^1_K) = \vol(\ii^1_{K, S}/(K \cap \ii^1_{K, S})) = \frac{2^{r_1} \cdot (2 \pi)^{r_2} h_K R_K}{\abs{\mu(K)} \abs{\frakd_K}^{1/2}},
\end{equation*}
as desired.
\end{proof}

We now proceed to prove the corresponding result in the positive characteristic case.

\begin{theorem}
    \label{thm: dedekind zeta func in char > 0}
Let $K$ be a global field of positive characteristic with field of constants $\ff_q$ and genus $g$. Then
\begin{equation*}
    \zeta_K(s) = \frac{P(q^{-s})}{(1 - q^{-s}) (1 - q^{1-s})},
\end{equation*}
where $P \in \zz[t]$ of degree $2g$, with $P(0) = 1$ and $P(1) = \abs{\pic^0(K)}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since $\abs{\ii_K}_{\ii_K} = q^\zz$ (by Proposition \ref{prop: idelic norm image generated by q}) and the global $L$-function in this case takes $\abs{\cdot}_{\ii_K}^s$ as an input, we immediately see from Theorem \ref{thm: global L-func} that $\zeta_K(s)$ may be written as a meromorphic function $f(q^{-s})$, with simple poles at 1 and $\inv{q}$, corresponding to $s=0$ and 1 in the proposition statement respectively.

By mimicking our Euler expansion in Example \ref{example: riemann zeta function}, we see when $K = \ff_p(t)$ that we have
\begin{equation*}
    \zeta_K(s) = \sum_{\text{monic } x \in \ff_p[t]} p^{- \deg(x) s} = \sum_{d \geq 0} p^{d(1 - s)} = \frac{1}{1 - p^{1-s}}.
\end{equation*}
This is clearly greater than 1 for any $s$, and for general $K$ of seprable degree $n$ over $\ff_p(t)$ we may bound $\zeta_k(s)$ in absolute value from above by $\abs{\zeta_{\ff_p(t)}(\re{s})}^n$, for $\re{s} > 1$. In any event, the expression makes it clear that $\zeta_{\ff_p(t)}(s) \to 1$ as $\re{s} \to \infty$, and the same must therefore hold for any $K$.

Thus, our meromorphic (away from 1 and $\inv{q}$) function $f(q^{-s})$ tends to 1 as $q^{-s} \to 0$, and so it must be holomorphic at 0, and be equal to 1 there. From all these and the standard characterization of complex analytic functions we deduce we must have
\begin{equation*}
    f(x) = \frac{P(x)}{(1 - x)(1-qx)},
\end{equation*}
for $P$ an holomorphic function on the complex plane such that $P(0) = 1$.

Recall that the functional equation of the global $L$-function (Theorem \ref{thm: L-function functional eqn}) gives us in this case:
\begin{equation*}
    \zeta_K(1-s) = \epsilon(s, 1) \zeta_K(s).
\end{equation*}
Letting $x = q^{-s}$, this tells us that
\begin{equation*}
    P(1/(qx)) = \epsilon(s, 1) P(x) 
\end{equation*}
Noting that in this case our multiplicative character $\chi = 1$ is trivial and thus $\ord_v(\chi_v) = 0$ at all $v$, we recall from the proof of the local functional equation the expression for the local $\epsilon$-factor $\epsilon(1_v)$ (from (\ref{eqn: local epsilon factor non-Arch unramified})), given by
\begin{equation*}
    \epsilon(s, 1_v) = q^{\ord_v(\psi_v) s} \vol(\pp_v^{\ord_v(\psi_v)}, \d{x_v}).
\end{equation*}
Here the Haar measure $\d{x_v}$ must be chosen as to be self-dual with respect to our character $\psi_v$. This is exactly the one that sets the volume of $\calo_v$ to be $N(\pp_v^{\ord_v(\psi_v)})^{-1/2} = q^{- \ord_v(\psi_v)/2}$. It thus follows that
\begin{equation*}
    \epsilon(s, 1_v) = q^{\ord_v(\psi_v)(s - 1/2)},
\end{equation*}
and so the global $\epsilon$-factor is given by
\begin{equation*}
    \epsilon(s, 1) := \prod_v \epsilon(s, 1_v) = q^{(s-1/2)(-\sum_v \ord_v(\psi_v))} = q^{(s-1/2)(2g -2)},
\end{equation*}
using the fact that $\smalldiv(\psi)$ is by definition a canonical divisor, and thus has degree $2g-2$ by our geometric Riemann-Roch theorem. Thus we have:
\begin{equation*}
    \frac{P(1/(xq))}{(1 - q^{s-1})(1 - q^s)} = x^{-2g} q^{-g} q^{1 - 2s} \frac{P(x)}{(1 - q^{-s})(1 - q^{1-s})}. 
\end{equation*}
Multiplying both sides by $1/(q^{1-2s})$ gives us:
\begin{align*}
    x^{-2g} q^{-g} \frac{P(x)}{(1 - q^{-s})(1 - q^{1-s})} &= \frac{1}{q^{1-s} \cdot q^{-s}}\frac{P(1/(xq))}{(1 - q^{s-1})(1 - q^s)}\\
    &= \frac{P(1/xq)}{(q^{1-s} - 1)(q^{-s} - 1)}.
\end{align*}
We can cancel out the denominators, since each factor on the right side is off by a factor of $-1$, giving us:
\begin{equation}
    \label{eqn: fn field dedekind zeta function func eqn}
    P(1/(xq)) = x^{-2g} q^{-g} P(x).
\end{equation} 

Note as $\abs{x} \to \infty$, we have $P(1/xq) \to P(0) = 1$, as already shown. Thus we have $\abs{P(x)} = O(\abs{x^{2g}})$ for $x$ or large enough norm. For any $a \in \cc$ of large enough norm, we therefore have $\abs{P(a)} \leq B \abs{a}^{2g}$ for some $B \in \rr$. Pick some $R > \abs{a}$, and let $C_R$ denote the circle of radius $R$ centered at the origin of the complex plane. Cauchy differentiation tells us
\begin{align*}
    \abs{P^{(2g)}(a)} &= \abs{\frac{(2g)!}{2 \pi i} \int_{C_R} \frac{P(z)}{(z-a)^{2g+1}} \dz}\\
    &\leq \frac{(2g)!}{2 \pi} \int^{2\pi}_0 \frac{BR^{2g}}{R^{2g + 1}} \dtheta \\
    & \leq C B,
\end{align*}
for some constant $C$ independent of $R$. In other words, we have that the $2g$-th derivative of $P$ is a bounded entire function, hence constant by Liouville's theorem. It follows that $P$ must be a polynomial of degree at most $2g$. Analyzing (\ref{eqn: fn field dedekind zeta function func eqn}) as $x \to 0$ (and recalling that $P(0) = 1 \neq 0$), we see that $P$ must in fact have degree \textit{exactly} $2g$.

Finally, we want to compute $P(1)$. Note that Theorem \ref{thm: global L-func} tells us that $\zeta_K(s)$ has residue $- \vol(C^1_K)$ at $s = 0$. Since $\inv{(1 - q^{-s})}$ has residue $\log(q)$ at $ s= 0$, we have: 
\begin{equation*}
    - \vol(C^1_K) = \frac{P(1)}{\log(q) 1-q} \implies P(1) = \vol(C^1_K)(q-1) \log(q).
\end{equation*}
Recall from Proposition \ref{prop: properties of the divisor map} that the divisor map gives us the following exact sequence
\begin{equation*}
    1 \to \ii^1_{K, \emptyset}/\ff_q^\times \to \ii^1_K/K^\times \to \pic^0(K) \to 0. 
\end{equation*}
By similar reasoning as in the number field case (i.e every $U_v$ must be given the measure that recovers the global measure against integration by $q^\zz$, in the sense of Fubini), we see $\vol(\ii^1_{K, \emptyset}) = 1/\log(q)$. The exact sequence above then tells us
\begin{equation*}
    \vol(C^1_K) = \frac{\abs{\pic^0(K)}}{\log(q)(q-1)},
\end{equation*}
and combining this with our previous observation immediately yields $P(1) = \abs{\pic^0(K)}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Theorem \ref{thm: dedekind zeta func in char > 0}, in particular the observation that zeta function of a smooth projective curve is rational with specific degree properties related to its genus and reciprocal roots is also linked to the Weil Conjectures, earlier discussed in Example \ref{example: Hasse-Weil zeta func}. Our theorem can be seen as a proof of the Weil conjectures (modulo the Riemann hypothesis statement) in the special case of a smooth projective geometrically connected curve.
\end{remark}
